Z232.M187P45 1989 070.5′09426′5-dc20 89-3351 ISBN 0-19-812887-8: Printed in Great Britain at the Alden Press Oxford London and Northampton TO MY MOTHER AND THE MEMORY OF MY FATHER # ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS HIS book began alnost accidentally in 1978 when I devoted a summer at the Library of Congress to poring over nineteenth-century English printers' trade magazines, trying to discover what they had to say about bookdesign.
My quest was largely fruitless, because Victorian printers seemed inarticulate on that subject, but as I turned the pages and reflected upon the typography of the last century, I frequently stumbled upon the name of William Morris.
At first I resisted the lure of Morris, with his enormous reputation among bibliophiles, since it appeared selfevident that there could be nothing new to say about the Kelmscott Press, for I was aware that it had been discussed by almost every English-speaking printing historian of this century.
Eventually, however, I realized that there was no avoiding Morris — who seems to many of us the most significant figure in modern book typography - and I then discovered, to my great pleasure, that much fresh evidence about the Kelmscott Press, unavailable to earlier scholars, was now to be found in public and private collections.
Only gradually did I grasp the larger cultural significance of Morris's little typographical adventure' and understand that the meaning of this episode extended far beyond the boundaries of printing history.
That moment of illumination came to me a decade ago, and since then I have had the pleasure of examining the handsome books of the Kelnıscott Press in many British and American libraries, of studying a good deal of manuscript material relating to the history of the Press, and of receiving innumerable courtesies from libraries and collectors.
I am keenly aware of the inadequacy of merely supplying simple, undifferentiated lists of institutions and persons who have given me assistance along the way, yet I could not record here all the nuances of generosity and friendship without writing a memoir of that decade.
I feel confident, however, that many of the persons mentioned will recognize the warm memories that lie behind this litany of names.
Without several fellowships and travel grants, it would have been impossible for me to complete my research.
They were supplied by the American Philosophical Society, the Division of Arts and Hunanities of the University of Maryland, the John Simon Guggenheim Menorial Foundation, and the National Endowment for the Humanities.
Those who have given me permission to quote unpublished documents and to reproduce illustrations are cited elsewhere in this book, but I must register my special indebtedness to the Society of Antiquaries (London), the owner of the William Morris copyright.
John Dreyfus, Joseph R.
Dunlap, and Julia Markus read drafts of the book and offered stimulating criticisms, from which I hope I benefited.
My wife Eileen, my daughter Heather, and my son Glenn have cheerfully tolerated my prolonged preoccupation with the Kelmscott Press and have given me much love and support.
At an early stage in the research Heather also helped in tracking down books at the Library of Congress.
The following have assisted me in ways too numerous to describe: Seymour Adelman, Iain Bain, Nicolas Barker, Ben Bass, B.

BE G AN printing books with the hope of producing some which would have a definite claim to beauty, while at the same time they should be easy to read and should not dazzle the eye, or trouble the intellect of the reader by eccentricity of form in the letters.' With this brisk introduction William Morris began writing on 11 November 1895 a brief explanation for an American admirer of how and why he had established the most famous of all private presses, the Kelmscott Press.
The essay first appeared in Modern Art, a Boston magazine, in 1 896 and was later reprinted, under the title A Note by William Morris on his Aims in Founding the Kelmscott Press, as the last publication of the Press in the spring of 1898.
Morris's formulation of his purposes was characteristically terse: it was the essence of my undertaking', he wrote, to produce books which it would be a pleasure to look upon as pieces of printing and arrangement of type.' The rest of the essay was devoted to a discussion of paper, type-design, spaces between words and lines, margins, and ornaments.
Morris's Note is an undeniably important document.
As one of his few public statements about the Kelmscott Press, it has been quoted frequently since 1898 in histories of the Press; indeed, in 1924 Bernard H.
Newdigate went so far as to suggest that the Note was one of the three books that every student of English book-production ought to read.' Yet one might argue that the essay is cryptic, even evasive, leaving a host of questions unanswered.
How did Morris develop an interest in book-design and production?
Why did he turn to fifteenthcentury German and Italian books for his models?
What was the relationship between the Kelmscott Press books and the best work of other nineteenth-century English printers?
How exactly did Morris rediscover some of the fundamental principles of fine printing?
How did he find the craftsmen to carry out those principles for him?
We need the answers to these and other questions, for there is nowadays general agreement - even among those who dislike his style of typography - that Morris is one of the most important printers of the modern era and that the establishment of the Kelmscott Press was an event of major cultural significance.
Throughout northern Europe and the English-speaking world, particularly in the United States, the impact of Morris's typographical theories and of the Kelmscott books thenselves was pervasive.
Historians are still arguing about the nature of that influence (whether, in particular, it was always a good or enlightening influence), but no one disputes that the forces set in motion at the Kelmscott Press eventually spread round the globe.
Fron a merely quantitative point of view, the achievement of the Kelnscott Press does not seem especially impressive.
Between 1891 and 1898 it produced fifty-two books and a set of specimen pages of another book that was never completed.
The books were designed by Morris, printed on hand-presses, and issued in limited editions averaging about 3oo copies.
All were ornamented with initials and borders by Morris, and some were illustrated, usually by Edward Burne-Jones.
So why are these few Kelmscott books famous everywhere today, and why have they profoundly affected twentieth-century theories of book-design?
One explanation of their potent appeal lies in the fact that Morris's ideas, like his ornamented books, are richly suggestive.
When he appears to be speaking about typographical matters, he is in reality examining how we ought to live.
IfI were asked to say what is at once the most important production of Art and the thing most to be longed for, I should answer, A beautiful House; and if I were further asked to name the production next in importance and the thing next to be longed for, I should answer, A beautiful Book,'he wrote in an unfinished essay. ‘To enjoy good houses and good books in self-respect and decent comfort, seems to me to be the pleasurable end towards which all societies of human beings ought now to struggle." That is a most characteristic passage by Morris: not content with treating typography as ‘a minor art' (to quote Stanley Morison's phrase),3 Morris insists upon enlarging the context and seeing the beautiful book as an essential part of his attempt to redeem the late-Victorian world from dreary mechanical ugliness.
It is surely this passion for moral, aesthetic, and social reform which lends resonance to Morris's typographical pronouncements and which makes the history of the Kelmscott Press a matter of some importance even to non-bibliophiles.
Morris, like many of his contemporaries, had grave misgivings about a world that was increasingly being shaped by industrialism.
He looked about him at the Victorian city — smoky, polluted, crowded, and gloomy — and asked himself whether the human race was really in a better state now than it had been in the Middle Ages.
In an essay published in 189o Morris took his readers on a tour of an unidentified but typical English industrial town (apparently Peterborough) and reyealed, street by street, building by building, the sheer horror of the urban landscape.
With great eloquence Morris described the dreary terraced houses, the small, shabby shops, the ‘ugly modern iron bridge’, the depressed and underfed workers in the streets, the wilderness of dirty brick and stone.⁴ This was the grimy present that Morris found about hin; the past which he then immediately conjured up was a medieval world of simplicity, purity, and cleanliness.
Morris's Hamlet-like injunction to Look here, upon this picture, and on this' reminds one of that famous pair of plates which had appeared in A.
W.
N.
Pugin's Contrasts (second edition, 1841): Catholic town in 1440' and ‘The Same Town in 184o'.
In the lower plate we see a serene city of spires pointing towards heaven; in the other plate the multitude of lovely churches has been supplanted by factories, belching smokestacks, and a particularly grim Victorian workhouse.
Pugin's pictures, in turn, evoke Carlyle's Past and Present (1843), the very title of which vibrates with the fervour of Victorian moral idealism, and its implied contrast, like Pugin's, between a lost Golden Age and the bleak world of nineteenth-century England may help us to grasp the passionate intensity of Morris's rejection of the present.
We may perhaps wish to protest that Pugin, Carlyle, Ruskin, Morris, and others of their outlook idealized the Middle Ages and ignored the material benefits of our mechanized civilization; yet we fail to do them justice unless we recognize that the Gothic Revival was not ultimately an attempt to resuscitate a period style of architecture or art but rather a morally powerful protest against the psychological and socil disintegration produced by the modern world.
The Kelmscott Press was, in effect, the final phase of the Gothic Revival.
Burne-Jones compared the Kelmscott Chaucer (1896) to a cathedral; by the same analogy, the smaller books can be regarded as parish churches, each faithfully rendered in quasi-medieval style.
But such a sweeping rejection of contemporary life brought with it apparently insoluble problems.
How, for example, could one reproduce the general appearance of a fifteenth-century book and still escape the dangers of mere imitation?
By turning one's back on contemporary printing machinery, how could one improve the appearance of the ordinary book?
In reverting to methods of hand-production, how could one print books that readers with modest incomes might afford?
These doubts about the Kelmscott Books — which are so redolent of Victorian revivalism and historicisn — were in fact being expressed Uathohrc torn un 1440.

Pugin's Contrasts (1841) is a pictorial rendcring of that nostalgia for a medieval Goldcn Age which pervades the writings of many Victorians, particularly Carlyle, Ruskin, and Morris.
The mood of Pugin's drawings is connected with Morris's ‘typographical adventurc', for the Kelmscott Press represcnts the final phasc of the ninctecnth-century Gothic Revival. already during Morris's lifetime.
Why, it was asked repeatedly in the press, did Morris, a committed socialist, not print books for the poor?
Why did he insist upon producing anachronistic books whose glamour derived primarily from their resemblance to medieval volumes?
Morris was conscious of such accusations, as we shall see, and he offered persuasive answers to some of them.
The dilemmas he faced at the Kelmscott Press are of more than technical interest today, because they illustrate both the rewards and dangers of a reversion to handicraft methods.
If, as Morris argued, our complex technological civilization bears within it the seeds of its own destruction, then the story of the Kelmscott Press — possibly the best-known example in the Western world of a serious, thoughtful attempt to revive a traditional craft — becomes a fascinating parable of the extent to which we may be able deliberately to turn the clock backwards.
One suspects that Morris has been written about more extensively than any other printer except Gutenberg.
Even before the Kelmscott Press was officially opened, enthusiastic reports about Morris's ‘typographical adventure’ (as he described it in a letter) began to appear in British and American periodicals.
By 1893, in distant Chicago, the Inland Printer was prepared to proclaim that ‘a new era has dawned in English printing'.
Reporters flocked to Kelmscott House, Morris's home in Hammersmith, for interviews with the Master Printer'.
The Kelmscott edition of Chaucer was widely described as the most beautiful printed book since the fifteenth century.
In the last volume produced at the Press, Sydney Cockerell supplied A Short History and Description of the Kelmscott Press' andAn Annotated List of All the Books Printed at the Kelmscott Press in the Order in Which They Were Issued'.
In 1924 H.
Halliday Sparling, who had been Morris's son-in-law and, like Cockerell, a Secretary of the Press, published a book-length history entitled The Kelmscott Press and William Morris Master-Craftsman.
Since then many other more specialized studies of the Press have appeared in print.7 How, then, can one justify still another book on the Kelmscott Press?
The short answer is that there is really only one extended history, Sparling's, which is by now very much out of date; since 1924 a great quantity of manuscript material relating to the Press's history - including letters, diaries, manuscripts, and corrected proofs of Kelmscott books – has become available to scholars.
The dispersal of Sir Sydney Cockerell's collection in 1956 and the acquisition by the Pierpont Morgan Library of the Kelmscott materials of John M.

## Introduction
Crawford, Jr., are only two notable instances of the emergence in recent decades of important new documentary evidence.
With these fresh records in hand, and with the calmer perspective that the passing of time usually brings, it ought (one hopes) to be possible now to write a history of the Kelmscott Press that avoids both the embarrassing proselytizing tone of Sparling's book and the biliousness of certain anti-Morris polemics published in the past.
The Kelmscott Press, despite — or perhaps because of — its fame, is a subject encrusted with myths and misconceptions.
It is surely worth the effort to try to set the record straight, if only because Morris's curious typographical experiment may force us to reflect on the meaning of that fascinating yet morally ambiguous technology which threatens today to envelop the world. # ONE THE WORLD OF VICTORIAN PRINTING HE history of the printed book may seem, at first glance, to be a perfect confirmation of Morris's view that the older crafts had steadily deteriorated in quality since the Middle Ages.
Printing', as Richard Durnford, Bishop of Chichester, put it trenchantly, is an art which beginning perfect, ever afterwards declined." And wno, comparing the typical massproduced volume of the nineteenth century - nastily printed in greyish ink on shoddy, acidic paper - with the Mainz Psalter or Gutenberg's Bible, could doubt that Morris and the Bishop were right?
Yet the story of the decay of printing is a good deal more complex than Morris ever acknowledged.
For him, it was all quite simple: the earliest printed books were still the best because they were produced at a time when the spirit of medieval craftsmanship was strong, but, with the advent of false historicism and a weakness for pseudo-classical ornament in the sixteenth century, printing, like architecture, began to decay.
Only in the second half of the eighteenth century, according to Morris, were the consequences of this typographical dry rot fully revealed in the sweltering hideousness of the Bodoni letter, the most illegible type that was ever cut'.
Bodoni and Didot, even more relentlessly than Baskerville (whose achievements Morris certainly underestimated), carried the development of the modern' typeface to its logical conclusion.
It was reserved for the founders of the later eighteenth century', wrote Morris, to produce letters which are positively ugly, and which, it may be added, are dazzling and unpleasant to the eye owing to the clumsy thickening and vulgar thinning of the lines. . . ." The nineteenth century's further contributions to this melancholy tale were increased mechanization and cheaper paper, ink, and binding materials.
When he reflected upon the contemporary printing scene, Morris found a small source of optimism only in the growing popularity of Caslon's old-face type and its imitations.
Because of the wide influence of Morris's typographical writings, this sweeping indictment of nineteenth-century printing became accepted dogma for several decades following his death in 1896.
There came in time the inevitable reaction against his oversimplifications and his doctrinaire hatred of the modern world.
Writing in an American typographical journal, the Dolphin, in 1938, Peter Beilenson remarked with obvious irony that ‘there had developed the general opinion that between the early great classical period of Didot, Bodoni, and Bulmer, and the late arts-and-crafts period of Morris, the printing of the century was one continuous sacrifice on the altars of Machinery and Mammon'.
In fact, he argued, many nineteenth-century printers had been preoccupied with the problem of quality, and technically bookmaking averaged at least the level of preceding centuries (except that papermaking and binding sought and found lower commercial levels) and . . . artistically the century offered a greater variety of good things, which on the whole unfortunately suit our present taste less than those of any preceding century'.3 Recently a less cautious revisionist, Ruari McLean, has insisted that ‘more exciting things happened in book design between 1837 and 189o than in any other comparable period in the history of the world's printing; and most of them happened in London'.4 (In fairness to McLean, it must be added that he is referring to the most sumptuous Victorian books; he elsewhere acknowledges that the ordinary commercially produced book of the last century was a very dismal thing indeed.) Not surprisingly, nineteenth-century printers themselves did not share Morris's low estimate of their achievements.
The trade journals of the time were filled with hymns of self-praise. ‘What wondrous strides have been made in almost every department of the Art!' the Printers' Register (6 July 1881) declared. ‘Hand-presses have become almost exterminated, pushed out by power-driven machines; typefaces . . . may now be counted by hundreds in their varieties; the uniformity of black ink, relieved on rare occasions by red initials, now gives way to mosaics ofevery imaginable colour, combined with gold, silver, and many-shaded bronzes; while borders, ornaments, picturetypes, engravings, and lithographs are made use of to an extent never before conceived.' The naïve enthusiasm for machinery — and the implication that technological advances always produce aesthetic triumphs - in this representative pronouncement may make us reluctant to attend very seriously to the testimony of Morris's contemporaries in the printing trade; but at least it should be observed that they rejected his assessment of their craftsmanship, for Victorian printers believed, or professed to believe, that nineteenth-century bookwork was as fine as that of any preceding era.
New technology — which Morris was to reject so dramatically at the Kelmscott Press — had completely changed the character of the printing trade in his day.
Before the third Earl Stanhope perfected an iroņ press in about 1 8oo, all printing — from Gutenberg's day onwards — had been done on a wooden press which was so simple in structure that it was frequently built by the local carpenter or joiner.
The earliest detailed pictorial representations of the ‘Common Press' (as it came to be called) appeared in the early decades of the sixteenth century, and they show the same unsophisticated mechanism that was still being used nearly three hundred years later.
If Morris had really been motivated solely by the antiquarian impulses that sometimes are attributed to him, he presumably would have reverted to the wooden press; there were still a few of them around in late-Victorian England, and they were occasionally seen in operation on special occasions such as the Caxton Exhibition in 1877.
But Morris recognized that the older presses were actually inferior to the iron hand-press of the nineteenth century.
One of the great drawbacks to wood-block printing in those times [the fifteenth century] was the weakness of the presses,' he wrote.
'Their only resource was to print with the paper very wet, and with very soft packing, so that the block went well into the paper; but many books . . . have suffered much from this cause.' Lord Stanhope's press, despite some enhancements, was in fact only a slightly improved version of the medieval press.
More dramatic technological developments appeared in the printing trade within two decades.
Friedrich Koenig invented a steam-driven cylinder press which was first used to print The Times on 29 November 1814, and for the rest of the century the presses that were employed by newspapers and mass-circulation magazines grew ever more elaborate and imposing.
By the 184os the machine-minders who tended these clanking monsters had come to resemble flies crawling over the skin of an enormous elephant.
In the latter half of the century the handpress (always iron by now) was used much less frequently even in book-printing; when Morris founded the Kelmscott Press in 1891 such presses were still readily available but were regarded as useful mainly for the printing of posters and the taking of proofs.
The growing mechanization of printing led to a reduction in the cost of books and periodicals.
Some observers at the time were overjoyed by this means of spreading literacy.
Charles Knight (a printer and publisher as well as a writer), in The Old Printer and the Modern Press (1854), argued fervently that the new technological developments were a heaven-sent gift that would lead to the wider distribution of books through book clubs and public libraries.
The Society for the Diffusion of Useful Knowledge, of which Knight was an active leader, was symptomatic of the energetic, cheerful Victorian response to the ever-faster spinning cylinders of the steam press.
It is also significant that a number of the edifying examples cited in Charles Babbage's On the Economy of Machinery and Manufactures (fourth edition, 1835) were drawn from the world of printing.
Figure 3 By the middle of the last century, this sort of massive, clanking press was commonly used for the printing of newspapers and magazines; even books were produced on somewhat smaller steam-driven cylinder presses.
Morris's adoption of the hand-press was a direct reaction to such technological developments.
Of course there were other voices, sometimes from within the trade, warning that the growing acceleration of the printing process was leading to a debasement of traditional standards in typography and materials.
T.
C.
Hansard, in his Typographia (1825), a technical manual for printers, gave an example of the problems that grew out of the new preoccupation with speed: The system of hurrying works through the press, lately adopted by some booksellers, by giving them among a variety of houses, is destructive of uniformity. . . .
It is not unusual to discover, in volumes, so divided, one to contain more lines in a page than another, or, even if the number of lines are the same, the page differs in length; or, should the type agree in depth, it is very probable that it varies in thickness; and still more probable that it varies in the quantity of work it has done; — all of which produce a glaring want of that uniformity which constitutes the beauty of the typographic art' (p.
394).
At the same time there was a noticeable cheapening of inks and papers.
Until the third decade of the century ink continued to be made from the usual ingredients — lampblack and linseed oil — according to the traditional recipes (one of which can be found in Moxon's Mechanick Exercises on the Whole Art of Printing, first published in 1683—4).
Soap began to find its way into ink, because ink thus diluted gave a sharper impression, and soon the chemists were able to provide a whole range of artificial substitutes for the old ingredients.
When Morris in the 189os insisted upon using an ink similar to that employed by printers of earlier centuries, it was seen as a revolutionary gesture.
Developments in paper-manufacturing were even more alarming.
With the supply of good white rags declining, paper mills began, in the final decades of the eighteenth century, to bleach inferior rags and to introduce gypsum and alum into the fibre.
The disastrous consequences were evident long before the Kelmscott Press came on the scene.
John Murray (not related to the publisher), a popular writer on scientific subjects, in 1823 contributed to the Gentleman's Magazine an article which was reprinted as a book the following year under the title Observatious and Experiments on the Bad Composition of Modern Paper.
After describing the disintegration of particular books, Murray suggested that ‘the chief causes of destruction consist in the employment of sulphate of lime, &c. in the pulp, and bleaching the rags previously, or the paper subsequently, with oxymuriatic acid gas.
(Chlorine.)' (p.
8).
Worse was to come.
In the latter half of the century book papers were more frequently made fron various rag substitutes, the poorest of which was mechanical woodpulp.
As a general rule (with naturally a few exceptions), the older a book, the more likely its paper is to be in good condition today; librarians around the world are overwhelmed by the prospect of the steady disintegration of nearly all books printed since the mid-nineteenth century.
To Morris, the most insidious aspect of modern book papers was that they frequently masqueraded as something better, with fake wire and chain lines (to suggest that they were handmade) and adulturants (to add weight and shininess).
In certain kinds of books and periodicals, he suggested, cheap paper was acceptable, but only if it frankly acknowledged its own cheapness and made no effort to disguise itself.
Probably the ordinary reader a century ago paid no more attention to such matters than readers nowadays do; the most appalling woodpulp paper, when it is new, may seem decent enough to the unpractised eye.
But changes in type-styles were sufficiently drastic to be obvious to even casual observers.
Everywhere around him — in posters, advertisements, shop signs, sometimes even in books — the nineteenth-century Englishman witnessed an unprecedented efflorescence of new type-designs.
Egyptians, grotesques, Gothics, Tuscans: their very names were mysterious and exotic, their shapes heavy and insistent, perfectly suited to the frenetic commercial atmosphere of the times.
Book-typography, meanwhile, had moved in the opposite direction, with letterforns that grew ever more delicate and fragile.
Historians of printing usually classify serifed typefaces designed primarily for bookwork into three major categories.
Except to specialists, however, the first two groups – Venetian and old face - look very much alike; on the other hand, the differences between old face and modern face are obvious to all.
Until the eighteenth century, despite a slow evolution in the shape of the Roman letter, typefaces used in books changed remarkably little.
Caslon's Roman types, for example, which began to appear in the 173os, are not fundamentally dissimilar from the type used by Aldus in his Hypuerotomachia Poliphili (1499), even though they are separated by more than two hundred Figure 4 Victorian typefounders produced a bewildering variety of new letterforms, as shown in John Parry's painting, A London Street Scene (1835).
These decorative, sometimes bizarre typefaces were used mainly in posters and advertisements, though occasionally they crept into books as well. years.
But in the generation after Caslon a ne w style of type-design was introduced throughout Europe, subsequently known as‘modern face', which was to have an immense influence on the appearance of books in Morris's century.
Although a Frenchman, Philippe Grandjean, is usually regarded as the progenitor of the modern face, it was left to Giambattista Bodoni, an Italian designer, and the French foundry of Firmin Didot to carry the new style to its logical conclusion.
In England, at about the same time, John Baskerville produced a transitional version of the modern face that seemed better suited to conservative tastes.
What all these varied type-designs had in common was vertical rather than oblique shading, unbracketed (i.e. unsupported) serifs, and greater contrast between thick and thin strokes.
The effect was — as Morris always said disparagingly — dazzling to the eye.
Whereas old-face types had grown directly out of the letterforms used by Renaissance scribes and Roman stone-cutters, the newly fashionable letters were geometrically precise and brilliant in their effect.
Old-face designs convey a mellow charm, like that of an ancient building; modern-face types suggest the smooth regularity of a well-made machine.
It is not difficult to understand why Morris repudiated modern face.
Though they are not to everyone's taste, the books of Bodoni and Baskerville are extremely handsome.
(Morris, needless to say, would never have admitted that.) Yet the eighteenth-century type-designers must be judged not only by their own types and books but also by the mainly unwholesome effect they had on their successors.
As Daniel Berkeley Updike remarked, Grandjean, Baskerville, Bodoni, and the Didots had a mischievous influence on type-forms; for the derivations from types that their work made popular culminated in a kind ofletter which was capable of greater vulgarity and degradation than was ever the case with old fonts.
The ordinary English, French, or Italian book printed between 1830 and 185o was very often a cheap and mean-looking production.
Perhaps Bodoni and other great persons were not wrong in their day; but they put type-forms on the wrong track." The modern-face types for books that evolved in the nineteenth century from the Didot-Bodoni models were bland, undistinguished, and boring, and they changed very little as the century went on, except to become progressively lighter.
It is also important to bear in mind that the evolution of type-designs is influenced by developments in technology as well as shifts in fashion.
With the introduction of the steam press, it became necessary to cast types in a harder metal than Quousque tandem abutêre, Catilina, patientiâ nostrâ ? quamdiu nos etiam furor iste tuus eludet? quem ad finem sese effrenata jactabit audacia? nihilne te noctur- M.

S there are so many objects of interest to be seen and described in the interior, it will be necessary to leave the coast for a timie.
The Land's End, following the carriage toad from Penzance, is about ten miles distant.
Hlundreds of visitors pass over this road in the summer time, and we also will now take this route, not hesitating to step aside occasionally ome curious monument or legendary spot.
The beautifully wooded grounds of Trereife, about a mile from the town, first attract attention; the roadway is here arched with a long avenue of noble elms ; near its extremity it is crossed by another avenue,--the road turning from the left leads to Newlyn,--a few yards up on the right is Trereife Hfouse, aimost buried in foliage ; a yew tree is trained over the front of the building, giving it the appeaiance of a living wall ol leaves.
I'he rooks have loug been established here in their "old ancestral trees," and we always know when spring is coming by their noisy preparations for building.
The walks and peeps of woodland scenery around this place are very beautiful.
The Sibthorpia Europea, previously noticed, is met with herc again, as it is indeed in alinost every rill in the district.
On the ascent of a hill, a little beyond Trereife, an old oak forins a complete archway over the road, and if you are on the seat of the carriage besidle the driver, he will probably tell you that it is the only instance of the kind to be met with on the journey from London to the Land's End.
A short distance from this, on the Ieft hand side of the road, stands an ancieut cross; such monuments are very numerous throughout the county, but more particularly in this western part.
They are pleasing and picturesque objects by the wayside, reninding us of interesting periods of our history.
The crosses in this district are probably the most ancient of any in England.
Some of the earlicst Figure 6 A typical late-Victorian book: J.
T.
Blight, A Week at the Land's End (Truro, 1893).
It is set in Old Style (a symptom of the growing disenchantment with modern-face types) and has dull wood-engravings and curious ornamented initials that owe something to the craze for ‘artistic' printing during that decade. previously, so that they could withstand greater abuse; and this in turn led to the discovery that it was now possible to make the strokes and serifs of letters much finer.
Such typefaces required less ink from mechanical roller systems, which functioned well only with thinner inks.
Later in the century it became increasingly common for printers to use dry, smooth papers on presses with a hard backing; whereas the ink on hairlines had once spread slightly on damp, more coarsetextured papers, it did not do so when the new technique of printing was employed.
From an engraver's point of view, new types so printed were exquisitely sharp and clean,' Theodore L.
De Vinne, the American printer, explained; ‘but from a reader's point of view, the general effect of the print was relatively mean and wiry, gray and feeble.
Each letter lost some of its individuality.
A new style of delicate but weak presswork came in fashion."8 De Vinne himself bore some responsibility for the developments that he lamented, for this style of printing came to England largely through the influence of American periodicals such as Harper's Magazine and Century Magazine (the latter printed by De Vinne), which had pioneered the technique of hard-packing in order to improve the reproduction ofillustrations.
However, the progressively lighter typefaces, though they may have had their origin in technical decisions, exactly suited the taste oflate-Victorian readers and printers, and there was much boasting in the trade journals of the time that the growing delicacy of types was a sign of increased refinement': ‘in the present day we see the effect of an artistic taste, and appreciation for delicacy of outline and freedom of ornamentation developed in the excessively light types and floral embellishments now so popular with the best printers,' according to a contributor to the Printers' Register (6 April 1885).
Even the heaviest styles were materially toned down to meet the current refinement of taste.' By contrast, older typefaces were condemned as heavy, clumsy, and crude.
It is in the context of this widespread preference for delicacy and lightness that Morris's almost oppressively dark types must be seen: discarding what seemed to him the attenuation and weakness of nineteenth-century typography, Morris reacted (over-reacted, some would say) by designing faces that were solid, black, and aggressively self-assertive.
But Morris was to argue on more than one occasion that typefaces alone did not determine the beauty and harmony of the printed page: even with mediocre type, if certain other principles of design were observed, it was possible to produce a satisfactory page.
Morris had profoundly held views on margins, for example, and nothing infuriated him more than the popular practice of placing the text virtually in the centre of a page.
That Morris was fighting a firmly entrenched eneny is illustrated by a series of articles written by De Vinne, the most skilful and best-informed printer in America, on Book Margins which appeared in the Printers' Circular (Philadelphia) between May and September 1871.
De Vinne, who certainly ought to have known better, offered the unconvincing argument that ‘the text or printed page is the picture, and the margin is the frame; all the proprieties require that the picture, when framed, shall be in the centre of the frame'(June 1871).
In fact, he was merely endorsing what printers on both sides of the Atlantic already did.
Similarly, there was a broad consensus - which was not to be seriously questioned until Morris and Emery Walker attacked it in the 189os — that generous spaces between words and lines of type improved legibility.
The combination of lighter typefaces and ample spaces produced a page in which the text appeared to be, at best, greyish.

## Yet
such a damning summary of Victorian printing practices fails to take account that printers of the last century were sometimes, when not subjected to the usual commercial pressures, capable of producing very impressive work.
All the printers' manuals of the day discussed what was variously known as fine press work', fine work', or (later in the century) simply fine printing'.
As the terms suggest, the printer who did this sort of thing earned his additional wages (double or more the usual rate) by taking greater care in the presswork, especially the make-ready; there appears to have been no distinction made between ordinary' and fine' composition.
The greatest achievements of printing during the last century undoubtedly are to be found in the striking technical skill with which illustrations were produced.
In the eighteenth century woodcuts had disappeared almost completely from books, which were illustrated instead with copperplate engravings, often quite elegant in themselves but bearing no relationship to the typography.
The engravings and the text pages were printed by entirely separate processes and were joined together only when the book was bound.
Morris regarded this as a pernicious development, because it destroyed that harmony which seemed to him the supreme attribute of a well-printed book.
The cautionary examples which Morris, Walker, and others in their circle frequently cited were Samuel Rogers's Italy (183o) and Poems (1834), both handsomely illustrated with steel engravings designed by Turner.
Albert L.
Cotton summarized the Morrisian view when he wrote in the Contemporary Review (August 1898): Beautiful as pictures, they bear no apparent relation to the volumes which they illustrate; their charm is independent and extraneous; the artist clearly did not concern himself to harmonise them with the text or with each other; taken apart entirely from the books, they would lose nothing of their force.
They are steel engravings, pure and simple, which might just as well have been issued separately in a portfolio.' With less justice, Morris brought the same accusation against Thomas Bewick,º who had revolutionized English book-illustration at the beginning of the century by perfecting the wood-engraving, which, unlike the woodcut, is done on the end grain of boxwood with graver's tools and is therefore capable of rendering very subtle effects.
Morris did not especially admire subtlety in art; he preferred the bold, simple outlines of medieval woodcuts to the intricate delicacy of Bewick's vignettes of English rural life.
Probably Morris also harboured an irrational prejudice against Bewick because the brilliant technique he had created degenerated very quickly into the facile, characterless style of wood-engraving that was to be found in so many English books and magazines for the remainder of the century.
Within a generation Bewick's successors were producing, on an unprecedented scale, illustrations that were both technically dazzling and aesthetically empty.
The facsimile' engraving reigned triumphantly in Victorian England.
Its supremacy was challenged finally in the 188os by a new mechanico-chemical process then known as zincography, which had been invented by Eberhard, a Bavarian.
This technology bypassed the human engraver entirely, since the artist's design was transferred photographically on to a zinc plate, which was then engraved by immersion in a chemical bath.
An illustration that previously had been engraved by hand over several days could now be prepared for the press in a few hours.
By the time the Kelmscott Press came into existence, wood-engraving had disappeared almost overnight, and process engravings were to be seen everywhere, even in the most expensively produced books.
Morris held zincography in contempt; it is one of the minor ironies of the Kelmscott Press that his chief adviser was Emery Walker, by profession a process engraver, who probably knew more about the latest developments in techniques ofillustration than anyone else in England.
In the end Walker was able to wring one small concession to modern technology from Morris: the borders and initials used at the Kelmscott Press were all printed from electrotypes, another new means of duplicating engravings.
Chromolithography was used less frequently in books, but it was capable of producing really splendid effects, as in some of the folios by Henry Noel Humphreys.
Morris, curiously enough, seems to have been intrigued by the process: in 1894 he and Walker spent a day at the Bodleian Library, Oxford, to investigate the possibility of reproducing a beautifully ornamented fourteenth-century manuscript known as the Douce Apocalypse, no doubt under the Kelmscott Press imprint.
Nothing came of their plans, but it is certainly significant that Morris regarded such an undertaking seriously.
(Morris probably had chromolithography in mind on this occasion, though he may have intended to call into service some form of photographic reproduction, such as the newly invented halftone process.) In a letter written earlier, in 1882, Morris suggested that an Indian carpet recently presented to the Boston Museum of Fine Arts might be reproduced by the same means: some of the chromo-lithographers are so good nowadays that a reproduction of a good & careful drawing of it would be of great service to those who are interested in such matters.'o Despite these occasional flickers of interest in the latest methods of facsimile illustration, the one subject that most deeply preoccupied Morris was type-design, and it was there, he felt, that the contemporary book displayed its gravest weakness.
Morris was not alone in his dissatisfaction with the modern-face type.
There had been several efforts earlier in the century, both in England and on the Continent, to revive old-face designs," but the one attempt which had the most far-reaching consequences for English printers, including Morris, was the reintroduction by the Chiswick Press of Caslon's old-face Roman in the 184os.
Notwithstanding an earlier debate among historians as to which books first appeared in Caslon type, recent scholarship has confirmed that it was The Diary of Lady Willoughby, a novel published by Longmans in the spring of 1844." At about the same time, however, the Chiswick Press was also setting up two other books entirely in Caslon: George Herbert's The Temple, which was issued later in 1844, and Juvenal's Satires, which appeared the following year.
Two points should be made about this revival of Caslon.
First, unlike modern printers in search of historic designs, Charles Whittingham the Younger, the proprietor of the Chiswick Press, was not compelled to have the Caslon type recut; he simply went to the firm founded by # Some Paffages from the Diary of Lady Willoug hby.

.

The Birds fang,and the Air was fweet with the Scent of the Wood-binde and the frefh Birch Leaves.

It represents the first revival of Časlon Old Face type in the nineteenth century; Morris was later to use Caslon in his commercially produced books.
Figure 8 William Pickering and Charles Whittingham often met in the latter's summer house at Chiswick to plan the books that they jointly produced.
Their books were among the finest printed in nineteenth-century England, and Morris himself learned much about the art of book-design from the Chiswick Press.
Caslon and discovered that the original matrices were still in storage.
Second, though the Caslon type was generally admired as attractive when it appeared in The Diary of Lady Willoughby, there was no rush by other printers to use it, for Caslon appeared too self-consciously archaic and odd to Victorian eyes.
Charles T.
Jacobi, who in Morris's day was manager of the Chiswick Press, said ofit: the excellent design of these different founts [i.e. the several sizes of Caslon] was not generally appreciated until about the year 1888, when the Arts and Crafts Exhibition Society first came into existence, and Mr.
Emery Walker was perhaps more responsible than anyone else for making known this series of faces.
Since that date these types . . . have gradually been adopted largely for the printing of the better class of bookwork, especially that of a more antiquarian nature. . . ."3 Caslon type does not look particularly remarkable to us today, because its frequent use in the early twentieth century has made it seem ordinary and very English'.
(In time Caslon became so closely identified with Englishness that it was adopted by at least some printers for patriotic motives.) Bruce Rogers, the distinguished American bookdesigner, complained in 1917 that Caslon was, if anything, excessively familiar — ‘a much overrated type', he said; its best quality was ‘a certain amount of irregularity, restful to the eye after the smoothness and monotony of our more modern letters'.'⁴ England seemed to him at the time a Caslon-ridden country'.
It was precisely the occasional clumsiness and crudeness of Caslon, so offensive to Rogers, which recommended it to Morris, and in the last decade of his life he adopted it enthusiastically for his own commercially printed books.
Morris in turn influenced Bernard Shaw to use it in his books from the late 189os onwards; this had the effect of bringing Caslon into even more general circulation.
Because Caslon seemed appropriate only for ‘period' typography to Victorian readers, the Edinburgh typefoundry of Messrs Miller and Richard offered their customers in 186o a design by A.
C.
Phemister called at first ‘Modernized Old Style' (later simply ‘Old Style'), which attempted to capture the flavour of Caslon without sacrificing many of the characteristics ofmodern-face type.
It also had the merit of being designed to work well with modern inks and papers.
Other typefounders soon sold their own versions.
Old Style was a deliberate compromise, and a good deal of scorn was poured on it through the years.
One of the earliest to register a protest was Emery Walker, who, though he admitted that it was more suitable for smooth-surfaced papers than Caslon, described it as ‘far inferior in beauty to types of the early printers'; similarly, Morris remarked that the design of the letters of this modern “old style" leaves a good deal to be desired, and the whole effect is a little too grey, owing to the thinness of the letters'.' Bruce Rogers found it at best inoffensive, at worst monotonous.
Whatever its limitations, Old Style proved to be an immensely popular typeface — by some estimates, indeed, the most widely used book face in England during the final decades of the century.
(Modern face, however, continued to be the preferred type in periodicals, school-books, and scientific works.) A writer in the Printers'Register (6 November 1884) offered the opinion that probably no variety of type at present in use has so firm a hold upon the cultivated taste of the printer or of the public'.
It was eventually supplanted in the second decade of the twentieth century by the revivals of historic types that began to emanate from the Monotype Corporation.
The resurrection of Caslon by the Chiswick Press in mid-century, followed by the introduction of Old Style by Miller and Richard, brought about gradual but important changes in the appearance of English books.
We must remind ourselves that the rejection of modern-face types had begun long before 1891 and that the Kelmscott Press was, in a sense, merely the most visible manifestation of the antiquarian winds which had been stirring the bibliographical waters in England for a number of decades.
The two figures who - apart, of course, from Morris — played the most decisive roles in sponsoring the revival of older types and ornaments were the publisher William Pickering and Charles Whittingham the Younger, the owner of the Chiswick Press.
Stanley Morison, in fact, has suggested that Morris's celebrated achievements as a printer were firmly rooted in the less spectacular work ofPickering, Pugin, and certain publishers associated with the Oxford Movement.
In a letter to Geoffrey Keynes, written in 1924, he remarked that ‘Pickering was the first to design books in an eclectico-antiquarian spirit: I mean that he was the first to go back to XVIth century Venice & Lyons for inspiration.
IfI am right Pickering is the tribal chief of the Morris-Ricketts-Rogers school."7 It is an astute observation: period typography - that is to say, typography which deliberately imitates the style of older books — has become so commonplace in the twentieth century that we assume it has always been with us, but the evidence suggests very strongly that it was invented by Pickering and several contemporaries of Catholic or Anglo-Catholic sympathies.
As early as 183o Pickering acquired a set of wood-engraved initials designed by Thomas Stothard, but it was a decade later when the ornamentation of the Chiswick Press-Pickering books began in earnest.
Inspired perhaps by the publication of Henry Shaw's Alphabets, Numerals and Devices of the Middle Ages (1845),18 Whittingham commissioned more than a dozen sets of initials from Mary Byfield and launched a series of sumptuous reprints — including several versions of the English Prayer Book — adorned with blackletter titles and attractive borders, initials, and tailpieces.
(Gladstone testified in 189o that ‘the pages of their Prayer Book are a perfect picture, reminding one of those early printed books which are still marvellous examples of printing'.9) Though Whittingham turned occasionally to other printers, his finest books were produced by the Chiswick Press, which had the deserved reputation of being the most distinguished old-style' printer in England during the latter half of the nineteenth century.
Even Morris had to serve what was, in effect, an apprenticeship at the Chiswick Press before he could set up a press of his own.
But there were other old-style printers, less frequently remembered today perhaps than the Chiswick Press but famous in their own time.
The firm of George Falkner and Sons in Manchester, for example, were celebrated for thelarge collection of woodcuts, decorated initials, and head- and tailpieces with which they ornamented pseudo-antique books.
Falkner productions were invariably described by reviewers or the firm itself as ‘quaint' — i.e. self-consciously antiquarian in flavour.
(It is significant that Morris never uses the wordquaint' when writing about his own variety of old-style printing.) The Falkners had set up a wooden hand-press in ‘the quaint Caxtonian Printing Offices in Old Manchester', and their printer's mark, designed by Walter Crane, showed a pressman at work on a hand-press.
Printers like Falkner and Sons issued a good deal of ephemera as well as books: their Old Style Calendar for 1882', for instance, contained ‘many delightful and entertaining particulars, very commodious indeed and profytable, worth the labour of your reading.
(A reprint from many early Kalendars.)'Their book titles included The Shakespeare Show Book and The Old English Fayre Volume; many of the books were stained both internally and externally to suggest great age.2° (This sort of fakery was, needless to say, the typographical equivalent of the fraudulent restoration of ancient buildings which Morris condemned near the end of his life.) # AT YE REFECTORIE HERE willen ben moche goode Suflenaunce and Cunnynge Drynkes ferven for alle fike as ben wearied wythe ye prefs or wythe moche Pourchafynge, wythe Cakes for Yule-tyde, ornymented for y Chyldrene, and manie Boxes of goode Thynges.
