It shows four books in progress: The Earthly Paradise, The Water of the Wondrous Isles, Sigurd the Volsung, and The Śundering Flood. the British Museum.43 The story may be apocryphal, since the Museum's records do not indicate that Mrs Peddie held a reader's ticket during the 188os or 189os; but if it is true, it lends wonderful irony to an irrelevant attack upon typewriters made by Sparling in his history of the Kelmscott Press.44 (Sparling's surviving letters, incidentally, are all typed.)

## 率 Journalists,
after visiting the Kelmscott Press, often rhapsodized about the extreme simplicity of the equipment used by Morris's printers: It was like stepping into Caxton's shop' (or words to that effect) was the usual response.
One writer, so overwhelmed by the medieval splend– our of the Press, even made Kelmscott House four hundred years older than it was.4 In fact, as we have seen, Morris was using nineteenthcentury hand-presses - Albions - that had been commonplace fixtures in nearly every English printing office until the middle of the century.
The hand-press did of course seem slightly archaic by 1891, but it was still widely used for pulling proofs, printing posters, and producing limited editions; hence we must avoid the vulgar error of admiring Morris's reversion to medieval technology.
It should be observed that Morris had no great difficulty in finding printers who were skilled in the use of the hand-press, for what he revived at the Kelmscott Press was a handicraft whose survival had been threatened only within the previous three or four decades.
Nevertheless, Morris was engaged in a deliberate simplification of the printing process when he founded his Press.
But, unless one returns to scribal methods (which Morris always longed to do), books cannot be manufactured without machinery of some sort; even an Albion press is a machine, simple though it may be.
The choices that Morris made in acquiring equipment for the Kelmscott Press were complicated by his well-known aversion to mechanical devices, yet his hostility appears to have been directed primarily against what he called the great intangible machine of commercial tyranny' rather than this or that tangible steel and brass machine'.46 His types were cast mechanically; his inks were manufactured in large factories; he used photography constantly as a labour-saving device at the Kelmscott Press; Frank Colebrook records that Morris even visited and admired the modern printing establishnent of R. and R.
Clark in Edinburgh, and that a Linotype salesman who called at the Press positively escape[d] with a whole skin'.47 Morris's attitude towards Victorian technology was altogether more ambivalent than some of his doctrinaire disciples supposed.
The small cottages strung along Hammersmith's Upper Mall in which Morris housed his Press were as unpretentious as the equipment the printers used.
The rent book4⁸ for 16 Upper Mall, the first home of the Kelmscott Press, shows that Morris's tenancy began on 12 January 1891; it ended in June of the same year, because in the meantime (from 2o May⁴9) Morris had begun leasing Sussex Cottage, 14 Upper Mall, which remained the primary building ofthe Press until its closing.
In 1893 Morris persuaded Cobden-Sanderson to lease 15 Upper Mall, opposite Sussex Cottage, for the Doves Bindery, with two rooms to be reserved for the Kelmscott proof-readers: one of Cobden-Sanderson's workers recalled that ‘this brought Morris in and out many times a day, and with his marvellous interest in the detail of all craft-work, he made himself a familiar figure to the employés . . .'.sº This, however, was only a temporary arrangement, for on New Year's Day 1895 Morris took possession of still another small house, 21 Upper Mall (Sunderland Cottage), which was used for the large Albion press printing the Chaucer and for Cockerell's office.
W.
Irving Way, the American publisher, visited the Kelmscott Press in April 1895, and the vivid (albeit slightly sentimentalized) description he offers is probably of Sunderland Cottage: No sign marks the Kelmscott Press, . . . but . . . the right one is finally reached, and here Mr.
S.
C.
Cockerell . . . greets the visitor with a smile, and at once puts him at ease.
A quiet, tidy, orderly place it is, but with nothing modern about it.
No noise of machinery, escaping steam, or hum of electric motor, distracts one.
No mahogany furniture, Axminster rugs, or click of type-writer reminds one of nineteenth century progress.
The furniture is plain deal like the floor, and with a “gray goosequill" the visitor registers his arrival in the guest-book."

## 率 The
modesty of the Kelmscott Press's machines and buildings, though it had an ideological basis, also reflected Morris's limited goals.
His original intention had been to print only a small number of books — just enough of each title, perhaps, to distribute to his friends — and he did not at first envision beconing his own publisher.
Morris's publisher during the 187os and early 188os was F.
S.
Ellis, but in 1888 he turned to Reeves and Turner, who operated a bookshop and small publishing establishment in the Strand.² Thus there was nothing unexpected about Morris's decision to allow Reeves and Turner to publish most of the early Kelmscott books.
Nevertheless, those close to Morris grumbled occasionally about the inefficiency they detected in his publishers. ‘You don't see [Morris's] books on bookstalls and I think it's for want of a businesslike publisher,' Burne-Jones commented; Jane Morris, in a similar vein, described them as the most unbusiness-like publishers but extremely honest'.
Reeves and Turner published eleven of the Kelmscott Press books, the last of which was More's Utopia, issued in September 1893, and Morris's connection with them was officially severed in June 1 896.4 His relationship with Reeves and Turner, while it lasted, seems to have been happy, but he was uneasy about any suggestion that the Kelmscott Press books were less than completely his own productions.
Reeves and Turner were merely the distributors for the Kelmscott Press, not the publishers in the usual sense, and Morris emphasized this in the formula he adopted in the Kelmscott colophons: ‘Sold by Reeves & Turner.' (In the proofs of The Defence of Guenevere that line read Published by Reeves & Turner' but was changed.) I don't mind having a publisher', Morris declared, ‘so long as he has nothing whatever to do with any question except purely business ones.' One possibility that he considered briefly was to print a book and then sell the entire edition at auction, thus eliminating the need for a publisher at all.s6 Four of the Kelmscott Press books were published by Bernard Quaritch, who, following his arrival from Berlin in 1842, had rapidly made himself London's leading bookseller.
To his first English employer, Henry G.
Bohn, Quaritch announced, ‘Mr.
Bohn, you are the first bookseller in England, I mean to become the first bookseller in Europe.'7 Energetic, aggressive, learned, Quaritch was a very different person from nice, inoffensive old William Reeves, and there were serious misunderstandings between him and Morris from the beginning.
Since the price of The Golden Legend was not specified in the contract, Quaritch raised it on the day of publication and eventually doubled it. ‘The trade, being dissatisfied with the terms which Mr.
Q. offered to supply them, did not push the book at the time, & the result was that a good many copies remained on Mr.
Q's hands,' Cockerell wrote two years later.s8 There was other evidence of what Morris took to be sharp dealing by Quaritch, and early in 1893 he reached a decision that infuriated Quaritch.
Mr.
W.
Morris wishes to have all the profits of the Kelmscott press,'Quaritch wrote to his son in America on 8 February; he will retail himself the [“]Godfrey of Bouloyne" — he will be a loser by it, but it will be a nuisance to me on account of the Chaucer.'9 Morris was at first conciliatory: It is true that I shall probably publish the Sidonia myself, as I have done with the Godfrey, but I do not suppose I shall generally publish the Kelmscott books myself,'he declared in a letter to Quaritch the following July.6° Yet this was precisely what he did do.
Quaritch, who had obviously hoped to be the publisher of the Chaucer, was furious.
Morris's decision therefore had the unfortunate effect of poisoning the later negotiations with Quaritch about the number of copies of the Chaucer — and on what terms — he would be supplied.
Mr.
Wm Morris will call on me tomorrow,' Quaritch informed his son on 20 August 1894; he is laying down the law to me about the Chaucer, — and I must submit.
Figure 59 Bernard Quaritch, London's leading bookseller, commissioned The Golden Legend, the most ambitious book issued by the Kelmscott Press during the early years ofits existence.
Later he and Morris quarrelled about publishing arrangements for some of the other Kelmscott books.
This has cooled my ardour to obtain the transfer of his publications from Reeves & Turner.
Mr.
Morris's success has turned him into a despot.'The following day Quaritch reported that ‘W.
Morris did not dare to show his face' but instead sent Cockerell (a very sharp fellow’) as his representative. ‘My friendship with Morris is at an end, lamented Quaritch.
In the autumn they reopened negotiations, and at last Quaritch felt satisfied with the trade discount he was to receive on the Chaucer.
Mr.
William Morris & I are on the road of being reconciled, he sees now how hastily he acted,'Quaritch announced triumphantly to his son on 12 November 1894.
But it was a partial victory at best, because Quaritch was never allowed to publish another Kelmscott book.
Several publishers — Ellis and Elvey, George Allen, Macmillan, and Way and Williams — commissioned books to be printed at the Kelmscott Press, and, at F.
S.
Ellis's suggestion, Ellis and Elvey even proposed that they be allowed to succeed Reeves and Turner.
Yet there was little inducement for Morris to seek the services of another publisher, for J. and J.
Leighton, his binder, performed some of the same functions as Reeves and Turner (the books were sent directly to buyers and book dealers fron Leighton), and Morris was eager to replace, as much as possible, the middlemen between the craftsman and his customers.
Acting as his own publisher, as Sparling pointed out, also gave Morris exact information with regard to the real and immediate sale of each book, and thus placed him in a position to guard against over-printing'.º Many of the Kelmscott Press books, especially the 16mos and octavos, were completely sold before they were off the press.
Any advertising of the books was at first done by Quaritch, and Reeves and Turner, but in May 1892 Morris issued a simple, two-page list indicating the titles already and soon to be printed at the Kelmscott Press, and periodically thereafter he announced his books by means of these lists (which grew longer and more elaborate) and similar circulars describing individual volumes, some of the latter containing specimen pages.
We only send out special announcements of books which are not nearly sold out when they go to the binder,'Cockerell explained.63 They proved to be an effective, low-key method of advertising: in all fifty-one such circulars were issued by the Kelmscott Press, and they are prized by collectors today.
Because the Kelmscott Press was operated in the glare of publicity – the British Bookmaker (July 1892) summed up a popular attitude when it declared simply, ‘The doings of the Kelmscott Press are of interest' — Morris did not have to advertise his books widely.
Every bibliophile, bookseller, and librarian in the English-speaking world seemed to be aware ofthe existence of the Kelmscott Press.
Even when the Press was attacked in print, its fame increased.
And strong attacks there were: someone writing in 1895 under the pseudonym ofArthur Pendenys' addressed an open letter to Morris which began, I presume that the Kelmscott Books are published for your own amusement, because I have inquired extensively and find they do not amuse any one else.
They are very dull books, much more dull than Law books and Medical books, and there are only two on your list, the Shakespeare Sonnets and the Keats, which I should prefer to a Post Office Directory.'4 The letter went on to condemn Morris's prices, types, and every aspect of the Kelmscott books.
Morris was presumably enraged when he saw this abusive yet amusing document, but he might have found consolation in the commonsense principle — subsequently elevated to a divine truth in Hollywood — that bad publicity is better than no publicity at all.
None the less, it was necessary for the Kelmscott Press to communicate with its potential customers in some systematic fashion.
The Press's list ofAddresses to which announcements were sent [in] 1897' has survived (in the Morgan Library), and it makes instructive reading.
The document includes more than 8oo names - mostly book dealers — of whom twenty-one were Americans (We have at present very few private customers in America,' Cockerell wrote in 1894°), with several other nationalities represented; among the names of individuals 24, 1891.

The Chaucer, in a deleted passage, is described as being ‘in preparation', though it was not to be published until 1896. are Bernard Shaw, Ellen Terry, George Meredith, A.
Edward Newton, Holman Hunt, C.
R.
Ashbee, F.

Furnivall, and Luther A.
Brewer (identified as State Inspector of Illuminating Oils, Cedar Rapids, Iowa’).
Walker was later to make use of this same list at the Doves Press.6 Morris and Burne-Jones often protested that only a handful of people had any interest in the Kelmscott books (I do the books mainly for you and one or two others; the public does not really care about them à damn,' Morris confided to Philip Webb67), but the size of the address list and the actual sales of the books belie this claim.
Morris offered a fairer appraisal when he wrote to Quaritch before the publication of The Golden Legend: for my part I feel sure there will be a moderate public who will like it very much.'68 There was a small group of customers who were prepared to buy anything Morris printed at the Kelmscott Press.
In March 1898, when he was closing down the Press, Cockerell sent Jane Morris a list of twelve names (ten British, two American) of persons who owned either all the Kelmscott books or all those written by Morris.69 And Morris always took care to see that his closest friends received complimentary copies: a letter from Cockerell specifying those who were to be given the second volume of The Poetical Works of Percy Bysshe Shelley mentions Burne-Jones, his wife Georgiana, Philip Webb, Emery Walker, Elizabeth Burden (Jane's sister), Theodore Watts, Swinburne, Kate Faulkner, May (Morris) Sparling, and F. and R.
Smith (the last two employees of Morris and Company).7° Review copies were, according to Cockerell, posted to less than half a dozen of the London newspapers, though the Daily News and the Daily Chronicle, whose political views Morris approved, were consistently on this short list." Despite the commercial success of the Kelmscott books in general, there were naturally variations in this pattern from one volume to another, and Morris never ceased to worry about the possibility of printing too many copies.
The first Kelmscott book, The Story of the Glittering Plain, stunned everyone when the entire edition was disposed of long before publication — indeed, long before even the price was announced by Reeves and Turner - and Morris found himselfshort of copies for his friends.7² Another of the early books, The Poems of William Shakespeare, was probably the most popular volume ever issued by the Press.
At the other extreme, The History of Godefrey of Boloyne, as glumly predicted by Quaritch, sold so badly that Jane Morris had to donate the overstock to British public libraries in 1897.
Morris abandoned one of his announced books, Vitas patrum, when the advance subscriptions proved to be too meagre.
The last circular announcement distributed by the Kelmscott Press - dated 25 February 1898 — reveals that, as the Press was winding down its operations, it still had on hand copies of the following books: The History of Godefrey of Boloyne (two), The Well at the World's End (eleven), Sidonia the Sorceress (six paper, two vellum), and The Tale of Beowulf (thirteen).
Morris was distressed to discover that many of the Kelmscott books were being bought by dealers who held them in stock until their value rose — as it did almost immediately.
A contributor to the Contemporary Review in 1898, praising the Chaucer, adopted an unfortunately apt figure of speech when he wrote that the book's paper was firm and crisp to the touch like the paper of a Bank of England note'.73 Works printed in the Golden type generated real gold in the sale rooms, though none ofit found its way into Morris's pocket.
Periodicals such as the Connoisseur and the Scottish Review began to publish league tables in which the escalating prices of Kelmscott books were charted in stupefying detail.74J.
H.
Slater, an experienced observer of the book trade, commented in 1899 that the Kelmscott books will soon be unprocurable except at a ruinous price'.7 Though, like other examples of fine printing, the Kelmscott Press volumes have fluctuated in market value from time to time (especially during the first two decades of this century), the trend to this day has been upward: one could not reasonably expect in 1987, for example, to pay less than £6,5oo for a paper Chaucer or $\mathcal { L } ^ { \mathrm { ~ I ~ } 3 }$ ,ooo for a vellum copy.
(And these figures continue to escalate unpredictably, for as this book went to press, newspapers throughout the world were trumpeting the astonishing price of \$330,000 realized by a vellum Chaucer sold at Christie's, New York, in May 1989.) P.
H.
Muir once remarked that ‘anyone well well informed on book-collecting history could venture a shrewd guess at the state of the money-market from the current price of a Kelmscott Chaucer'.76 As an old bookseller, Ellis professed to be bewildered by this development but could scarcely conceal his professional excitement.
Chaucer $\oint 2 7 ~ -$ at Sothebys last week.
We shall (some of us) live to see it bring $\mathcal { L }$ 100 – . . . ,’ he wrote to Cockerell in 1899.
And in another letter later that spring: The K.P. books keep up in a wonderful way!
Shelley £23 — & Keats £26!
And we were told they were dear at $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { - } 3 .
1 5 - \& \ 3 \mathrm { o } \big \vert - . } \end{array}$ What a comedy we live in.'77 While Éllis secretly rejoiced, Morris - who saw this happening in his lifetime — was frustrated by his inability to control the book speculators.
I am afraid that the “forestallers and regraters’ got most of the benefit,' he said in an interview in 1 895; but although I don't like that, I don't see what's to be done to alter it as things are.'78 The two unfamiliar terms of condemnation that Morris used are medieval words meaning, respectively, those ‘who bought up produce to hold it for a rise' and those ‘who bought and sold in the market or within five miles of it'.79 But although Morris would not tolerate the freewheeling entrepreneurial style of Quaritch (a ‘vulture’, Cockerell called him in private, no doubt reflecting Morris's opinion8°), he could not prevent his books from falling into the hands of other, even more unscrupulous, forestallers and regraters.
Another form of capitalistic exploitation of his goods that Morris resented was the pirating of the Kelmscott Press types and ornaments, particularly in the United States where he had no legal protection.
Morris insisted that he was concerned not about preservation of private property but about the improper use of his designs, which often appeared in books printed in weak modern typefaces on cheap paper.
To G.
W.
Jones he remarked, Men have a perfect right to use my designs, they have a perfect right to copy me if they can use my designs rightly, but not if they cannot use them.
Every man has a right to everything he can use well in this world, but only to what he can use well.'8 In practice, these high-principled sentiments translated into a consistent refusal by Morris to allow his types and ornaments to be employed by another printer.
He reluctantly authorized the periodical Bookselling to reproduce several of his borders but grew agitated when E.
F.
Strange requested permission to use them as an illustration in a book as well.
Morris allowed them to appear there as it is an ephemeral publication, but he does not think them very satisfactory, & would not like to see them in a permanent book', Cockerell declared in a letter to Strange.8² Even when George Allen reprinted three of the Kelmscott Press books under the title Old French Romances Done into English by William Morris (1896), Morris declined to let him make use of the engraved title-pages which had appeared in the Kelmscott editions.83 But the Kelmscott Press had scarcely issued its first book before trouble began to brew in the United States.
Roberts Brothers of Boston, who in 189o had published an apparently unauthorized edition of News from Nowhere, wrote to Morris in 1891 ordering some copies of the Kelmscott Glittering Plain and requesting permission to produce an American edition of Poems by the Way from early sheets.
I will send them sheets on the definite understanding that they are not to attempt to imitate the style of the typography,'Morris informed Reeves and Turner.84 What Roberts Brothers did instead was to create a photographic facsimile of The Glittering Plain, which they issued in a limited edition of 50o copies in October 1891.
News of still more alarming developments came from across the Atlantic.
In early 1894, while Morris was preparing an edition of Rossetti's Sonnets and Lyrical Poems for Ellis and Elvey, the latter unexpectedly received from another Boston publisher, Copeland and Day, a version of The Housę of Life done in elaborate imitation of the Kelmscott manner.
With three borders and 114 initials designed by Bertram Grosvenor Goodhue, The House of Life sold for \$2.50 (ordinary edition) and \$5.oo (Michallet paper, rubricated initials).
However much we may disapprove of this Publication, & the bad imitation of the Kelmscott Press we fear we cannot help it,' Ellis and Elvey wrote to William Michael Rossetti, who was seeing the Kelmscott edition through the press. ‘They do not tell us what the price is but from their letter we infer it is comparatively cheap.'The only consolation Ellis and Elvey could offer him, a few days later, was that ‘it is not likely to interfere with the Kelmscott edition'.8 The reaction at Kelmscott House was less restrained.
Morris himself was ‘very scornful' of the American imitation but temporarily tricked his wife into believing it was one of his own books; Jane, once enlightened, described it as ‘an impudent swindle'.86 The Roberts Brothers and Copeland and Day volumes were merely the first trickle of the great flood of Kelmscott-style books to be produced in the United States during the last decade of the century, most of then printed in types closely patterned after Morris's.
Have you heard of the wrong they've [i.e. the Americans] just done him. . .?’Burne-Jones demanded of his studio assistant in January 1896. ‘Some wicked wretch over there has been photographing and copying all his letters and ornaments, and those he reproduces exactly he calls the Jenson letters, and those he mommocks [sic] and alters and spoils he calls Morris letters.
That is irritating, isn't it?
He's in a great rage, and it's no wonder."87 The fame of the Kelmscott Press had travelled rapidly across the ocean, and photoengraving techniques made it absurdly easy for American typefounders to reproduce – and modify to any extent they wished – Morris's designs.
Either Joseph W.
Phinney of the Dickinson Type Foundry (Boston) or H.
L.
Bullen Figure 61 Jenson Old-Style type, patterned after Morris's first typeface, had a considerable vogue in the United States and elsewhere during the last decade of the nineteenth century.
THE JENSON OLD-STYLE SERIES, MADE BY AMERICAN TYPE FOUNDERS' CO.
(DICKIN-SON TYPE FOUNDERY BRANCH), BOSTON.
N THIS specimen the Dickinson Typefoundery (Boston) exhibits a variation of the types of Nicholas Jenson, a Frenchman, who at Venice, in 1470, founded the true Roman, which thereafter dominated European typography.
William Morris, of the Kelmscott Press, has modified these shapes from the originals, but in all the essentials they belong to the Jenson font.
The form is round and clear, with great boldness and dignity; the lines are full of strong black and white effects, adapted to old-style work.
Jenson derived his Roman characters from a mixture of alphabets of various countries, modelling these letters into marvellous perfection.
The original font is on about a 14-point size, and consists of twenty-three capital alphabets (J, U and W then not being in use); twenty-three lower-case characters, fifteen contractions, six double letters and three points (period, colon and interrogation), making a total of seventy-three punches.
Aldus Manutius finally inherited these punches, matrices an types, and later became famous for the fine alphabets designed by himself.
The attempt to lessen Jenson's fame as the originator of the present forms of Romans by crediting their use to the Weidenbach monks, at their monastery, in 1465 (five years before Jenson's publication), has not succeeded with thoughtful students.
Specimensof the Satanick Letters from theAmerican Cype-founde rs'Company of the United States distill'd Figure 62 The Satanick ty pe, an obvious copy of Morris's Troy type, as advertised in an Amcrican periodical, the Inland Printer, in 1896. of the American Type Foundry (Jersey City, N.J.), or possibly both (the historical evidence is ambiguous), approached Morris with the proposal that they make his types and ornaments commercially available in the United States.
Mr.
Morris wrote back and told them to go to a hot place,' according to Bullen, and Phinney therefore proceeded without his permission, manufacturing initials, borders, and fourteen sizes of a typeface called Jenson Old Style which was similar to the Golden type.88 An imitation of the Troy type named Satanick (allegedly to commemorate Morris's heated response to Phinney's letter) followed, and by mid-1895 the advertising pages of American printers' trade journals were awash with announcements of pseudo-Kelmscott types and ornaments in every conceivable shape and size.
Under various names many of them became available throughout Europe.
Eventually some of these reproductions reached England, where Morris and Cockerell struggled to keep them off the market.
Early in 1896, for example, Cockerell was consulting a solicitor about the piracy by an American firm of a great quantity of Kelmscott initials & ornaments' which were being sold in England (Cockerell diary, 15 and 2o January 1896).
A more serious problem arose in December 1897 when Cockerell discovered that Sir Charles Reed and Sons, Morris's own typefounders, were advertising an American imitation under the name Kelmscott Old Style' in their latest specimen sheet.
Cockerell, always an alarming adversary when he thought Morris's interests were in jeopardy, moved against the Reeds at once.
Threatened with legal action, they agreed to withdraw the offending specimens, to announce that the name of Kelmscott could not be used by any customers who had already purchased the type, and to surrender the matrices to Cockerell.
It was total capitulation.
Reeds have climbed down & are reprinting their spec[ime]ns but I don't feel very warmly towards them,' Cockerell wrote grimly.89 The anxiety that Morris felt about American imitations of the Kelmscott types and books indicates more than an artistic concern that his designs might be used inappropriately.
Like the Victorian industrialists that he condemned, Morris was trying to protect his market against cheap foreign imports.
This point is worth emphasizing because, whenever one attempts to assess the business side of any of Morris's activities, the evidence suddenly becomes obscured by a smoke-screen of socialist piety.
Jeanette L.
Gilder, for instance, interviewed Morris in the last year of his life and recorded this characteristic exchange: I asked Mr.
Morris to tell me, if it was not an impertinent question, whether it paid him pounds, shillings and pence to print such handsome books.
He answered that it did; but his secretary [Cockerell], who had just entered the room, begged leave to contradict this statement.
“You have not," said he, “counted your work and your time, Mr.
Morris.
You are thinking only of the mechanical part; but if you put in your time and your skill, at what you would be paid for them by another person, you could hardly say that your publications made money."Mr.
Morris smiled but said no more on the subject.
The profit did not seem to interest him very much: it was the artistic results which he accomplished that pleased him.'9 Cockerell's point is of course well taken: Morris's own invaluable contributions were never appraised in calculating the profits and losses of the Kelmscott Press, and we must never forget the genuinely impressive idealism that lay behind the whole of Morris's career, including the Kelmscott Press.
Nevertheless — whatever Miss Gilder may have supposed — he was indeed preoccupied with the profits made by his books.
This is not to say the profit motive was his main concern at the Press, which really was, as he said, a typographical adventure and therefore financially risky, but Morris never lost sight of the need to sell books and to pay his employees.
This unexpected combination of hard-headed business sense and Utopian idealism has puzzled many admirers of Morris, and it has prompted others to accuse hin of hypocrisy.
Morris himself, as one might expect, had thought long and deeply about the problem.
He argued that, until the socialist revolution came, the employer was obliged to work within the capitalist system, no matter how much he might personally despise it.9 What Morris failed to take sufficiently into account was how tainted even the most pure craft activity might become through commercial considerations; this, after all, is what happened to the Kelinscott Press, which began as an attempt to restore aesthetic (and moral) integrity to bookmaking and ended as the publisher of expensive books purchased mainly by wealthy collectors.
Morris's friend and fellow-socialist, Walter Crane, saw very clearly the extent to which the achievements of craftsmen and artists were coloured by the corrupting ethos of the market-place.
My difficulty', he wrote to Ashbee in 189o, . . . is how to establish any good system of working or teaching while the present economic system exists to work its destruction ultimately.
Do what one will it seems one is bound to be devoured by commercialism in some form or another."92 Another source of difficulty in analysing the financial state of the Kelmscott Press is that Morris, despite his business acumen, took a decidedly casual attitude towards record-keeping.
When he sent Crane a cheque for his share of the profits from the second version of The Glittering Plain, Morris promised to get an account made out which I shall take care is not too much humbugged by the accountant, who (any one of them) could easily make out that there was no profit at all.
But I will see that it is all fair, as I really know what the profit is effectually.'93 There was a confusing overlap between Morris's personal bank account and that of the Press, and during Sparling's time as Secretary the financial records, like much else at the Kelmscott Press, were in disarray.
In future we may be able to keep a closer record of expenditures than hitherto,' Cockerell reported to the Press's accountant in August 1894, shortly after replacing Sparling.94 In addition to invoices and various other kinds of scattered records, there exists a ledger (in the Morgan Library) describing the cost of production of all the individual Kelmscott books printed between 1893 and 1896 as well as other profit-and-loss accounts and inventories; but the ledger has unfortunately not yet been subjected to careful scrutiny by scholars familiar with nineteenth-century bookkeeping conventions, and so it is not now possible to say with absolute certainty how often, and to what degree, Morris realized a profit on his books.
The safest generalization one can offer at present is that he nearly always broke even (bearing in mind, again, that his services were unpaid) and that sometimes he came out slightly ahead.
Selecting one title completely at random, for instance, one can readily calculate, based on the most conservative assumptions, that Morris's gross receipts from More's Utopia, an octavo published in an edition of 3oo paper copies (thirty shillings) and eight vellum copies (ten guineas), could not have been less than £3oo, whereas it cost only about £255 to produce.
Morris was eager to explain to journalists that he was not a bloated capitalist: You have, no doubt, heard a great deal of the enormous profit I must be making on these books,' he told an interviewer in 1895, because of the high prices I ask for them.
I assure you that, if the people who go about talking of my profits were to see my balance sheet, they would speak quite differently.'9s The two examples he cited were Beowulf, which he said he sold at a loss, and the Chaucer, which represented Morris's largest investment and thus caused him much apprehension before it was finally published.
When he feared that word about his ink troubles with the Chaucer might spread to the public, he wrote in great agitation to F.
J.
Furnivall (who had a notoriously loose tongue): I must beg you not to say a word about it to anybody or you will do me very serious injury.' Only after the pressrun of the Chaucer was increased from 325 to 425 copies did Morris feel secure from financial disaster.
On the other hand, Morris sold his smaller books without difficulty and, as a matter of deliberate policy, used them to finance the printing of the folios and quartos.
I count rather on these little ones for getting cash to carry on the Chaucer, as the little books pay me much better than the big,'Morris admitted to Quaritch.97 There is also evidence to suggest that Morris squeezed every penny he could from publishers who commissioned Kelmscott Press books: Maud, for example, cost him only £2o2 14s.
1d. to produce (according to the Press ledger), yet he charged Macmillan £4oo for the paper copies alone.98 It is obvious that the slow, careful methods of design and production followed at the Kelmscott Press increased the cost of the books, and there are thirteen known instances of alterations being made in the Kelmscott volumes after printing had begun, usually involving the resetting and reprinting of a signature or a pair of conjugate leaves.99 Another unusual expense at the Press was Batchelor's paper, for which he charged about five or six times the price of machine-made book paper; Walker pointed out that the cost of the paper in a single copy of the Chaucer was approximately 25s.1º On the other hand, Morris recovered some of this expenditure by allowing a smaller trade discount than was usual, a policy which caused occasional friction between him and the booksellers.
For Reeves and Turner, and Quaritch, the discount was 2o per cent; for all other booksellers, and for a few friends like Robert Proctor, it was 15 per cent.
There is an undated Kelmscott Press circular printed in the Golden type and addressed ‘To the Trade only' which reads as follows: ‘'We send you the enclosed, and shall be glad to receive your orders.
The discount to the trade will be 15 per cent., and we must ask you to understand that these teris are strictly for cash.
We shall write you when the book is actually ready for delivery, and on receipt of your cheque your order will be executed.' The tone of this communication would not have been uncongenial to Dickens's Mr Bounderby.
Beneath the beauty and elegance of the Kelmscott Press books lay a hard, flinty foundation of commercial shrewdness.
When we are tempted to burst into rhapsodical praise for Morris's triumphant revival of medieval craftsmanship, it is useful to remember that. # SEVEN THE STORY OF THREE BOOKS HE Kelmscott Press books, as we have seen already, come in a variety of sizes and forms: some are long (Chaucer, 564 folio pages), some short (Epistola de contemptu mundi, sixteen pages); a few are heavy with ornaments (Godefrey of Boloyne), while others are decorated only with a few small initials and a border on the first text page; a minority are typographically elaborate (Atalanta in Calydon (1894) has three typefaces on the title-page), yet many of the books are almost austerely simple in design.
Despite Morris's preference for medieval texts, there is also literary diversity in the Kelmscott books, which reprint works written over a span of more than a thousand years.
For these reasons it is difficult, indeed dangerous, to offer broad generalizations about how the books of the Kelmscott Press were produced.
The process by which Morris designed and printed the Epistola, for example, was very different from that followed in manufacturing The Well at the World's End, which was in production from 1 892 to 1896 (the Interminable', Morris called it).
If we wish to move beyond glib truisms, therefore, we must look carefully at particular instances of Morris the printer at work and try to understand the ordinary, day-to-day problens with which he struggled at the Press.
In this chapter we will examine the production history of three Kelmscott books: Morris's Poemns by the Way (1891), Wilfrid Scawen Blunt's The Love-Lyrics & Songs of Proteus (1892), and The Golden Legend (1 892).
Because they are all early works of the Press (the second, third, and seventh books issued, respectively), there is an unusually large amount of documentary evidence available, and though unfortunately the Press's ledger does not cover the period when they were printed, it is possible to reconstruct financial information from other sources.
These particular books also possess the merit of representing the three major types of literary texts that Morris printed: his own books, the writings of other Englishmen (mainly poets), and medieval works.

## Even
though it was the last of these three books to be published, The Golden Legend deserves to be considered first since it was the earliest book planned for the Kelmscott Press.
The following notice appeared in the Literary Gossip' column of the Athenaeum on 13 September 189o: Students of English mediaeval literature will be gratified to learn that an exact reprint of“The Golden Legend" as originally set forth by William Caxton is in preparation, under the joint editorship of Mr.
William Morris and Mr.
F.
S.
Ellis.
If it is found practicable to obtain the use of a copy of the first edition it is proposed to reproduce that text with scrupulous accuracy, supplementing it with a glossary and index.
In place of the black letter, to the use of which there are manifold objections, a fount of types newly designed by Mr.
Morris after the fashion of those employed by Nicholas Jenson will be substituted. . . .
Some time must necessarily elapse . . . before the work can go to press, though it will be proceeded with immediately.
The editors have agreed to give their labours gratuitously in consideration of Mr.
Quaritch bearing all expenses of production.
As the impression will be a limited one, subscribers would do well to send their names to the publisher forthwith.'This announcement, which came from a remarkably well-informed source (probably F.
J.
Furnivall), constitutes in fact the earliest reference in print to any of the Kelmscott Press books.
During the summer of 1 89o Morris, as he was designing his Roman type, pondered the possibility of reprinting one of two Caxton books - either The Golden Legend (1483) or The Recuyell of the Historyes of Troye (1474) – which would be published by Bernard Quaritch.
With this in mind, he purchased on 21 July 189o a copy of a slightly later edition of The Golden Legend (Wynkyn de Worde, 1 527), and, when Cockerell saw Morris on 14 August, he found that ‘he thinks of reprinting [it] when his new type is all cast' (Cockerell diary).
To Ellis, who had offered to edit the book, Morris wrote on 29 August: I did want to weigh between the Golden Legend and the Troy book for reprinting: now I have borrowed a Recueil of the Histories of Troy (the Wynkyn de Worde of course) from Quaritch, and have no doubt that the G.L. is by far the most important book of the two...." (Eventually Morris printed the Troye as well.) What was the nature of The Golden Legend's appeal to Morris?
A collection of saints’ lives compiled by Jacobus de Voragine (or Varagine) and originally published under the title Legenda aurea sanctorum, sive Loinbardiea historia (Strasburg, c.1472-4), it struck some Victorian readers who did not share Morris's antiquarian enthusiasms as an improbable choice for the first big book of the Kelnscott Press.
In his Menoranda, Bibliographical & Explanatory' which appeared at the end of the third volume of the Kelmscott edition, Ellis offered this justification for reprinting it: Among the books which serve to illustrate the religious life and mode of thought that prevailed in the middle ages, none holds a more important place than the "Legenda Aurea." Compiled and put into form in the latter half of the 13th century, the welcome with which it was received by the clergy & the lettered world is attested by the great number of manuscripts of it which are still extant, while its ready reception by the people generally is evidenced by the fact that within a short time of its appearance it was translated into the vulgar tongue of most of the nations of Europe, frequently with the hagiological preferences of the people of the country for which it was designed' (iii.
1282).
Quaritch, always the sly businessman, saw this venture as a means of snaring Morris.
You are no doubt aware of the high standing, as a poet, of William Morris,' he remarked to his son on 5 June 1 89o; Ī have sold to him lately several early printed books.
I also told hin I should like to publish some future book of his.
I am in a fair way of getting such a new book from him; - and this may lead much further afterwards." In early September 189o letters were flying back and forth among Morris, Ellis, and Quaritch as they negotiated a contract for the book.
Quaritch was surprised to discover that the only modern reprint of The Golden Legend was an incomplete facsimile published by the Holbein Society in 1878 (a book that Morris owned).
Furnivall's Early English Text Society, however, had printed several other Caxton translations during the 187os and 188os, and David Nutt, a London publisher, issued a limited edition of Caxton's Le Morte Darthur between 1889 and 1891; the latter was, like the Kelmscott Golden Legend, an exact reprint, but its typography was undistinguished and two of the three volumes were devoted to learned analyses and bibliographical studies ofthe text.
Quaritch, who did not yet fully grasp Morris's new preoccupation with book-design, inquired innocently whether he perhaps intended to make The Golden Legend uniform with Nutt's book.³ My interest in the book is double,' wrote Quaritch to Ellis, first I think every man of culture should be acquainted with the alleged Lives of Saints, and second I am aware of the philological value of all books printed by Caxton.' But then he offered a typical aside: ‘Some of the naughty Saints stories will be relished by the numerous readers of Burton's Arabian Nights.'4 The contract (which was dated 11 September 189o) specified that Morris and Ellis were to contribute their Figure 63 The contract for The Golden Legend, the first book planned for the Kelmscott Press.
Morris and Ellis were to be unrewarded for their labours except for some complimentary copies of the volume, but the agreement specified that Morris was ‘to have absolute and sole control over choice of paper, choice of type, size of the reprint and selection of the printer'.

SMis William Mors services as editors gratis, to be reimbursed only with twelve copies of the book; that Quaritch was to pay for the paper, printing, and binding of soo copies; and that Morris was ‘to have absolute and sole control over choice of paper, choice of type, size of the reprint and selection of the printer'.
The odd phrasing of the last clause remind us that Morris at this stage still had only a very indistinct notion of the future Kelmscott Press and was undecided whether to ask another printer to produce the book (with Morris's types, of course); significantly, Quaritch assumed that as publisher he would furnish the paper.
By the end of 189o it was clear that Morris would print the book hinself.
In selecting the edition he would reprint, Morris displayed less hesitation — it would be Caxton's first version, not the Wynkyn de Worde edition that he owned — but this meant finding a copy that could be borrowed.
The Cambridge University Library agreed to lend its Caxton Golden Legend, heavily insured, to Ellis, whose daughter Phyllis (later Mrs Payne) then laboriously transcribed the entire book, so that the volume itself would not have to be touched by the inky hands of Morris's printers.
It was an heroic undertaking, for which she never received the public recognition she deserved, and the few surviving scraps of her nanuscript confirm W.
H.
Bowden's testimony that not one word ofit was illegible.
Later her father read the Kelmscott proofs directly against the book.
Though the contract with Quaritch referred to Morris and Ellis as co-editors, Morris was only nominally involved in editorial decisions, thus setting the pattern for many later Kelmscott books.
Ellis established the text, directing the occasional query to Morris; Morris agreed or disagreed with Ellis's proposals.
That is how it always went between them, with much joking on Ellis's side and some blustering on Morris's.
But in his concluding Memoranda’ Ellis rather exaggerated Morris's participation: ‘That I express myselfin the first person throughout this notice, and sign it, is only because I am responsible for the statements as to matters of fact which it contains, & which I could not ask Mr.
Morris to guarantee with his name, but it should be understood that he has gone carefully over the whole book, and nothing of any importance in the editing has been done without his advice and sanction’ (iii.
285-6).
The colophon is less equivocal, listing Ellis as the editor and Morris as the printer.
As usual, Ellis worried a good deal about bibliographical and textual matters while the book was in production.
He found a French version of the Legenda aurea which he believed was Caxton's source, and corresponded enthusiastically with Francis Jenkinson, University The byt of ant Ely zal eth wapyng of leeryo.

He also struggled to persuade Morris to include a glossary and a bibliography of English editions of the book; the glossary appeared in truncated form as A List of Some Obsolete or Little Used Words’, but Morris declined to use the bibliography prepared by Edward Gordon Duff.
In September 1892 Ellis wrote to Duff, The difficulty is that he has not the necessary types or the small fount which it would be desirable to use.
He said he would get it printed by Whittingham [i.e. the Chiswick Press] as a separate paper to be given with the Golden Legend but whether he will think of it again or not I cannot say – but seeing the many irons he has in the fire I am fearful of its getting forgotten & do not like to bother him further on the subject."7 The real reason for its omission, however, was, as Ellis had admitted in an earlier letter, that the ‘author-printer kicks much against introductions'.8 The colophon of the Kelmscott Press Golden Legend describes it as a reprint ‘in which there is no change from the original, except for the correction of errors of the press, & some few other amendments thought necessary for the understanding of the text'.
Ellis's textual alterations in this and other Caxton books he edited for Morris have displeased some twentieth-century specialists, but to assess his work fairly we have to see it within the tradition of energetic amateur scholarship which flourished in Victorian England, particularly among the members of the societies founded by Furnivall.
Ellis, though infinitely more scrupulous and careful in his treatment of documents than Furnivall himself was, lived at a time when the editing of historical and literary documents was not yet solely the preserve of professionals as it mainly is today.
Ellis's aim in preparing the text of The Golden Legend for the Kelmscott Press was not only to achieve verbal accuracy but also to anticipate the needs of non-specialist readers.
Although much has been written about the typography and design of Kelmscott books, historians of the Press have failed to observe that Morris and Ellis were also engaged in a pioneering attempt at intelligent popularization ofliterary works drawn, in some instances at least, from obscure or remote sources.
Measured by that standard, Ellis's achievements as an editor are impressive.
While Ellis concentrated on the text, Morris began to think about the physical shape of The Golden Legend.
Ellis was eager to reproduce the general appearance of Caxton's book, but Morris had misgivings about the wisdom of this, because he recognized that the Roman type he would be using was different in nature from Caxton's Gothic, and perhaps also because he had learned from The Story of Gunnlaug the futility of such mock-quaintness.
Of course I should like the reprint to be of the same form as the original if the Roman type can do it, which I doubt, as black letter takes up less room: in any case some kind of folio it will have to be,' he reported to Ellis in September 189o.
In one thing I think I differ from you a little, i.e., about the joined letters or queer signs: since our book is to be a reprint, not a fac-simile, I do not think we need reproduce these: indeed I should extend the abbreviations in order to make the book more readable." By December Morris's ideas about the dissimilarities between Roman and Gothic types had been taken a step further: One thing may disappoint you — to wit, that we cannot make a double-column page of it, the page will not be wide enough.
For my part, I don't regret it: double column seems to me chiefly fit for black letter, which prints up so close.
Jenson did not print even his Pliny in double column.
But it is a case of a fortiori in modern printing: because we have no contractions, few tied letters, and we cannot break a word with the same frankness as they could: I mean we can't put whi on one side and ch on the other.
This makes the spacing difficult, and a wide page desirable."º When the first delivery of paper arrived from Batchelor early in the following year, Morris concluded that it did not have sufficient sizing and reported to Ellis (11 February 1891) that, since it is only a little lot (9 reams)', he intended to print ‘a little edition of the Glittering Plain on it'.
Meanwhile, he said, he would set up a signature of The Golden Legend as an experiment until more paper was available.
But I can sympathize with my pardner's anxiety,'Morris commented; ‘and accordingly send him a [trial] page of the G[littering] P[lain], of course full of defects, but on the paper and with the types.
I don't know what you will think of it; but I think it precious good." It would be gratifying to know exactly how Morris went about designing a book, but, although he wrote at length about the broad principles of book-design and left us a legacy of fifty-two books at the Kelmscott Press, there is surprisingly little documentation of his actual procedures and techniques.
Unlike some designers of our century — Bruce Rogers or Jan Tschichold, say – he seems not to have nade elaborate, precise drawings of the pages he envisioned.
Designs of ornaments, types, and lettering for frontispieces we have in abundance from Morris's hand, but not one drawing to show the layout of a Kelmscott page.
(There is, in the Bodleian Library, a rough sketch by Morris of the title-page of the Reeves and Turner edition (1 894) of The Well at the World's End,'² but it is not very instructive and is merely patterned after the title-page of the Reeves and Turner Poems by the Way.) Probably this dearth of evidence reflects the way in which Morris worked: he always distrusted excessively detailed designs by an artist, because he felt that they inhibited the freedom of the craftsman who would execute then.
No doubt the instructions he gave his compositors at the Kelmscott Press were generally delivered orally, with Morris often at their elbow, and he never hesitated to demand that they produce one trial page after another until he was satisfied.
Trial pages of The Golden Legend, which survive in particular abundance,"3 reveal that by the time they were printed (in March 1892) Morris had settled on an approximate page size, $\mathbf { I } \mathbf { I } _ { 4 } ^ { 1 } \times 8 \frac { 1 } { 1 6 }$ inches, and the length of the line of type, 5 inches (though he continued to experiment with the latter), but these dimensions were largely determined by the paper size.
Morris was much more preoccupied with the proportions of the margins, on which he made countless small adjustments.
Once the general appearance of a page was established and the margins were deemed satisfactory, Morris's compositors began setting the text, usually providing him with galley proofs, though sometimes, as in volumes of poetry, page proofs were pulled immediately.
(This was probably unwise, since last-minute alterations frequently shifted lines on to following pages.) Spaces for initials were left blank, and in the proofs Morris specified which initials and borders were to be inserted by writing in their numbers from his ‘bloomer book'.
The Golden Legend was by far the largest book issued by the Kelmscott Press in the first two years ofits existence — more than 1,3oo pages printed on a ton and a half of paper¹⁴ — and progress was correspondingly slow.
Morris had begun designing initials for it even before the first supply of type arrived in January 1891, and he hoped to ornament the book profusely: I think I shall make some scratch of a border to each life or section,' he wrote in April.
'I want to make it grand." Such a scheme was far beyond the resources of the Press at this early stage, but Morris was able to design four full borders (the first one drawn at Folkestone in April), corner borders, and a large number of six- and ten-line decorated initials.
A book of this size strained the capabilities of Morris's small group of printers; to make matters worse, in late April a slab holding fifty pages in galleys collapsed, with the type scattering in terrible confusion on the floor.
By the following December, responding to the pressure of The Golden Legend, Morris had eight or nine men working for him in Sussex Cottage.' Figure 65 A proposed design by Burne-Jones for the second illustration in The Golden Legend, which differs from the published wood-engraving.
In 1892 Burne-Jones was still developing the distinctive style of book-illustration that came to be associated with the Kelmscott Press.
Quaritch began to express uneasiness about the slow pace of the book.
On 25 July Morris wrote to him, ist as to the extent of the book – 1 5oo p.p. about (this is pretty certain) 2nd as to time of completion. probably next June (1892) 3rd the cost: from £1200 to £1400 including doing up in boards covered with grey hand-made paper, which I think a satisfactory temporary binding.
Of course if you please you might have some copies done up in a more costly way, at an increase in cost, which would mean an increase in selling price.
I should add that I propose to give my work on designing the ornament, just as Ellis does his transcribing.'He also offered a further piece of news which must have encouraged Quaritch: Mr.
Burne Jones has promised to design me a frontispiece for the book.'8 Eventually Burne-Jones supplied two designs, which had to be inserted on separate leaves because the artist was so late in delivering them; and as a substitute for a frontispiece Morris designed the first of his lovely engraved titles.
Burne-Jones, shown proofs of the book in September 1891,‘went crazy with pleasure'.'9 The two illustrations and the engraved title were not cut by W.
H.
Hooper until the summer of 1892, when the third volume was nearing completion.
The editing of the Golden Legend has been the greatest pleasure to me,'Ellis commented that summer.
It was with a heavy sigh of regret that I returned the last proof for press."° The colophon was dated 12 September 1892; the books were delivered to Quaritch from Leighton on about 4 October; and the official date of publication was 3 November.
There were soo copies printed on paper — but none on vellum - at a price of five guineas.
On 7 October Morris sent Quaritch his receipt for the printing and binding costs (£,1,3 5o) and this valediction: ‘I must needs say that, looking at the finished book, I am proud of it, and of having pushed it through so promptly.
It is just 2 years ago that Ellis & I began talking about doing it in this very house where I am writing [Kelmscott Manor]." Towards the end of the month Morris and his friends celebrated its publication at a dinner attended by Ellis and his son, Furnivall, Duff (whose bibliography never saw the light of day), Joseph Knight, Richard Garnett, William Blaikie, William Michael Rossetti, and Walker.
Reviewers and readers responded enthusiastically to the book.
The Times (6 October 1892) described it as ‘truly magnificent’and considered it a bargain for judicious bibliophiles’ at its price.
The Library, the journal of the Bibliographical Society, suggested that The Golden Legend marked a new epoch in the production of beautiful books in this country'.

Though Morris is described here as co-editor, in the book itself Ellis calls himself the sole editor but adds that Morris has gone carefully over the whole book, and nothing of any importance in the editing has been done without his advice and sanction'. reviewer wrote, the initials were too large and the margins too small; but now these faults had been corrected, and he could only describe the result with the same word used by The Times — magnificent'.2 The most lavish praise ofall came from Swinburne, who declared in a letter to Ellis that The Golden Legend was the most superbly beautiful book that ever, I should think, came from any press'.
Now, he observed, he would be able toenjoy the study of the most famous hagiology in the world under a form which would make Euclid or Adam Smith readable & Aristotle or Hallam attractive'.²

## The
production history of Poems by the Way, the second Kelmscott Press book, is much briefer and simpler than that of The Golden Legend.
In appearance it is similar to its predecessor, The Story of the Glittering Plain: its distinguishing characteristics are a page size of about $7 _ { 8 } ^ { 7 } \times 5 _ { 8 } ^ { 5 }$ inches (the first Flower paper folded twice), the Golden type (the only typeface Morris then had), a full border on the first page of text, and a scattering of six- and ten-line initials originally designed for The Golden Legend (and thus too large).
Unlike the earlier book, however, Poermns by the Way is printed in two colours, with red ink being used for a few lines and the shoulder titles — a pattern thereafter followed by Morris.
Except for the second colour, it is an unimposing little book (224 pages), one of several works Morris pushed into print during 1891 and 1892 in order to use up the smaller paper which now seemed unsatisfactory to him.
For a time Morris had thought of entitling the volume Flores atramenti.
Morris announced his plans to Reeves and Turner in June 1892: ‘The title of my new book will be Poems by the Way; the format the same as the Glittering Plain.
It will be printed in red and black.
The poems will include some recently written and some written many years ago.
Some have appeared in magazines, but with the two exceptions of a little piece out of the Jason and one out of the Ogier [Ogier the Dane’, not included after all], they will none of them have been printed in any book of mine."4 In preparing a manuscript for his printers, Morris found it necessary to seek the assistance of Charles Fairfax Murray, who, according to May Morris, had preserved a number of unpublished poems of early date . . . and who also had kept a record of those that had appeared in periodicals from time to time'.2 In order to make the book slightly longer, Morris wrote one poem, Goldilocks and Goldilocks', especially for it on 2o May 1891.
Printing began in July but was delayed when an error was discovered in one of the sheets, which then had to be reprinted.
Blunt, after lunch at Kelmscott House on 7 August, strolled with Jane Morris over to Sussex Cottage and (as he recorded in his diary) ‘saw the printing.
Morris's own poems were being struck off, most beautiful they are with their rubrics.
The sheet I saw being printed contained the Ballad of John a Wood.'Despite some last-minute alterations in the colophon (for the purpose of improving word-spacing), printing was completed by the following month.
I expect the book will be all printed to-morrow,'Morris wrote to Jenny on 23 September, and will go to the binders on Monday.
They are printing the colophon sheet to-day, which is exciting.' The date of publication was 20 October 1891; the next day Cockerell, who had been watching the progress of the book with keen interest (though he was not yet employed by Morris), noted in his diary: 'J R[uskin]'s Poems, edited by [W.
G.] Collingwood, published as well as Morris's “Poems by the Way" beautifully printed in red and black at the Kelmscott Press.
Of course no mention of the latter in the papers. . . .' The edition consisted of 3oo paper copies (two guineas) and thirteen vellum copies (approximately twelve guineas); all were bound in stiff vellum covers, a few of which were experimentally stained red, yellow, blue, and green by Morris and Company at the Merton Abbey works.
The book was listed as out of print in the Kelmscott Press circular of 1 December 1893, but as late as September 1897 Leighton still had eight vellum copies in its warehouse.²7

## ぶ The
name of Jane Morris does not appear frequently in this or any other history of the Kelmscott Press.
She took little interest in the activities of the Press and had almost nothing to do with its daily operation.
However, in the case of Wilfrid Scawen Blunt's The Love-Lyrics & Songs of Proteus she read and criticized both manuscript and proofs, conveyed messages between her husband and the author, and in general carried the responsibilities that were later to be borne by Cockerell in overseeing the work through the Press.
The explanation for this departure from her usual behaviour is that she had been, for nearly a decade, Blunt's mistress — a fact that lends peculiar colour to the story of how the Proteus volume came to be printed at the Kelmscott Press.
The relationship between Blunt and Morris's wife was first revealed in a biography of Blunt by Lady Longford published in 1979 and was described more fully a year later by Peter Faulkner in a lecture to the William Morris Society.28 Both of these accounts are based on Blunt's voluminous diaries in the Fitzwilliam Museum, to which scholars had earlier been denied access.
Despite the massive quantity of words recorded in the diaries, Blunt strikes one as an enigmatic figure whose motives and inner life defy analysis.
He was in many respects an old-fashioned Tory squire, a Member of Parliament (briefly), a traveller, a writer of some reputation, and a fearless opponent ofimperialism at the zenith of the British Empire.
And he was devastatingly handsome and charming.
Initiated as a young man into the mysteries of sex by Catherine Walters (Skittles’), the most celebrated Victorian courtesan, Blunt thereafter romped amorously through life, leaving behind him a trail of seduced women, outraged husbands threatening divorce suits, and numerous illegitimate offspring.
These adulterous affairs, even when they produced some of his most serious and intense love poetry, often had comic overtones: when Blunt was visiting Kelmscott Manor, a jealous Morris would from time to time burst into the room where Blunt and Jane were sitting, and midnight assignations at Kelmscott were made more hazardous than usual by creaking floors.
Blunt's bizarre career as an amorist inevitably calls to mind the adventures of Byron's Don Juan, and it seems wonderfully appropriate that in 1869 he married Byron's granddaughter, who mysteriously tolerated her husband's erotic adventures until they at last separated in 1906.
Figure 68 Wilfrid Scawen Blunt as a parliamentary candidate for Camberwell in 1885.
Politician, cxplorer, poet, and womanizer, Blunt persuaded Morris to publish his poems as the third book printed by the Kelmscott Press.
It must be emphasized that Blunt was not a cold-hearted villain who had stepped out of a Victorian melodrama.
He was unusually kind to women and wrote high-minded poems about his affairs, which in time were generally transformed into solid friendships — as was to be true of his relationship with Jane Morris.
Throughout his life Blunt oscillated dizzily between manic hedonism and devout Roman Catholicism.
After his worst debauches he often went on retreats or tried to confess his sins to any eminent Catholic churchman he encountered: Manning, Newman, on one memorable occasion even Pope Leo XIII himself.
Despite frequent introspection, especially during his more religicus phases, Blunt was never able to justify or explain to himself the sexual obsessions that dominated his life.
The nearest he came to doing so in his diaries is this revealing passage, in which, significantly, he falls back on a gambling metaphor: Friendship plain and simple with a woman is to me an impossibility.
It is not that in theory I should not delight in such a friend.
I have often wished for one, but in practice and in spite of all possible good will I find it freezes me.
If I do not make love, I don't know what to do or say and she is bored and I am bored and insensibly the friendship and all else withers away.
I lose my interest in a game where there is nothing staked at all to win or lose.
It is not that I want much but there must be just something."9 Blunt met Jane Morris in 1883 and her husband a year later.
Exactly when she joined Blunt's stable of mistresses is not clear, nor do we know precisely when their sexual relationship subsided into a generous, mutually satisfying friendship.
Blunt preserved the 145 letters Jane wrote to hiın between 1884 and 1913, but for the most part they are oddly formal (the two lovers never address each other by first name) and tell us only what is self-evident: that she felt great affection for Blunt and was flattered by his attentions.3° Blunt's diaries - our other chief source of information - are slightly more helpful and contain accounts of his love-making' (a phrase that he uses in both the Victorian and modern senses) in the Morris household.
It is obvious that Blunt was attracted to Jane partly because she was one of the legendary beauties of the nineteenth century, partly because she had earlier been the mistress of his hero Rossetti, and partly because she was the wife of a man he admired very deeply.
Seducing the wife of Morris may seem a peculiar way to honour him, but Blunt's mind always moved in a mysterious way.
Faulkner has suggested that the romantic entanglement reached its peak in 1889; certainly Blunt's references to Jane in his diaries during the 1 89os are elegaic in tone, speaking of both her beauty and their love in the past tense.
When Morris died in 1896, Blunt called at Hanmersmith to pay his respects (5 October).
The coffin lay in Morris's bedroom on the ground floor; upstairs he found Jane reclining on the sofa as usual, not — he emphasized - wearing black, and he kissed her which I had not done for long and comforted her as well as I could'.
Jane's words, as recorded by Blunt, were chilling: I am not unhappy . . . though it is a terrible thing, for I have been with him since I first knew everything.
I was 18 when I married — but I never loved him.' Blunt, always resourceful, seized the occasion to persuade Jane to join him in Egypt that winter — something he had been urging her to do for years, since he found the Levant a highly satisfactory backdrop for amorous encounters.
After the drama of that scene at Kelmscott House, the expedition to Egypt, was an anticlimax, for Jane brought along May, whom Blunt found awkward and intimidating.
On 29 November Blunt made this unhappy, self-mocking entry in his diary: ‘Mrs.
Morris's visit has been rather a disappointment - her daughter May is an obstinately silent woman³ and Judith [Blunt's daughter] is bored by her and I fear they are likely to be a little bored by us.
Neither of them can ride, even donkeys, though Mrs.
Morris has made an attempt and life without riding here is impossible.
One cannot walk on account of the heavy sand, so they sit indoors all day.
I go to see them twice a day but with May it is impossible to keep up any conversation and I am now at my wit's end how to amuse them for I cannot make love to either of them and what else is there to be done.' Jane and May stayed on until early April.
When we turn to the production history of The Love-Lyrics & Songs of Proteus, we are constantly aware of this strange triangular relationship in the background.
In November 189o, before the Kelmscott Press was officially in operation, Blunt convinced Morris to print a volume of his poems, and Jane's comment on the decision (in a letter to Blunt dated 4 December 189o) captures perfectly Morris's mood of euphoria in the early days of the Press: My husband wrote to you consenting to print the poems with his new type. he does not begin till after Xmas — when he will first print his "News from Nowhere". if it succeeds I believe he contemplates bringing forth quartos and folios in large numbers.' Another letter (29 December) also reflects Morris's naïvety about the costs of the kind of printing he proposed to do at the Kelmscott Press: About the book, my husband says he thinks the cost of printing will be very little above the ordinary cost. he has not started yet, but will soon. he has taken a little place where he can set up the press.' Morris's letter of agreement to Blunt (19 November 189o) was brisk and businesslike: I have put off writing to have more news first of my type-founding adventure.
I think now that I shall be ready to set to work in about 3 months perhaps earlier.
I shall of course be very pleased to print your book, if I may have my own way about the get up — which, I take it is what you want.' There are in fact only two known instances in which Morris succumbed to outside pressure in designing the Kelmscott books: Charles Fairfax Murray claimed that he was responsible for the format of the Epistola de contemptu mundi,³² and Blunt, who had found the rubrics in Poems by the Way extremely attractive, persuaded Morris, against his better judgement, to print the initials of the Love-Lyrics in red.
Morris's restrained comment, when he saw the first sheet of Blunt's book, was that ‘it looks very gay & # SONG.YOU HAVE LET THE BEAUTY OF THE DAY GO OVER.
OU have let the beauty of the day go over, You have let the glory of the noon go by.
Clouds from the West have gathered close and cover All but a remnant now of our proud sky.
