skies; One hour remains, one only of joy blended With coming night; ah! seize it ere it flies.
61 Figure 69 At the urging of Wilfrid Scawen Blunt, Morris printed the initials of Blunt's The Love-Lyrics & Songs of Proteus (1 892) in red, but Morris was not happy with the experiment and never repcated it Song.
You have let the beauty of the day go over. pretty with its red letters, but I think I prefer mine [i.e.
Poems by the Way] in style of printing'.3 It is not surprising that Blunt held strong views on fine printing, for it was a subject that had long interested him; several of his books were handsomely produced at the Chiswick Press, and for one of them, In vinculis (1889), Jane had designed the shamrock border which appeared on its front cover.
Morris announced to Blunt on 23 February 1891 that his type had now arrived and he could begin printing Blunt's poems.
A few days later (28 February) Jane offered this report to her lover: ‘The printing [of The Story of the Glittering Plain] is now fairly started, though the work goes very slowly with our two men, soon we shall take two more and get on much faster, your book I believe will be the third produced by the “Kelmscott Press".’ Blunt's book — the partial manuscript of which he delivered to Morris in January — was to consist mainly of poems previously published in Sonnets and Songs by Proteus (1875) and The Love Sonnets of Proteus (188o), with [as the Kelmscott title-page phrased it] many sonnets omitted from the earlier editions'.
The expressive pseudonym ‘Proteus' had also been employed by Blunt in an exchange of letters about religious belief published in 1878, and in the preface to the first edition of The Love Sonnets he remarked, ‘The author of these sonnets, styling himself Proteus, acknowledges thereby a natural mood of change.
However, in February 1891 Blunt completed an additional sequence of thirty-one sonnets entitled Natalia's Resurrection' which he submitted to Morris for inclusion in the Kelmscott volume.
It is a peculiar group of poems, filled to overflowing with surrealistic allegory, exotic imagery, and supernatural events.
Young Adrian falls in love with a Roman matron (the setting is ambiguous but probably medieval), who rejects him because she feels an obligation to bear a child for her decidedly unpleasant husband.
The baby is stillborn, Natalia dies in childbirth, and Adrian falls into deep mourning.
In a dream Natalia comes to him declaring her love, for she says (and here the logic of the sequence becomes rather tenuous) that she is now free of the legal restraints of marriage; strangely, even her husband, who is also present in the dream, agrees.
As Natalia and Adrian are about to marry, he awakens to discover that her funeral procession is at that moment passing by on the road.
That evening Adrian goes to Natalia's grave and opens it, whereupon Natalia rises up, very much alive, and they dash off to get married.
Natalia's Resurrection' is not an impressive poem (though on other occasions both Yeats and Pound expressed admiration for Blunt's verse), but it requires no great psychological insight to understand why it appealed so strongly to Jane Morris.
Writing to Blunt on 13 April 1891, she commented, ‘Yesterday I finished reading the 2nd part of your M.S. poems.
I like the "Natalia's Resurrection" so very much.
I should like to see that placed first in the book.
What do you say?'A comment on the sonnets by Blunt's wife Anne in her diary offers a different perspective: ‘Poets' theories of "love" ignore honourable feelings.'4 Her husband, following Jane's advice, agreed to put Natalia's Resurrection' at the beginning of the book, and on 21 April, from a ship bound to Naples, he posted the completed manuscript to Morris.
Printing of the Love-Lyrics went much more slowly than Morris had anticipated: he was still naïvely optimistic about the amount of work his few printers could do on a hand-press, the rubrication required double presswork, and The Golden Legend was in production at the same time.
Typesetting did not begin until the summer of 1891, and proofs of Blunt's volume reached him in the country by early September, but his pleasure in seeing his words set in the beauty of the Golden type was considerably diminished by an unexpected letter from one of his former lovers.
Nearly all of Blunt's love poetry reflects his relationships with particular women – Esther, for example, offers a fictionalized account of his prolonged, unhappy passion for Skittles - and the evidence in Lady Longford's biography suggests that Blunt's ladies were usually flattered to find their amours celebrated in graceful verse, with of course no real names or incriminating circunstances mentioned.
During 189o, however, Blunt had carried on an affair with Margaret Talbot, the wife of the Military Secretary at the British Embassy in Paris, that ended abruptly when Mrs Talbot confessed everything to her husband; uncharacteristically, there were bitter recriminations on both sides.
During the summer of 1891 Mrs Talbot learned about Natalia's Resurrection' and concluded, no doubt rightly, that it contained indirect references to her.
Like Natalia, she was married to an older man of considerable social standing but was childless; she too had turned away a lover because of loyalty to her husband.
Whatever message she now sent to Blunt was sufficiently peremptory to force him into action at once.
On 28 September, Blunt, his wife, and daughter spent the day in London: Blunt went to his club while the two women shopped.
That evening, when Blunt did not return home, Anne surmised correctly that he had decamped to Kelmscott Manor, where he had an urgent conference with Morris.
Blunt made the following entry in his diary on 3o September (though part of the explanation he offers here appears to be a later interpolation): I have been away for two nights to Kelmscott to see Morris about the printing and to wish Mrs.
Morris good bye for the winter.
It was very perfect weather, and we did our gudgeon fishing and walks as usual, and I made a little love to Mrs.
Morris, poor woman, for the quite last tine. . . .
We have arranged that Natalia is to be suppressed and instead ofit that the old Proteus Sonnets shall be reprinted as the second part of the Kelmscott volume with cęrtain new sonnets and some of those omitted from the earlier edition.
The suppression of Natalia was decided on in consequence of a wild letter from Margaret who imagined that the poem described the relations between herself and her husband and me.
And, although the poem had really nothing to do with her, she was so urgent that I could not refuse.
It put Morris to great inconvenience, for Natalia was already in print, and I was obliged to explain the circumstances to him, when he at once and most good naturedly consented to the change.
In a volume of proofs of the Love-Lyrics Cockerell added one of his characteristic notes indicating that it contained a portion of the cancelled Natalia's Resurrection'— with which', Cockerell wrote, the K.P. volumes was to have opened — but a lady, so WSB told me, pretended that the poem was about herself, and, to please her, he removed it'.³ Though Cockerell is usually a reliable witness, we must recall that he always displayed a fierce loyalty to his friends and turned a blind eye to their faults: asked once whether he was aware of Blunt's infidelities, Cockerell replied, I realized that there was usually a lady in the offing.
But Blunt always behaved like a gentleman: he never told a dirty story.'6 The proofs preserved by Cockerell show that eighteen pages of type for the Love-Lyrics had been set in vain, and, despite his complacent note, we can assume that Morris and Blunt did not make this decision lightly or merely on the basis of an unfounded accusation.
How much did Jane Morris know about the ‘Natalia's Resurrection' episode?
Since she proof-read Blunt's book (something that she did for no other Kelmscott Press volume), she must have learned what had happened.
Yet she never mentions it in her letters; what does emerge in her correspondence with Blunt during 1891 is her great admiration for a group of twelve poems entitled ‘A Woman's Sonnets' in the Love-Lyrics: the “Woman's Sonnets"I liked immensely', she wrote to Blunt on 11 December, and I think them some of the best, though I don't agree with all the sentiments therein contained.'The sequence is obviously different from others in the volume, for it describes the breakup of a love-affair from a woman's point of view.
What Blunt apparently never told Jane — or the readers of the Kelmscott Press edition – was that the sonnets were in fact written by Lady Gregory, another of Blunt's former mistresses, about the change in their relationship.
Lady Gregory also read the proofs.
The Love-Lyrics & Songs of Proteus — a 264-page small quarto — was finally issued on 27 February 1892 in an edition of 300 paper copies (two guineas), and it sold out very quickly.
On 26 April, having received an inscribed copy from Blunt, Jane sent him her final evaluation of it: I think it is beautiful in all ways.
I spent much time alone with it yesterday, and read again and again with the greatest pleasure my favourite poems.
I admire more than ever the “Women's Sonnets" series, they flow so sweetly and smoothly, no hesitation anywhere. . . .
I had no copy of your book.
We bought two and gave one away, then we sent for another, but all had been sold within the fortnight of publication.
Jane's husband, meanwhile, was supplying financial reports to Blunt from time to time.
Blunt was in effect his own publisher, paying Morris for the printing and providing a fee of ten shillings to Reeves and Turner for each copy sold.
On 12 March 1892 Morris was able to offer him good news: ‘The 22o copies offered for sale have all been sold, and Reeves has handed me £2oo on account for you[.] . . .
I am charging you £1 5o for the printing which was a good deal more than I thought it would cost: but the book finally was much longer than I had estimated and the red printing, there being a great deal of it nearly doubled the cost of the press-work. . ..
I am glad that you will make something out of the book; for when Reeves pays up the whole there will be a good balance in your favour, without reckoning the copies you keep.
So I think we may congratulate ourselves on a success.’ A more detailed account, sent by Morris to Blunt on 3o April, indicates that the total cost of production was £195 and that Blunt's net profit was £142.
In the second letter Morris rejoiced that the book had been well received by reviewers: ‘The only discordant note on the subject was by [Joseph] Pennell of the Star.
However as he is a cur by profession, one should not be annoyed at his making his livelihood by the only art he understands.' But there were also mutterings of discontent fron Ellis, who took a dim view of Blunt's poetic pretensions: ‘The fact is Morris is so good natured that he does not like to say “no" but is printing this book surely against the grain.'37 The great unanswered, perhaps unanswerable, question (which lies beyond the scope of this history of the Kelmscott Press but is irresistibly fascinating nevertheless) is whether ‘good natured'Morris was aware, as he designed and printed The Love-Lyrics & Songs of Proteus, that he was making a book for his wife's lover.

## What
general conclusions, if any, can we draw from the story of these three richly diverse books?
The Golden Legend illustrates Morris's weakness for the large, sumptuous volume that he was not yet really capable of producing; impressive as The Golden Legend may be, it is not the book that Morris envisioned or the sort of book that he was later able to create at the Kelmscott Press.
Poems by the Way, a much less grandiose undertaking, shows the slow evolution of Morris's typographical style while the Kelmscott books were still in an experimental stage.
The Love-Lyrics & Songs of Proteus gives off echoes of very odd psychological resonances from the Morris marriage.
If the three books have anything at all in common, it is a certain note of improvisation (sometimes urgent, sometimes amusing) in the history of their production; if we grasp this, we will perhaps avoid thetemptation of treating the Kelmscott Press, with reverential solemnity, as merely a portentous episode in nineteenth-century cultural history. # EIGHTTHEKELMSCOTTCHAUCER ONG before it came off the press, the Kelmscott Chaucer was famous.
One of the earliest references to the book (in the Athenaeum, 22 October 1892) struck the note of admiration bordering on adoration that recurs in nearly all future allusions to it: ‘Lovers of Chaucer on both sides of the Atlantic will rejoice to hear that [Edward Burne-Jones] has made very great progress with a series of designs, fifty or sixty in all, which are to be cut in wood under his own superintendence, and intended to illustrate the “Canterbury Tales" and the other poems of Chaucer.
These designs promise to be charmingly graceful and beautiful in execution.
Indeed, we are confident that since, precisely a hundred years ago, Flaxman . .. designed for the first Countess Spencer the famous "Illustrations to the Tragedies of AEschylus," poet and artist have never been better fitted to each other.
The text to which these designs are adapted will be collated with the best manuscripts and carefully edited by Mr.
W.
Morris and Mr.
F.
S.
Ellis.
The typography will be worthy of the occasion, and the volume a stately quarto [sic].' In 1892 The Works of Geoffrey Chaucer was still in embryo, but as its birth neared there was widespread gossip about the astonishing folio in production at the Kelmscott Press.
A New York periodical, the Book Buyer (18 November 1895), published a facsimile of the first page of The Canterbury Tales and declared that the Chaucer will be without question the grandest book of the century'; in 1895 some sheets of the Chaucer were shown at the Arts and Crafts Exhibition in Dublin, prompting the Studio (January 1896) to describe the book, not yet published, as holding the place of honour among the other productions of this press'.
Morris's associates did not hesitate to bestow superlatives upon it.
'It's the finest book ever printed and it's more than likely there'll never be such another,' Burne-Jones said.
Ellis argued that ‘for typography, ornament and illustrations combined', the Chaucer was the grandest book that has issued from the press since the invention of typography'.' Many outside the Morris circle have repeated this extravagant language: a reviewer in the Library (1896), for example, described it as 'the finest book ever printed', and the Athenaeum (3 October 1896) called the Chaucer the crowning achievement of the Kelmscott Press'.
Even in the second half of the twentieth century there is still a tendency to abandon all rhetorical restraint when discussing the Kelmscott Chaucer, as evidenced by this passage from an American library exhibition catalogue of 1976: ‘This is one of the two greatest ikons of the bibliographic faith, the other being the Gutenberg Bible.
As the cross of the Christian community proclains by its very presence the gospel of Christ, so the Kelmscott Chaucer on vellum stands surrogate for all that was important in the book world before it was completed in 1 896. . . .
While there have been many litanies of praise heaped upon William Morris, and upon the Kelmscott Chaucer on vellum in particular, none of them have been adequate.
Not until one has had the opportunity to go through a copy does the full majesty of the Kelmscott accomplishment appear. . . .
The ultimate limitations of words are reached when one tries to describe the Kelmscott Chaucer on vellum." And so on for several more breathless paragraphs.
Naturally such ecstatic approbation invites a cynical response, and there have always been some readers willing to argue that the Kelmscott Chaucer is a ponderous, overdecorated non-book which threatens the eyesight and physical safety of anyone foolhardy enough to hold it on his lap (a vellum copy in the pigskin binding weighs more than thirteen pounds).
We have seen that the Chaucer is far from being a typical Kelmscott volune, but it is certainly the most celebrated and frequently photographed, and it represents, in a sense, Morris's tendencies in book-design and ornamentation carried to their logical conclusion; thus, rightly or wrongly, the Chaucer has become a litmus test which measures one's response to Morris's work as a printer.

## That
the most ambitious, richly ornamented book of the Kelmscott Press proved to be an edition of Chaucer came as no surprise to any of Morris's admirers, for he and Burne-Jones had counted Chaucer as one of their great discoveries at Oxford, and throughout his life Morris's poetry was constantly being compared by critics with that of his medieval predecessor.3 Though Morris himself protested occasionally against the injustice of the analogy, he had only himself to blame for his identification in the public mind with Chaucer: he had, after all, addressed Chaucer as ‘my Master' in both Book XVII of The Life and Death of Jason and the Envoi' to The Earthly Paradise, and the latter poem was plainly patterned after The Canterbury Tales.
Still, Morris remained slightly uneasy about the comparison, and it is noteworthy Two fyrcs on the autcr gan shc bcete, And dídc hir thyngcs, aš mcn may biholdc In Stace of Thcbcs, and thisc bookes oldc.

And haddest hire in armes at thy wille, Although thee ones on a tyme mysfille, that he declined to write an introduction to Chaucer for T.
H.
Ward's The English Poets in 1878.
Not only did he feel ill-equipped to supply such an introduction, but Morris also objected to Ward's plan to print merely a small selection from Chaucer's works.⁴ Behind the Kelmscott Chaucer lies a long lineage of Chaucer manuscripts and printed editions, many of them illustrated, with which Morris, as à collector and antiquarian, was well acquainted.s The practice of wedding picture with Chaucer's text extends back to the earliest manuscripts, the most celebrated of which is the fifteenthcentury Ellesmere manuscript of The Canterbury Tales (now in the Huntington Library) containing twenty-three portraits of the individual pilgrims, each mounted and placed in the margin near the decorated initial at the beginning of his tale.
The tales themselves are not illustrated; this convention, which may seem rather odd to us today, was observed by all illustrators of Chaucer until the late-eighteenth century, and only in the Kelmscott edition is there at last a serious attempt to represent the action of the pilgrim's stories beyond a few simple tableaux.
Burne-Jones's departure from this iconographic pattern is so complete, in fact, that he shows us none of the pilgrims except Chaucer himself (and he is not mounted), nor does he draw the pilgrims in procession or at table in the Tabard Inn, two other conventions of Chaucer's early illustrators.
The portrait of Chaucer in the Ellesmere manuscript is one of only two presumed to be reliable likenesses; the other is known as the Hoccleve portrait, also found in an early manuscript.
A third likeness of Chaucer which has captured the imagination of later generations is an early fifteenth-centry painting of Čhaucer reading his poems to the royal court - a theme that recurs in a mid-Victorian Pre-Raphaelite representation by Ford Madox Brown.
It is remarkable that, even though these images of Chaucer had been frequently engraved and were familiar to Victorian readers, Burne-Jones scarcely alludes to them in his illustrations for the Kelmscott Chaucer.
His portrait of Chaucer does show a general indebtedness to both the Hoccleve and Ellesmere likenesses; there are broad similarities in pose and costume, the outstretched hand, even the forked beard.
Yet Burne-Jones's Chaucer is more gaunt and solitary, more inward-looking (he is reading a book), and more visionary.
One notices that by the end of The Canterbury Tales he has aged perceptibly, and by the last page of the volume he looks quite decrepit.
The first printed edition of The Canterbury Tales was issued by 232 And loveth him, the which that right for love Upon a cros, our soules for to beye, first starf, and roos, and sit in hévene above; for he nil falsen no wight, dar I seye, That wol his herte al hoolly on him leye.
And sin he best to love is, and most meke, What nedeth feyned loves for to seke?
Lo here, of Payens corsed olde rytes, Lo here, what álle hir goddes may avaílle ; Lo here, these wrecched worldes appetytes; Lo here, the fyn and guerdon for travalle Of Jove, Hppollo, of Mars of swich rascaille!
Lo here, the forme of olde clerkes speche In poetrye, if ye hir bokes seche.
O moral Gower, this book I dírecte To thee, and to the philosophical Strode, To vouchen sauf, ther nede is, to corecte, Of your benignitees and zeles gode.
And to that sothfast Crist, that starf on rode, With al myn herte ofmercy ever I preye; And to the Lord right thus I speke and seye: Thou oon, and two, and three, eterne on lyve, That regn est ay in three and two and oon, Ancircumscript, and al mayst círcumscryve, Us from visible and invisible foon Defende; and to thy mercy, everichoon, So make us, Jesus, for thy grace digne, for love of mayde and moder thyn benigne!

Figure 71 The last page of the Chaucer displays Morris's printer's mark and a wood-engraving of an old, enfeebled Chaucer.
Caxton in 1478, followed by a second edition with illustrations and an improved text in 1484.
The crude woodcuts in the latter, the fourth illustrated book published in English, followed the pattern of the Ellesmere manuscript except that each mounted pilgrin appears twice: once in the General Prologue, and once at the beginning of his tale.
Caxton also added a charming picture of the Canterbury pilgrims seated at an enormous round table presided over by Harry Baily, landlord of the Tabard Inn.
Another fifteenth-century edition of the Tales (Richard Pynson, 1491 or 1492) contains a smaller and less striking representation of the same scene, and it reappears — in a very Augustan style, with Harry Baily looking like Dr Johnson in a spirited discussion at a coffee house — in John Bell's edition of Chaucer (1782).
Caxton's other Chaucerian woodcuts were also imitated and adapted by later printers for several centuries.
Figure 72 Caxton's second edition of the Canterbury Tales (1484) includes this woodcut of Chaucer's pilgrims seated around a table at the Tabard Inn.
Burne-Jones ignored all such precedents when he created his illustrations for the Kelmscott Chaucer.
John Urry's edition (1721) brings together, for the first time, all the mounted pilgrims and displays them at the moment of their departure from the Tabard.
This edition appears to have been a financial and critical failure: readers disliked Urry's textual emendations and — as a symptom of the growing nostalgia for all things medieval – they regretted the abandonment of Gothic for Roman type.
(One of the sponsors of the book, Christ Church, Oxford, was able to dispose of its remaining copies only by forcing every undergraduate to buy one when he entered the college.) In Bell's edition there is also an engraving by Thomas Stothard of a small group of pilgrims on horseback, and early in the nineteenth century both Stothard and Blake produced large-scale engravings — not intended as book illustrations — of Chaucer's pilgrimns leaving the Tabard.
What is most noticeable about all the eighteenth-century editions of Chaucer is how abruptly they depart from the medieval spirit of the earlier blackletter versions.
To read The Canterbury Tales in, for instance, Urry's Roman type, with neo-classical initials and ornaments and an extraordinarily anachronistic Squire, is a very different experience from that of studying a typical sixteenth-century page in Gothic type, illustrated with a woodcut of a pilgrim who is lineally descended from Caxton and the manuscripts before Caxton.
Bell's fourteenvolume edition of Chaucer has been praised for the excellence of its typography by Stanley Morison, and it deserves our attention also as the first attempt to illustrate the stories of The Canterbury Tales; but however admirable Bell's printing may be, one must recognize that it was precisely this eighteenth-century rendering of Chaucer — made over into the image of an Augustan gentleman ofimpeccable manners — that Morris attempted to obliterate at the Kelmscott Press.
Morris's aim was to recapture, without direct copying, the simplicity and vitality of fifteenth- and sixteenth-century editions of Chaucer that were closer in time and spirit to Chaucer's own age.
Morris believed that only by peeling off the Renaissance and neo-classical layers of cultural interpretation could he recover something like the Chaucer of the Middle Ages, and this involved both a careful restoration of Chaucer's text (including his spelling) and a return to a more medieval style of typography and ornamentation.
The Kelmscott Chaucer is in fact the splendid culmination of the Victorian rediscovery of Chaucer and of early English literature in general.
The person who was largely responsible for this revival was Morris's friend F.
J.
Furnivall, who founded, among others, the Early English Text Society (1864), the Ballad Society (1868), the New Shakspere Society (1873), and the Wyclif Society (1882).
Furnivall created the Chaucer Society in 1868 in order (according to its prospectus) to do honour to Chaucer, and to let the lovers and students of him see how far the best unprinted manuscripts of his works differ from the printed texts'.
The Chaucer Society was never a large organization — Furnivall frequently complained of its meagre membership in the annual reports, and in 19oo Ellis observed sarcastically that by then only sixteen private individuals belonged, about one in every two million and a quarter of the population of the British Isles" — and it was disbanded shortly after Furnivall's death in 191o.
Nevertheless, during the four decades ofits existence the Society laid the foundations of modern Chaucer scholarship by publishing early manuscript versions of Chaucer's works and other primary materials, mostly edited by Furnivall himself, a man of unflagging energy.
For one of these publications, a Chaucerian manuscript in the Cambridge University Library, W.
H.
Hooper, who was to engrave Burne-Jones's designs at the Kelmscott Press, made facsimile engravings of the original illustrations.7 The most important achievement of the Society, issued in instalments over several years from 1868 onwards, was Furnivall's parallel-text edition of the six Chaucer manuscripts judged to be the most trustworthy.
Like Morris, Furnivall believed passionately in the importance of digging down through the centuries of accumulated debris in order to find Chaucer's actual words rather than the glosses and conjectural emendations of later editors; like Morris, too, he took a relatively uncomplicated view of Chaucer, emphasizing what he called the most genial and humourful healthysouled man that England had ever seen'.8 Morris, of course, never remoulded Chaucer into a Muscular Christian as Furnivall did, but both men tended to ignore Chaucer the subtle Italianate poet, sometimes introspective and melancholy, seeing him instead as a cheerful realist whose value to modern readers lay chiefly in his portrayal of medieval life and manners.
During the 186os the University Presses of both Oxford and Cambridge were laying plans for scholarly editions of Chaucer, and, although Furnivall hoped that the former might print his six-text version, Oxford decided instead to publish a single text based on a collation of all the authoritative manuscripts, to be edited by the Revd Walter W.
Skeat, Professor of Anglo-Saxon at Cambridge.
Skeat was by no means an ideal editor: following the example of Furnivall (who was in many respects his mentor),º Skeat constructed a text based on intuitive, eclectic principles and drawn exclusively from Furnivall's six-text edition.
Both Ellis and Burne-Jones commented on the donnish humourlessness of Skeat's notes.
Every student of Chaucer must be grateful for the labour and learning bestowed on his works by Professor Skeat, but to be a parson without any sense of humour is not an advantage for an editor of Chaucer,’ Ellis remarked wryly.
Burne-Jones said to Sebastian Evans, Skeat is a bore, I must say.
He never edits a poem without saying it's dull and unimportant and long-winded.
How does he know and what business is that of his — if it's unimportant what does he edit it for?
He is himself always long-winded.° Yet Skeat's monumental six-volume edition of Chaucer — published under Oxford's Clarendon Press imprint in 1894, with a supplementary volume issued in 1897 - seemed a godsend to Ellis and Morris, for it was incomparably superior to all previous editions and relieved Ellis of the heavy burden of constructing his own text for the Kelmscott version.
Writing to the University Librarian in April 1892, Ellis declared, I shall also take the opportunity of calling on Mr.
Skeat if he is in Cambridge to hear what he says as to the text of the projected Kelmscott Press folio Chaucer.""Skeat shared Ellis's enthusiasm for the planned Kelmscott edition, but he could not by his own authority permit Ellis and Morris to reprint the text he was preparing for the Clarendon Press, and, not surprisingly, the Delegates of the Press displayed reluctance to allow Morris to produce an edition of Chaucer that would be potentially in competition with their own.
The Delegates first considered the request in November 1892, and their initial response was negative: ‘With regard to the Kelmscott Chaucer,'C.
E.
Doble wrote to Skeat, they do not think that you would gain anything by permitting Mr.
Morris to reprint your text, and they are not disposed to recommend you to sanction the use which it is proposed to make of it.
Would Mr.
Pollard's text be good enough for Mr.
Morris' purpose?
It is difficult to believe that the adoption of your text by Mr.
Morris would increase the sale of your books by a single copy.
Skeat suggested to Morris, as an alternative, that he might reprint Caxton's first edition of Chaucer, and meanwhile Ellis continued to study various Chaucerian texts; for The Canterbury Tales he intended to adopt the version found in the Ellesmere manuscript, but the other poems presented more complex editorial problems, and neither he nor Morris was willing to abandon their hope of using Skeat's text.
At the meeting of the Delegates of the Press on 26 May 1894, therefore, the request was renewed, again without success.
The minutes record the following action: Letter read from the Rev.
Profr.
W.
W.
Skeat proposing that the Delegates should grant permission to Mr.
Wm.
Morris to reprint the Oxford Text of‘Chaucer" amongst his Kelmscott Editions on condition of a contribution being made by him to the proposed English Lectureship Funds at Cambridge.
Resolved that the Delegates think that it would be injurious to the Clarendon Press edition to accede to Mr.
Morris's proposal.
Morris refused to admit defeat.
Aided by the intervention of Dr Arthur Evans (Keeper of the Ashmolean Museum, Oxford), he submitted the request yet again, but this time with a fuller statement of his intentions: ‘May I ask you to be so kind as to lay before the Delegates the request to allow me to make use of Professor Skeat's Chaucer under the following circumstances,' he wrote to the Secretary of the Clarendon Press on 9 August 1894.
I am publishing a Chaucer, which is being printed at the Kelmscott Press, & will be ornamented with picture[s] designed by Sir Edward Burne-Jones, and borders &c by myself.
This book I hope to make a specially beautiful one as to typography & decoration, and I naturally wish to make the text as good as possible.
I ask therefore to be allowed to avail myself of the corrections of errors which Professor Skeat's learning & acumen are making public, though we (Mr F.
S.
Ellis, the editor, & myself) by no means intend to produce a literal reprint of his text.
We should be pleased to make full acknowledgment of our obligations to Professor Skeat in the book itself.
'I should add that it is impossible that this book could come into competition with the text now being published at the University Press, as only 325 copies will be issued, at a high price (£2o), & without notes or commentary.
It is intended to be essentially a work of art."3 Morris explained in a second letter that he was willing to wait until the October meeting of the Delegates for his proposal to be considered.
'I do not think there is any hurry', he wrote, for I have only just begun printing, and it will be some weeks before I am far on in the Canterbury Tales, over which I take it no question could arise as we have the advantage of good MS.S.
(the Ellesmere is our foundation) and the emendations are few & slight.
It would [be] chiefly in the poems where there is no 1 5th century M.S. such as the House of Fame where we should need help.'4 Finally, on 19 October, the Delegates gave Morris permission ‘to reproduce in an illustrated edition de luxe of the Chaucer to be printed at the Kelmscott Press a limited number of readings from Prof.
Skeat's Text of"Chaucer" as printed in the Library Edition, provided due acknowledgement of the sanction of the Editor and the Delegates be made'.
Such an acknowledgement eventually appeared in the colophon of the Kelmscott Chaucer: The hearty thanks of the Editor and Printer are due to the Reverend Professor Skeat for kindly allowing the use of his emendations to the Ellesmere MS. of the Canterbury Tales, and also of his emended texts of Chaucer's other writings.
The like thanks also the Editor and Printer give to the Delegates of the Oxford University Press for allowing theın to avail themselves of Professor Skeat's permission.'But Morris's claim that he and Ellis did not intend ‘to produce a literal reprint of [Skeat's] text' was disingenuous, for their marked copy of the Skeat edition (which is owned today by the Beinecke Library, Yale University) discloses that they very rarely strayed from his textual readings.
Except for The Canterbury Tales, the Kelmscott Chaucer is, for all practical purposes, a reprint of Skeat's edition, but shorn of the pedantic notes that irritated Ellis and Burne-Jones.
And I want particularly to draw your exceptionally sweet attention to the fact that there is no preface to Chaucer, and no introduction, and no essay on his position as a poet, and no notes, and no glossary,' Burne-Jones announced to one of his female correspondents when the book was published.
So that all is prepared for you to thoroughly enjoy him - unvexed by the impertinence of the intruder.'s The thwarted intruder in this case was not Ellis but a solemn clerical professor in Cambridge.

## 上 It
was not merely the search for a suitable text that kept the Chaucer in production for so long; all aspects of the book - paper, ink, ornaments, illustrations, binding — presented formidable technical problems, if only because everything was on a much larger scale than for any other Kelmscott Press volume.
Even The Golden Legend and The Earthly Paradise were minor undertakings by comparison.
An edition of Chaucer had been on Morris's mind from the earliest period of the Kelmscott Press's history. ‘He thinks of printing a Chaucer from a blackletter fount which he hopes to design,' Cockerell recorded in his diary on 11 June 1891.
In the spirit of optimism which characterized the Press's announcements in those days, Morris intended to advertise the Chaucer in the first list distributed in May 1892, but at the last moment he deleted this line from the proof: THE POEMS OF GEOFFREY CHAUCER.
Black Letter.
Large 4to." In a list issued the following Decemnber, however, the book was at last announced: Chaucer's Works.
With about 6o designs by E.
Burne-Jones.'The reason for the postponement is that Morris had reluctantly reached the conclusion, early in 1892, that the Troy type was too large for the projected Chaucer.
When the type was first delivered from Sir Charles Reed and Son in January, Morris immediately had trial passages from Chaucer set up, since he then hoped the Troy would be satisfactory for the Chaucer, but Ellis, in a letter written in March, explained Morris's dilemma: I enclose you a specimen of the new type.in which as you see Caxton's “Recuyel" is to be & Chaucer is to follow but in a size smaller type but same pattern — and in double col[umn]s.
To print Chaucer in this size w[oul]d be delightful but I fear buyers would turn resentful[?] at the size which the book would necessarily make."7 Morris's own bemused observation on the Troy type was that ‘it is so big that it is no use thinking of printing the Chaucer in double columns with it unless the book were to be as big as Eggestein's Gratian's Decretum [1471]’ – an elephantine volume weighing nearly thirty pounds.18 Morris was never happy with the reduced Chaucer type, which was in his hands by the summer of 1892: I was driven by the necessities of the Chaucer (a double-columned book) to get a smaller Gothic type of Pica size,' he explained apologetically in his Note on the Kelmscott Press.9 Blunt, visiting Kelmscott Manor on 5 July 1 892, found himself in the midst of‘a great discussion as to whether it was to be printed in single or double columns'.
Double column it was, as Morris had foreseen from the beginning, and during July trial settings were struck off in the Chaucer type, at least one of which was completely unleaded.2° A new trial page established the basic design of the book: a folio (on the Perch paper) with two columns of sixty-three lines each.
A note by Morris on one of the few surviving proofs of the Chaucer indicates that the book was to some extent modelled after a specimen page, already set up, of the Froissart volume which was never completed at the Kelmscott Presst.21 With a trial page in hand, Morris was able to work out a remarkably accurate estimate of the book's length (544 pages, just a few pages short of the size it proved to be); the same document, on which he listed the proposed sequence of Chaucer's works, suggests that he may have originally intended to print them in their order of composition.² But the arrangement that he eventually adopted was, as he said, purely architectural'.23 A done.
2After 3 At got.4 Glory.
5 Thou 6 Now 7Iwgr 8 ThowgBy IN got- Ho: y Thaue to do 6 mre tuot but oue of Whosr is half Tone.
Ihave Litte. hitial wots to br dcoign5 for the Kelosunt Pro Chaucer - Figure 73 A list in Morris's hand of ornamented initials to be designed for the Chaucer.
Morris began designing the borders for the Chaucer on I February 1893, and for the first text page of Canterbury Tales - which is overwhelming in its richness ofeffect — Hooper engraved the border, the frame for the illustrations, the large initial word (Whan'), and the hand-lettered title on a single block of wood.
During the next three years Morris's prolific brush produced one ornament after another for the book, all on the same massive scale: according to Cockerell's concise summary, he designed a woodcut title, fourteen large borders, eighteen different frames round the illustrations, and twenty-six large initial words'4 — a list that does not include the scores of decorated initials, since many of the had been used already in earlier books.
It was a staggering accomplishment, and it took its toll on Morris's health.
By the spring of 1896 Burne-Jones was terrified that Morris might die before he could complete the engraved title-page: the Chaucer title page is done at last,' Burne-Jones commented on 28 March. ‘So that's all safe now, and there's nothing but the printing’ (Rooke diary).
Printing of the first sheet of the Chaucer started on 8 August 1894: BEGAN PRINTING THE CHAUCER,’Cockerell wrote in his diary in uncharacteristic capitals.
Morris also recognized the momentousness of the occasion: we printed the Ist side of the Chaucer on vellum; successfully, I think, after some trouble,’ he reported to Jenny.2 Towards the end of 1894 Morris acquired another Albion press — this one very large and reinforced with steel bands in order to withstand the additional pressure of printing his large borders - and rented Sunderland Cottage, 21 Upper Mall, to hasten the progress of the book. ‘Morris is very busy,'Charles Fairfax Murray wrote in November. ‘He is just setting up a new press to print the Chaucer faster, which he calculated would [take] him otherwise 2 years to print."6 During the spring and summer of 1895 the book fell several months further behind schedule when yellow stains began to appear on some of the sheets already printed (see Chapter Four).
Burne-Jones was, as always, dilatory in supplying his designs for the illustrations, and the difficulties that arose in printing the pictures and ornaments were legion.
Hooper inadvertently caused a last-minute delay when he made the engraved title too small, leaving an excessively broad white gap between it and the surrounding border, and Morris — with his strength rapidly failing — had to extend the design by about a fifth of an inch.27 The colophon was dated 8 May 1896, and on 2o May T.
M.
Rooke recorded in his diary Burne-Jones's relieved response: ‘Mr Figure 74 Two of Morris's pressmen, William H.
Bowden and W.
Collins, at work on the Chaucer.
Morris didn't look well yesterday, but the printing of the Chaucer's every bit of it done and in a month it'll be all dry and tightly bound.
They daren't bind it heavily as yet or the print would come off.' Most of the copies of the Chaucer were issued in the standard Kelmscott quarter-linen binding with blue paper-covered boards, but for forty-eight of the books Morris designed a pigskin binding — patterned after a binding by Ulrich Schreier of Salzburg (1478) that he owned²8 — which was executed at the Doves Bindery.
(Cobden-Sanderson also produced a binding for two copies of the Chaucer tooled from a more delicate design of his own.) A circular distributed by the Kelmscott Press in February 1 896 announced that Morris would design four Chaucer bindings in all: (1) a full pigskin from the Doves Bindery, (2) a half pigskin from the Doves Bindery, (3) a full pigskin from J. and J.
Leighton, and (4) a half pigskin from Leighton.
Although Cockerell believed that Morris was able to carry out only the first of these schemes, there is a dummy copy of the Chaucer in Columbia University's Butler Library that seems to match the description of the third binding announced in the circular — i.e. a full pigskin executed by Leighton and evidently designed by Morris.
A note in the book indicates that it is a specimen copy of the Kelmscott Chaucer 1896, made up from odd sheets [mostly pages 39-42, which were obviously cancelled] and bound by J. & J.
Leighton, to the order of William Morris; carried out in thorough 15th century style oak boards, covered with pigskin and elaborately ornamented in blind; the tools specially cut after the original designs as found on the Apocalypse Block Book and the Richel Bible, c.
1475 . . .
'.
The title on the spine isstamped in gilt.
It is likely that this less expensive pigskin binding was never produced by Leighton either because Morris was dissatisfied with it or because the dummy was completed when he was too ill to make a decision.²9 The typography, ornaments, and binding of the Kelmscott Chaucer are indeed sumptuous - the book is ‘essentially a work of art', as Morris predicted — but a special source of its appeal for many readers has always been the eighty-seven wood-engraved illustrations by Edward Burne-Jones.
In the earliest announcement of the Chaucer (December 1892) it was said that there would beabout 6o designs', but that number crept upwards inexorably as tine went by.
In a little trial dummy of eleven leaves stitched together, on which Burne-Jones sketched out his preliminary plans for the pictures, is this note in the artist's hand: ‘There are 48 planned here but I may add to the Knights tale & the early part — there may be 6o in all if I like.'3° Burne-Jones wrote on another sheet, 72 in all and he [presumably Morris] won't have more'.³' Emery Walker claimed, in a later lecture on Morris, that the number of illustrations continued to grow as long as the volume was in production: ‘When the book was printed no one, not even Burne-Jones, knew how many pictures there were in it.
He had gone on putting in as many as he liked, knowing that the more he drew the better Morris would be pleased.
If the artist liked the story particularly, then he did more pictures.
As the volume neared its end the number of illustrations increased; when they were counted after the first two volumes were bound it was found that a copy contained 87.'² Was Walker exaggerating slightly?
He did have à weakness for colourful anecdotes, and it seems inprobable that, even at the Kelmscott Press, where improvisation ruled supreme, no one had counted the illustrations until the book was bound.
Figure 75 The special pigskin binding for forty-eight copies of the Kelmscott Chaucer was designed by Morris and executed at the Doves Bindery.
Figure 76 Burne-Jones made some of his preliminary sketches for the Chaucer illustrations in a dummy book consisting of eleven lcaves stitched together, of which the first page is shown here.
In the upper right corner he wrote this note to himself: ‘There are 48 [illustrations] planned here but I may add to the Knights tale & the carly part — there may be 60 in all if I like.' By the time the book was published in 1896, there were in fact eighty-seven woodengravings.
But Walker was undoubtedly correct in his assertion that Burne-Jones illustrated those stories most abundantly which appealed to his own taste.
It was Chaucer the poet of courtly love and sophisticated teller of French and Italianate tales who most interested Burne-Jones (not the sturdy medieval bard that Morris found in The Canterbury Tales), and it was accordingly the stories of the Knight, the Clerk of Oxenford, and the Franklin which attracted him; the tales of bawdy realism he passed over in silence.
I have abstained from decorating certain of the Canterbury Tales,' he wrote to Swinburne.. . .
Morris has been urgent with me that I should by no means exclude these stories from our scheme of adornment - especially he had hopes of my treatment of the Miller's Tale, but he ever had more robust and daring parts than I could assume.'33 When his studio assistant congratulated him for ignoring Chaucer's 'base stories', Burne-Jones replied, 'I'd like to pretend Chaucer didn't do them.
Besides, pictures to them would have spoiled the book.
You don't want funny pictures either.
Pictures are too good to be funny.
Literature's good enough for that.'⁴ In private Burne-Jones did have a taste for ribald comedy: his notebooks and letters are filled with lively caricatures, mainly of emaciated, terrified men threatened by grotesquely obese women.
But his public art was reserved for high seriousness, which means that the Chaucer is populated with the usual mysterious human figures — willowy and graceful, intense but expressionless - that are so familiar from his paintings.
It is certainly not true, as one recent commentator has claimed, that Burne-Jones ruined’ the Kelmscott Chaucer by this partisan approach to illustrating it,³ yet it would be fair to say that his wood-engravings, by ignoring Chaucer's humour and irony, convey a very incomplete interpretation of the poetry.
Because the older English woodcut illustrations of Chaucer held no interest for him, Burne-Jones was compelled to turn to other visual sources.
We are told that he and Morris regularly pored over illuminated manuscripts at both the Bodleian Library and the British Museum, and an exhaustive study of the manuscripts he could have seen there would probably turn up a few miniatures from which he borrowed or adapted some details in his Chaucer illustrations.
There is (to choose a more remote example) a miniature in a manuscript of Boccaccio's Teseida which is remarkably similar to the wood-engraving which appears on page 9 of the Chaucer to illustrate the Knight's Tale: it is not clear whether or how he saw this particular manuscript (which is now in the national library of Austria), but probably the miniature represents an iconographic tradition which Burne-Jones encountered in other manuscripts as well.³⁶ He was also able to draw upon his own earlier work.
In 1857 Burne-Jones painted scenes from the Prioress's tale on a wardrobe as a wedding gift to the Morrises, of which the left panel is almost identical to the illustration on page 6o of the Kelmscott Chaucer.
(Another version of this scene appears in the last work Burne-Jones completed, a water-colour exhibited at the New Gallery in 1898.) On the right panel of the wardrobe Chaucer himself is portrayed, looking very much like the Ellesmere miniature and not yet as gaunt and melancholy as he was to become in 1896.
Chaucer also reappears in a series of cartoons Burne-Jones did for Ruskin in 1864 to illustrate The Legend of Goode Wimmen; Ruskin intended that the girls at Winnington Hall should embroider a set of hangings under Georgiana Burne-Jones's supervision, but the scheme came to nought, and a water-colour of 1871 — on which the Kelmscott illustration on page 416 is based – was the only tangible result of the aborted project.
Burne-Jones insisted that he was trying to see Chaucer's poetry afresh, not merely through the eyes of earlier illustrators, and this often led to a peculiar sort of literalism in his interpretation of the text: the example often noticed by readers of the Chaucer is the extraordinary image of the House ‘made of twigges' as a large wicker basket in The Hous of Fame.
Such an approach was not always easy for the illustrator.
'I wish Chaucer would, once & for all, make up his unrivalled & precious mind whether he is talking of a picture or à statue –– I do wish it,' wrote Burne-Jones, — for in this book I am putting myself wholly aside, & trying to see things as he saw them — not once have I invaded his kingdom with one hostile thought —'7 Burne-Jones has also left a revealing, albeit disjointed and impressionistic, account of the thoughts that passed through his mind while he was designing the first of the wood-engravings for The Romaunt of the Rose: like many of his epistles to female correspondents, it is a thinly disguised love-letter.
You come today & be with me while I draw in the little garden room,' he wrote to Helen Gaskell (whose face is recognizable in some of the Kelmscott Chaucer illustrations³⁸) in July 1894.. . .
It's the Romance of the Rose I am beginning — all new I shall make it & nothing that I have done before shall be here — all shall be better[.] Figure 77 Several of Burne-Jones's designs for the Chaucer were based on earlier works of his, such as this scene from the Prioress's tale in The Canterbury Tales which he painted on a wardrobe for the Morrises in 1857.
'I shall only picture the little part that Chaucer wrote — hope Morris will only print the Chaucer part³9 up to Narcissus at the well — a fragment it is — a kind of Pilgrin's progress — only Love is the goal & not heaven — at least not Bunyan's heaven — dont want his heaven I dont. . . . now come & stand by me while I draw the first scene — it will take two days' time — dont leave me — we shall be in Chaucers bedroom — but very discreetly — There I've sketched it — put him in bed little books by him — a curtain — & a window in his bed — & I've drawn his spirit going out at a door — they do — spirits do go out & travel far — all this is true – the spirit of him shall not be drawn faintly, but stronger than the sleeping figure — & thats true — we are getting to the truth at last. but be sure not to leave me or all the dream will go.'⁴º The illustration described by Burne-Jones in this letter is typical of many in the Chaucer: the space is claustrophobically enclosed by the walls of the tiny bedroom, the surface of which is broken only by a tiny window and an arched doorway.
Both Chaucer's gown and the hangings of the bed give Burne-Jones an opportunity to display his well-known virtuosity in drawing the sinuous folds of fabrics.
Duncan Robinson, who has done an extended analysis of the Chaucer illustrations, finds a recurring pattern of a ‘flat back of the room, often recessed, with a gently curved vault and pierced further with either a window or a door'; in the outdoor scenes, he adds, there is frequently athree-layer composition, using a wall or battlements to divide the picture plane horizontally'.4" The English Illustrated Magazine (of which Emery Walker was an assistant editor) had published in September 1884 a series of five wood-engravings designed by H.
Ryland to illustrate an article by Alfred Ainger entitled The Women of Chaucer', and they are eerily similar to Burne-Jones's later drawings of the same scenes; but the most plausible explanation ofthis correspondence is that Ryland was merely imitating stylistic conventions that Burne-Jones had already established in his paintings, which had been frequently exhibited.
Figure 78 Burne-Jones's design for the wood-engraving which appears on page 24I of the Kelmscott Chaucer.
In a letter to Helen Gaskell, Burne-Jones remarked of the illustration: I've drawn his [Chaucer's] spirit going out at a door - they do - spirits do go out & travel far - all this is true - the spirit of him shall not be drawn faintly, but stronger than the sleeping figure . . .' Figure 79 The final wood-engraving based on the design above.
Burne-Jones began the Chaucer illustrations in late 1892 or very early 1893, usually working on the designs on Sunday when Morris came to his studio to talk and read aloud.
As the task drew to a close, he was able to look back on it with satisfaction: ‘And I have been calculating that . . . the time I have given to the Chaucer work in the past two years and a half is exactly to an hour the time I should have spent in visits from Saturday till Monday at "houses," if I had been amiable and sociable — for I haven't let it invade the week's work, but have designed only on Sunday with very little exception -. . . .
I have fourteen more designs to make and then my share is done, and I may permit myself the luxury of one smile.
I have always been happy over it: it has never tired me but refreshed me always.' ,42 Yet this mellow retrospective mood fails to convey the genuine anxiety both he and Morris felt at earlier stages in the book's history.
Many of the illustrations had to be redone several times.
Burne-Jones said their rule was that in illustrating a story we must always choose the essential incidents',43 but their radically differing conceptions of Chaucer's poetry must have led to some very lively discussions between him and Morris; moreover, the elaborate procedure by which Robert Catterson-Smith translated’ Burne-Jones's delicate pencil strokes into thick, bold lines suitable for engraving (see Chapter Five) was time-consuming and expensive.
There were moments when it seemed that the book would never be done.
Burne-Jones completed the last designs at the end of December 1895 and wrote one of his delightfully whimsical letters about them: 'I have just finished my Chaucer work and in May I hope the book will see the light.
I hope sincerely it will be all the age does not want — I have omitted nothing I could think of to obstruct the onward march of the world.
The designs are carved in wood . . . the lines as thick as I could get then.
I have done all I can to impede progress — you will always bear me witness that I have not faltered — and that having put my hand to the plough I invariably look back.'44 Burne-Jones received £soo for his Chaucer designs; the full cost of the book was $\begin{array} { r } { \oint _ { \scriptstyle \mathbf { \nu } } 7 , 2 { \mathbf { I } } 7 ~ { \mathrm { ~ I ~ I ~ } } s . } \end{array}$ , an enormous investment (extending over four years) that was a source of worry to Morris, who occasionally used the word ‘panic’ in his diary to describe his response to the multiple problems encountered in its production.
Because of the unexpected delays and expenses, he announced to his customers on 14 November 1894 that the number printed will be 425 instead of $3 2 \boldsymbol { \zeta } ^ { \prime }$ and that they would be allowed to cancel their original orders if they wished to do so.
There is no evidence that any customer did.
Jane Morris wrote to Blunt that ‘at first it seemed likely to be a failure financially, then an extra 1oo were printed, which has made all the difference between loss and profit'.45 This increase in the size of the edition, however, produced further disagreements with Bernard Quaritch, who was still sulking because he had not been allowed to publish the Chaucer.
The terms under which Morris sold the book to dealers were a 15 per cent discount on the paper copies, with an additional 5 per cent allowed for cash payment within a week of delivery, and a flat 15 per cent discount on the vellum copies (cash only).
Morris had discovered that Quaritch, who was given a further discount, was offering the Chaucer to the trade at a lower price than he himself was, and he was therefore reluctant to sell him any of the additional hundred copies.
After protracted, unpleasant negotiations Morris agreed to supply fifty of the copies to Quaritch on condition that he not undersell the Kelmscott Press itself.
As Morris had remarked dryly to his wife a few months earlier, ‘They are printing the Chaucer very well.
Item it is all sold except 3 vellum copies & people are quarrelling over the privilege of buying it.'4 The 425 paper copies of the Chaucer sold for £2o each, though that price was rising among the dealers before it was published; the thirteen vellum copies went for 12o guineas each.
The pigskin binding cost an additional $\mathcal { L } ^ { \mathrm { ~ I ~ } 3 }$ .
Despite the fact that the entire edition had been sold before the date of publication, the Kelmscott Press ledger reveals that the book made no profit for Morris.
As of 3 October 1896 (slightly more than three months after publication) the Chaucer revenues amounted to $\mathcal { f } _ { 7 } , 0 0 5 ~ \mathrm { ~ I ~ } 6 s$ 1od — less than production costs — though an additional $\oint \mathrm { I } 2 3 \mathrm { ~ I } 4 ^ { s }$ dribbled in later from dealers who had taken the book on credit.
Nevertheless, the Chaucer was not a financial success: it had been subsidized, as Morris pointed out on several occasions, by the smaller Kelmscott Press books which had a healthy profit margin.
TRADE NOTICE: # WILLIAM MORRIS'S KELMSCOTT PRESS: # CHAUCER, Illustrated with designs by SIR E.
BURNE-JONES and elaborate borders by WILLIAM MoRRIS, will be ready this time next year in One Volume, folio, price £20. .
The edition will consist of 325 copies, nearly all taken up.
From the above notice it may be relied upon that the "Kelmscott Chaucer" will come out within a year.
There is every reason to believe from the superb artistic illustrations of the book that it will be over-subscribed.
I have your name down for cop ·, if you require more let me know at once before it is too late.

## TRADE
TERMS: 15 per cent. discount from the subscription price of £20.
The amount to be charged to current quarterly account; or, For Cash paid within a week of date of invoice: 5 per cent. extra discount from the nett amount.
Please acknowledge this and confirm your order.
I remain, Yours truly, BERNARD QUARITCH.
Figure 8o The discounts offered by Bernard Quaritch were so generous that Morris feared some customers would be tempted to buy the Chaucer from Quaritch rather than from the Kelmscott Press itself.
Even by the leisurely standards of the Kelmscott Press, the Chaucer was a long time in the making, and the abundant references to the book in contemporary letters and diaries make it possible for us to follow its progress with some precision.
Trial pages were pulled in July 1 892, and during the same month Morris began to discuss the illustrations with Burne-Jones.
Morris designed the first border in February 1893.
On 8 August 1894 the first sheet was printed (on vellum).
Cobden-Sanderson discussed the binding with Morris in October 1894.
On 21 December 1894 another Albion press arrived and was installed in Sunderland Cottage exclusively for printing the Chaucer.
By April 1895 all the designs for The Canterbury Tales had been redrawn by Catterson-Smith, and in the following month the printing of the Tales was completed.
Between May and August, however, there was a hiatus while Morris tried to solve the mystery of the yellow stains on the sheets already printed.
Burne-Jones drew the last illustration in December 1895; at about the same time Morris began to design the pigskin binding,47 and in February 1896 he started the engraved titlepage, finishing it on 27 March.
At the end of May the printing of the Chaucer was done, and Morris celebrated by giving presents to his pressmen.
Aside from the magnitude and complexity of the book, a major source of delay (at least during the final year of its production) was Morris's failing health.
His friends wondered whether he would live to finish it.
T.
M.
Rooke has left a moving account in his diary of a visit Morris paid to Burne-Jones's home on 2 March 1896: Morris appeared at the door ‘very ghostlike, feeble and old looking', and Burne-Jones declared that the Chaucer was the finest book ever printed — if W.M. had done nothing else it would be enough'.
Morris responded, 'I must own I'n complacent about it — must try though not to be too conceited.
And there's one thing that's not to be forgotten, that you backed me up well in it, old chap.
If you'd been at all slack over it and hadn't been as much excited about it as I was, we should never have got through with it.
The first two bound copies of the Chaucer were delivered to Morris and Burne-Jones on 2 June, the latter passing his copy on to his daughter the following day as a birthday gift.
On 24 June Douglas Cockerell took a train to Folkestone, where Morris had gone on the advice of his doctor, and handed him a copy in the pigskin binding.
'Douglas came with first bound Chaucer about 1. very satisfactory,' Morris wrote in his diary.
When he gave May her copy, she afterwards recalled, the look of profound melancholy that (perhaps unconsciously) he turned on me in smiling tenderly seemed like a glimpse into a very far country.
What it quite meant I don't think I realized at the time, though it moved me deeply: all was made clear not long afterwards . . . it was the look of an intensely lonely man — never to be forgotten while memory serves.'48 The Chaucer was Morris's last great achievement: he died the following October.
Figure 81 In this characteristically light-hearted caricature by Burne-Jones, Chaucer blesses Morris and Burne-Jones (who look like awkward schoolboys) for their work on the Kelmscott Chaucer.
The Works of Geoffrey Chaucer represents the culmination of Morris's lifelong search for the Ideal Book; it is the kind of book that he and Burne-Jones dreamed of creating when they were undergraduates and that they attempted unsuccessfully to produce during the 186os and 187os: it should have come out when Morris and I were at Oxford in our first term,'Burne-Jones wrote, and we should have lost our senses with bliss.'49 Instead it came at the end of Morris's life, when he was able to pour into this single volume all his bibliophilic passion and his unmatched skill as a designer of ornaments; it is significant that even those readers who find Morris's pages too heavy or congested for their tastes feel compelled, in the end, to pay tribute to the Chaucer as one of the great books of the world. ‘To me it is the most beautiful of all printed books,'Yeats declared;5º it is a sentiment that many, sometimes grudgingly, have echoed through the years. # WILLIHM MORRIS.
DiedOctober3rd,1896. hEidle singerofan empty day": Thy story, poet, otherwisé we read, for lol thy day, too early passed away, Was full of lofty thought and knightly deed.
HST on an age of change, and stress, and strife, Thy aim was still, through manhood úp from youth, To beautify the méanest things of life With that rich light that flows from Art and Truth.
ARTH'S soiledandmotley pageant to thine eyes as sad; thy dream was ofa dawn; golden dawn; Thy quest was for an"Earthly Paradíse": À brighter waits thee now where thou art gonel Figure 82 A poem composed and printed by Morris's printers shortly after his death.
Morris might well have had misgivings about the typography.
F William Morris had not died in 1896, how many more books might the Kelmscott Press have published?
We can never know the answer to that question; but it is evident that Morris had numerous schemes in mind at the time of his death, the most important of which were, according to Ellis, an edition of Shakespeare's plays, the English Bible, and Froissart's Chronicles.' Morris on his deathbed made one last, feeble effort to prolong the life of the Press, as recorded in Cockerell's diary on 30 August 1896: He asked whether I should be prepared to carry on the Press after his death, with Walker, & I said that I was in favour of its ceasing - as otherwise it would fizzle out by degrees, & the books already issued would suffer by inferior ones following them.
He said he thought I was perhaps right.'Nevertheless, there was much unfinished business which Cockerell and Walker had to supervise after Morris was gone.
The renaining five volumes of The Earthly Paradise were issued, and ten more titles (as well as the Froissart trial pages) were published during the last phase of the Press's history.
Not until the spring of 1898 — more than a year and a half after Morris's funeral — was Cockerell able to bring the Kelmscott Press to a definitive close.
For the most part the books produced during this period had been planned or even begun while Morris was still alive, but it is enlightening to discover that fully a fifth of the Kelmscott volumes were produced without his direct supervision by associates and employees who had learned to emulate his principles of typography.
Morris showed signs of deteriorating health in 1895.
Although one medical diagnosis suggested that he merely suffered from the disease ofbeing William Morris, and working 18 hours a day',2 it was obvious to his family and friends that — as Burne-Jones put it — his enormous vitality [was] diminishing'.3 By February 1896 the symptoms were alarming, and Morris was seeing doctors regularly, though Cockerell still reassured himself that all would be well in the end. ‘W.M. very unwell & weak,' he wrote in his diary on 2o February. . . .
Surely it is influenza.' Throughout the spring of 1896 there were further signs of decline, but of Morris's intimate circle only Burne-Jones and Blunt seemed to recognize the gravity of his illness.
Blunt's diary describes a visit on 14 May to Hammersmith, where I found my poor Morris looking very ill and aged, doddling feebly in front of his house.
We went in together however and he brightened up and told me of his maladies in a cheerful and not too desponding way. . . .’' When a fortnight later the Morrises visited Blunt at his country house, he observed in his diary (29 May) that Morris's health had worsened: he was now‘very feeble & aged.
I fear from the luck of things that it is some form of consumption and that he will not recover, but his spirits are fairly good and he talks at times as brilliantly as ever. . . . his illness does not make him gloomy, only it troubles him in his work.' (Blunt's amateur diagnosis of consumption was correct.) These developments plunged Burne-Jones into misery: now I am frightened — & can think oflittle else — steadily he seems to be consuming away - without pain — but growing weaker & weaker and I can't think about anything else,' he declared in a letter to Blunt on 6 June.⁴ A few days later, on his doctor's recommendation, the Morrises left London for Folkestone in search of the beneficial effects of sea air.
In July Morris, again on professional advice, took a voyage to Norway, but when he returned the following month to Hammersmith, his health displayed no sign of improvement.
He now withdrew to his bedroom at Kelmscott House, which he never thereafter left for more than a few hours, though during the last month of his life there was a steady flow of visitors — the Burne-Joneses, Ellis, Mary De Morgan, Charles Fairfax Murray, Blunt, and others - to the room.
On 9 September 1896 Morris made a new will, naming his wife, Ellis, and Cockerell as executors.
When Quaritch called at Kelmscott House on 17 September, he discovered Morris‘worn out and feeble', emaciated, and staring out of the window at the river. ‘I fear a catastrophe is impending,'Quaritch wrote later the same day.
To raise Morris's spirits, Burne-Jones came on Sundays and sat in the bedroom drawing illustrations for the Kelmscott Press edition of Sigurd the Volsung, and Ellis, elsewhere in the house, transcribed traditional Border ballads for another Kelmscott book that Morris hoped to publish.
Morris himself, at intervals, struggled with the design for an initial word (‘Empty’) that was to appear in The Water of the Wondrous Isles (1897).
'He sat up & was going to design “Empty". . . but only just managed to black in the spaces, & just rough in the word,' Cockerell wrote in his diary on 4 September.
And two days later: W.M. rather tired, but decidedly no worse.
He had done a little more to “Empty".' But Morris never completed the design, and after his death Catterson-Smith had to put the finishing touches on it.
On 28 Septenber Blunt witnessed Morris entering the diningroom at Kelmscott House like a man risen from the grave'.
The following day Morris was in better spirits and insisted upon being taken outdoors in a bath chair and then walking about within the house; a short time later Cockerell, returning from an errand, found him upstairs with blood streaming from his mouth.
Morris died on the morning of 3 October.
The effect on his friends was devastating.
Burne-Jones fell on his knees next to the body and burst into tears; Cockerell retreated to Morris's study opposite the bedroom and wept in private; Crane paid tribute to him by placing a sonnet and a wreath next to the Kelmscott Chaucer.that went on display the following Monday at the Arts and Crafts Exhibition.
But it is entirely characteristic of Cockerell that he did not indulge his personal grief for more than a few minutes.
When his sobbing abated, as he testified many years later, I set to work on all there was to be done."Jane Morris had little experience in handling money, Ellis was in poor health himself and lived in Torquay, and therefore almost the entire responsibility for managing the Morris family's financial affairs fell on Cockerell's shoulders.
During the following months he engaged in ceaseless, almost frenzied activity.
A buyer had to be found for Kelmscott House; arrangements were made for Morris's library to be sold; Jane Morris and her daughters required constant attention and sympathetic understanding.
Above all, Cockerell had to avert the immediate collapse of the Kelmscott Press by pushing through to completion the books already in production and by devising a rational scheme for gradually closing down the Press.
To the discerning eye there were soon signs of Morris's departure from the Kelmscott Press.
The largest book then passing through the press was The Earthly Paradise, three volumes of which had appeared before Morris's death.
In the fourth volume the colophon (dated 25 November 1896) was altered so that Printed by Williaım Morris at the Kelmscott Press' became ‘Printed by the Trustees of the late William Morris at the Kelmscott Press'.8 An announcement, issued on 12 November, of The Shepheardes Calender contained this unsettling final sentence: ‘The folio editions ofFroissart & of Sigurd the Volsung, described in the Juue list, have been abandoned.'Even in the books that were completed, the absence of Morris's guiding hand was evident, particularly in the lack of new borders, initials, and engraved titlepages.
(The Athenaeum, on 27 November 1897, was sufficiently alert to notice these deficiencies in its review of The Water of the Wondrous They were not yet of every joy bereft, For long ago was past the agony Midst which they found that they indeed must die; And now well-nigh as much their pain was past As though death's veil already had been cast Over their heads; so, midst some little mirth, They watched the dark night hide the gloomy earth.
End of Vol.
IV.
Printed by the Trustees of the late William Morris at the Kelmscott Press, and finished on the 25th day of November,1896.
Figure 83 The death of Morris in 1896 while The Earthly Paradise was in production at the Kelmscott Press required a last-minute alteration in the colophon of the fourth volume (below); the colophon of the third volume is shown above it.
Isles.) All the books published after Morris's death were of necessity much more lightly ornamented.
Love is Enough, the penultimate book printed at the Kelnscott Press, is an interesting example of how Cockerell was compelled to solve book-design difficulties on his own, since no work had been done on it before October 1896; Cockerell confessed that ‘it offered many difficult typographical problems on account of the varying length of the lines'.º It must be said, in fairness to Cockerell, that these last books of the Kelmscott Press display no dramatic falling off in quality, but, with the exception of the Froissart specimen pages, they represent a reversion to the simpler style of ornamentation characteristic of the very earliest Kelmscott books when Morris had not yet designed very many borders and initials.
There were countless books that Morris had thought of printing — the Bible (W yclife's version), Bunyan's Pilgrim's Progress, FitzGerald's translation of The Rubáiyát of Omar Khayyám, Malory's Morte Darthur, Piers Plowman, to name only a few — and Cockerell recognized that there was now no hope of ever doing them at the Kelmscott Press.
Two projected folios were abandoned, however, with greater reluctance.
In 1893 Furnivall had persuaded Morris to undertake an edition of Shakespeare's plays, with either P.
A.
Daniel or Furnivall as editor and Longmans as publisher.
Morris printed at least two trial pagesº in the Golden type: the design (two columns, stage directions and speakers' names in red, a six-line initial at the beginning of each scene) is simple to the point of austerity, and it is not known whether he intended to include illustrations.
In any event, the project never went any farther than this, because work on the Chaucer absorbed Morris's attention entirely.
The other aborted folio was Froissart's Chronicles, which was in a more advanced state of production than the Shakespeare.
Morris enthusiastically announced his plans to a journalist in 1895: My reprint is a full folio and will take up two volumes.
I also intend to publish it in four parts . . . no book that I could do would give me half the pleasure I am getting from the Froissart.
I am simply revelling in it.
It's such a noble and glorious work, and every page as it leaves the press delights me more than I can say.
I am taking great pains with it, and doing all I can to realise what I have long wished.
I am printing it in my Chaucer type."" Despite Morris's assertion, no sheets of the book were actually printed, but by November 1896 some thirty-six pages were in type, and Cockerell hit upon the ingenious solution of publishing a two-leaf vellum specimen (as well as a larger specimen privately L.ady M All our servuce, In every point twice done and then done double, Were poor and single business, to contend Against those honours deep and broad wherewith Your majesty loads our house: for those of old, And the late dignities heaped up to ther, We rest your hermits.

Fair and noble hostess, We are your guest to-night.
Lady M Your servants ever Have theirs,themselves and what is theirs, in compt To make their audit at your highness' pleasure, Still to retum your own.

He's herc in double trust; First, as I am his kinsman and his subject, Strong both against the deed; then, as bis host, Who should against his murderer shut the door, Not bear the knife myself.

I have no spur To prick the sides of my intent, but oaly Vaulting ambition, which o'erleaps itself And falls on the other ...
