He was LL noble hertís, to en, corage&to shewethem ensample and mater of honour, I Syr Johann froissart begynne to 8peke after the true res port and relation of my master Johan la Bele, 8omtyme Chanon of Saynte Lambertis of Figure 85 One of the great unfinished undertakings of the Kelmscott Press was Froissart's Chronicles, which was envisioned as a lavishly ornamented folio that would rival the Chaucer in splendour.
After Morris's death, only a few specimen pages were issued. printed on paper as a gift to some friends of Morris and the Press).
The Froissart specimen reveals a stunning new departure in Morrisian ornamentation: though the two-column text is arranged in the same pattern as that of the Chaucer, the borders, incorporating coats of arms, are extraordinarily vigorous and spiky in appearance, an initial word is connected to one of the borders, and the initials extend into the margins.
(Morris also designed some similar initials, with vines and tendrils trailing below, for the unfinished Sigurd."²) Such innovations will startle us only if we have mistakenly assumed that Morris's principles of book-design and ornamentation were static and wholly incapable of development.
The Froissart specimen pages leave no doubt that Morris's comparatively early death robbed the world of a work which would have rivalled the Chaucer as one of the finest printed books of the post-medieval era.
Swinburne's astute comment was, Why did not Morris take up & finish Froissart earlier — instead of the less appropriate work (Shelley, for one instance) on which the Kelmscott Press was occasionally wasted."3 Of the final group of books published at the Press, the one that caused Cockerell the greatest anxiety was Morris's The Story of Sigurd the Volsung, which was originally intended to be a folio adorned by some twenty-five Burne-Jones illustrations.
The book had been planned as early as 1891, but Burne-Jones was not able to turn his attention to it until January 1896 after he completed the Chaucer designs. ‘To pacify and please Morris I will do what I can to adorn the Volsung Tale,' he wrote to Lady Leighton at about this time.' But to his assistant he confessed, 'I shan't like doing it so much as Chaucer.
The scenery for one thing isn't quite a painter's scenery – it's more a poet's." By May, Burne-Jones was still working fitfully on the Sigurd designs and still complaining that he found the assignment uncongenial. ‘It's quite wonderful how distasteful the task of making drawings of it is to me,' he said. . . .
Terrible the tragedy of it is, and the fine things in it are so much fitter for literature than for my art, and I've no turn for the dramatic." As Morris lay dying at Kelmscott House during September, Burne-Jones kept his promise by bringing the designs to his friend's bedroom each Sunday, thus extending to the last moment the intense intellectual and artistic relationship that had existed between them since their Oxford days.
Burne-Jones was plainly relieved when the folio Sigurd was abandoned, but on the evening of 26 January 1897 Cockerell convinced him to furnish two illustrations for a scaled-down quarto version of the book, which was eventually published in February 1898.
It was his last piece of work for the Kelmscott Press, and both he and Cockerell were conscious that it was the end of an epoch in Burne-Jones's career.
Cockerell had lunch with him at the Grange, his home in South Kensington, on 7 October 1897, and made this terse record in his diary: EB-J alone & very nice.
He was working on the picture of Gudrun for the Sigurd which bids fair to be as fine as anything he has done for the Kelmscott Press — a fitting conclusion.
Figure 86 Designs by Morris for initials in The Story of Sigurd the Volsung.
Because of Morris's death, the ornamentation of the book had to be drastically curtailed when it was published in 1898.
The style of these unfinished initials, whose tails extend down into the margin, is siımilar to that of the initials Morris created for the Froissart, another aborted project.
There were in fact two more Burne-Jones illustrations in Love is Enough, published just a few days before the Kelmscott Press officially closed in March 1898, but they had been designed earlier.
As the colophon explained, ‘The picture on the opposite page was not designed for this edition of Love is Enough, but for an edition projected about twenty-five years ago, which was never carried out.' Similarly, the frontispiece of the last Kelmscott book, A Note by William Morris on his Aims in Founding the Kelmscott Press, was one of the wood-engravings designed by Burne-Jones and cut by Morris in the 186os for the unfinished Earthly Paradise.
And so, in these final two volumes, there were touching reminders of Morris's and Burne-Jones's lifelong preoccupation with the Ideal Book, and things came full circle at last.
Cockerell, who struck some observers as a coolly efficient technician-manager, was in reality a man of deep emotion, especially in his loyalty to Morris, and he wound up the affairs of the Kelmscott Press with evocative flourishes.

## During
1897 and early 1898 Cockerell was also kept busy sorting through the abundant records and papers of the Press.
The most interesting proofs and other documents he kept, with Jane Morris's permission, for his own collection; ever methodical, he even made use of cancelled cheques by clipping Morris's signatures and pasting them in his Kelmscott books.
Proofs and trial pages were also distributed to Cockerell's friends, particularly Robert Proctor, the brilliant young incunabulist and Morris-admirer at the British Museum.You are not unique in your taste for first proofs &c — but I will see what I can find for you later on,' Cockerell had written to him in November 1896,'7 and during the following year he supplied Proctor with a large quantity of such material.
Proctor attempted to make use of it in a Morris bibliography, never published, which he was compiling shortly before his untimely death in 1903.8 Of the vast quantities of Kelmscott proofs and related documents that survive today in both public and private collections, it is safe to generalize that nearly all of them have passed through the hands of Cockerell or Proctor at one time.
The gradual closing of the Kelmscott Press was punctuated by one reminder after another of Morris's death.
On 24 March 1897, for example, Cockerell, Walker, and Ellis met together again at Kelmscott House to celebrate Morris's birthday, with Ellis reading aloud part of The Lovers of Gudrun before they went to bed.
Between then and August Cockerell found himself burdened with the melancholy task of supervising the removal of Morris's belongings from Kelmscott House, and on 18 August he sold Morris's vellum set of the Kelmscott Press books (for £6so) to Henry Gamman, an old friend from the Coal Exchange.
The same morning, by coincidence, he disposed of the last set printed on paper.
In July he approached Sir Edward Maunde Thompson, director of the British Museum, about depositing the woodblocks of the Kelmscott Press ornaments and illustrations in the Museum, a transaction that took place in December.
F.
S.
Ellis came in the aft[ernoo]n & discussed the future of the K.P. type & ornaments,'Cockerell recorded in his diary on 12 November 1897.
I declined to consent to the ornaments being used after the close of the Press.' The remaining stock of Batchelor paper was transferred to the Chiswick Press, as were the founts of type.
To Jane Morris he offered this summary in December of the state of the Press: ‘The winding up is very anxious work — . . .
There are 5 books still to come outʃ:] Sigurd, Love is Enough, Sundering Flood, German Woodcuts, & the Note on the Press.
The first three are waiting for pictures that are nearly ready & the 4th is at the binders.
I shall have to see these books launched before I can take up other matters of a heavy kind.'9 Because of his added responsibilities — Cockerell was in effect directing the Kelmscott Press after Morris's death, with advice and occasional assistance from Walker and Ellis — Jane Morris paid him f1oo beyond his usual salary in March 1898, extending to him also my heartiest thanks for having brought the Kelmscott Press to such a successful end, it's career has been a marvel since the beginning, but the completeness of the success was certainly owing to your very skilful steering towards the end'.2º The last two announcements issued by the Press had a note of finality: that of 22 November 1897 declared that the Kelmscott Press will close early in the New Year', and that of 25 February offered for Khcott Press, Upper Mall, Hammersmith, W. cofhys velemen Chied Chriotspher ar 3.raher do. witout bloomers: for illmmui atüng.

Thes wen sant % weMoris whem Hhe Kelnseso Pnp closs in 1898 Figure 87 An inventory of Kelmnscott Press records sent by Sydney Cockerell to Jane Morris in 1898. sale all the books still in stock and added that ‘three hand-presses and the remaining type of the Kelmscott Press (exclusive of the type and ornaments), will be sold by private treaty'.
On 24 December Cockerell spent the day in moving from Sunderland Cottage to 14 Upper Mall [Sussex Cottage], thus closing another chapter, the last but one, in the history of the Press'.
Those were the words he entered in his diary that Christmas Eve; earlier in the day, as the workmen waited to carry off his desk, Cockerell wrote a final letter to Jenny Morris from his office in Sunderland Cottage, regretting that the fog prevented a last nostalgic view across the Thames from his window.
Builders at that moment were already beginning to turn the cottage into a granary.21 The last few sheets were printed in Sussex Cottage during the early months of 1898.
Following two springlike days, Cockerell reported to Jenny on 7 January: Our pressman, there is only one left now, says that we need a frost for the ink rollers, which get soft & difficult to print with in damp weather.
I hope it will come, as we have the second Sigurd picture to do next week, & the map for the Sundering Flood — and perhaps the first picture for Love is Enough, which Mr Hooper is now engraving — all of these will need his best skill —" The sole surviving pressman was Stephen Mowlem, who on 19 March printed the last copies of the last signature of the last Kelmscott Press book, A Note by William Morris.
The final sheet, appropriately, was given to Walker; the penultimate sheet, signed by Mowlem and W.H.
Bowden, the only other printer still on the premises, went into Cockerell's collection.
The official publication date of the book was 24 March.
The time had come, as Cockerell remarked to a correspondent the previous month, ‘to write EXPLICIT over the door of the Kelmscott Press'.²3 On 31 March Cockerell sent a benedictory letter to J. and J.
Leighton (thanking them for all your care & attention to the binding of the Kelmscott Press during the seven years of its existence'4), said goodbye to Jane Morris, had lunch with Georgiana Burne-Jones at the Grange, and sailed the next day to Siena, leaving behind Mrs Peddie to deal with the Press correspondence until he returned in early May.
The demise of the Kelmscott Press did not go unnoticed by the book trade or the press. ‘There has been a great rush on the German woodcuts & they were all sold out last week,'Cockerell observed on 30 November 1897.. . .
A good many people have been left out in the cold, as orders keep coming in!
I suppose it is the announcement that the Press is closing that has made people so eager."s Frank Hollings, the London dealer, trumpeted to his customers in December that there is no doubt that the dissolution of the “Kelmscott Press", . . . and the strictly limited number printed of each work, will cause them to rise in value by leaps and bounds', and he added with becoming modesty, 'I have a remarkable stock of these desirable volumes’.26 The Academy, in a long article entitled ‘The End of the Kelmscott Press' (12 December 1896), offered a balanced appraisal of Morris's achievement as a printer, concluding that ‘on one thing, at any rate, we are all agreed, that for better or for worse, the folio edition ofChaucer which appeared this summer forms a great landmark in the history of printing, and were sufficiently monumental in itself, had he produced no other book, to render the names of the Kelmscott Press and of William Morris memorable for all times'.

## Yet,
when Cockerell walked out of the door of Sussex Cottage on the last day of March 1898, he did not succeed in putting the problems of the Kelmscott Press behind him.
He had agreed to sell the presses and other equipment to C.
R.
Ashbee's Essex House Press (which will figure again in the next chapter), and several of Morris's printers, including Binning, decided to join forces with Ashbee as well.
Eventually Hooper, Catterson-Smith, and Douglas Cockerell also contributed their services to the Essex House Press; Ashbee's decorated initials were freely adapted from Morris's, and in one of his books he even invoked the name of the Kelmscott Press in the colophon.
Morris's old friends and associates were outraged by what they considered to be a shameless exploitation of a minor connection.
To Walker it seemed futile to try to imitate the Kelmscott Press. ‘The main-spring is broken,' he remarked with his usual moderation to Ashbee, ‘and when the watch stops there is nothing further to be done."7 Others used less temperate language.
The Press was brought to a definite end, and no one has the right to pose as successor to William Morris,' W.
H.
Bowden declared in a letter to a newspaper.28 Cockerell breathed threats oflawsuits, but Ellis counselled restraint.
I certainly do not think Ashbee worth taking any heed of,' he wrote to Cockerell in June 1899.
It is only the inmates or candidates for Earlswood [an Asylum for Idiots in Sussex], who are likely to confuse Ashbee with Morris, & I don't think they are worth troubling about. . . .
Ashbee's initial difficulty I take to be entire ignorance of [printing] whether from the literary or artistic point of view.'9 Ellis was slightly embarrassed about the uproar, because he had earlier agreed to edit Shakespeare's poems for Âshbee (just as he had done for Morris), and he added jovially in his next letter, Perhaps you will learn in his next circular that he has bought me as a freehold.
But I still think him quite unworthy of powder and shot.'º Cockerell devoted a good deal of time and energy during the closing years of the century to matters of this sort.
In general he was much more disturbed than Ellis and Walker were by supposed threats to the reputation of Morris or the Kelmscott Press.
When a copy of the Chaucer in unbound sheets appeared in a London auction room in the summer of 1899, Cockerell became agitated because he was convinced that it must have been stolen from the pressroom; a lengthy correspondence between him and Ellis ensued, with Ellis again advocating a calm response.
(The mystery was never solved, though another set of Chaucer sheets was offered for sale by Sotheby's the following year; in both instances May Morris's friend Robert Steele and Sparling appear to have been implicated.) Walker favoured a proposal to print Morris's collected works in the Golden type at the Chiswick Press, but Cockerell vetoed it, and only five slender volumes of lectures by Morris were printed thus.
The question of the ultimate disposition of the Kelmscott types continued to vex Cockerell for several decades.
When May Morris was revising her will in 1919, Cockerell suggested that the types ‘might perhaps go to Oxford University, if Walker has nothing better to propose’.3' Seven years later he wrote to her that, with Mr Hornby's approval [St John Hornby was now one of the trustees of the Morris estate], I am transferring the Kelmscott Press type from the Chiswick Press (which has now passed into hands that we know not of) to the Cambridge University Press, which is now doing very good work.
Apart from the fact that I am in Cambridge for a while the Press here is a permanent concern, whereas the Chiswick Press was recently in difficulties — so I hope you will also think it a wise change.'² Cockerell recommended to May in 1933 that she give the types to the Cambridge University Press, but it was not until 194o that they became the property of the Press by purchase.³ Morris's founts have recently been deposited in the Cambridge University Library.
Today the flotsam and jetsam of the Kelmscott Press are scattered round the world.
The types are in Cambridge; the woodblocks are in the British Museum; the presses are in Hammersmith, New York, and California; Morris's ‘bloomer book' is in Oxford; the address book and the ledger of the Press are in New York; a smaller account book is in Japan; and huge mounds of paper — all those precious scraps of proofs and trial pages so assiduously collected by Cockerell — have floated away to several continents.
Confronted with these sad reminders of the impermanence of all human endeavours, we may perhaps find some consolation in the thought that the more durable monument to the memory of William Morris and the Kelmscott Press is, as the Academy suggested, the fifty-two wonderful books, particularly the Chaucer, printed in Hammersmith between 1891 and 1898. # TEN THELEGACYOFMORRISAND HISPRESS HERE must have been moments when Morris, deafened by applause for his brilliant and famous adventure in printing, wondered privately whether the Kelmscott Press measured up to his own standards of achievement.
I aim at the idea and the ideal, and never get beyond the “collector",’Cobden-Sanderson lamented, .and Morris might well have echoed the complaint.' In 19oo C.
R.
Ashbee, visiting the Caxton Club, a collectors' organization in Chicago, heard the members speak of Morrises' as though they were particularly lucrative shares: one man expressed his enthusiasm for the rising value of the Kelmscott books by declaring that no trust that ever was formed has paid such a rate of interest'.2 In News from Nowhere Morris envisioned a socialist Utopia in which all men and women would have the opportunity to create beautiful objects, and the fruits of their labour would be generously distributed according to need; but, as Morris acknowledged in his evocative, bitter-sweet last chapter, such a world exists now only in our dreams and visions, and for the moment the ugly reality of dingy Hammersmith' (or greedy Chicago) prevails.
The Kelmscott Press books, intended to symbolize a protest against the ethos of Victorian industrial capitalism, became themselves, in all their opulent splendour, an example of conspicuous consumption.
The irony of this improbable transmutation was not lost on Morris.
If Morris found it difficult to evaluate the success of his Press, it is not surprising that others, from 1891 onwards, have offered remarkably contradictory appraisals of the Kelmscott books.
Probably the majority of bibliophiles have always been willing to accept the proposition trenchantly expressed by Emery Walker in 193o, that Morris showed how a printed book might be on its own plane a work of art'; but the Daily Chronicle was undoubtedly also speaking for a vocal minority when it declared in 1896, ‘Truly it is awful to contemplate the terrible effect that the Morris doctrine of decorative illustration has had upon the world." What is puzzling is that evidence to support either of these views lies abundantly at hand: the immediate consequence of the Kelmscott Press was to produce a horde of imitators who lacked Morris's skill and sensitivity as a book-designer, yet the ultimate impact of the Press has been so powerful and pervasive that it is not easy even now, a century later, to assess.
The issue is further clouded by Morris's insistence that his typographical adventure was linked with a root-and-branch rejection of modern life.
If we demand to know, therefore, what Morris's experiment in bookmaking means' to us today, we are asking a difficult question for which there are no unambiguous answers.

## The
most ardent imitator of the Kelmscott Press typography in England was C.
R.
Ashbee, who had established the Guild of Handicraft in London's East End in 1888.4 Ashbee saw himself as merely putting Morris's principles into practice, though with greater emphasis on the workshop rather than on the individual craftsman, but he received little encouragement from Morris, who suspected rightly that Ashbee wished to reform, not overthrow, the capitalist system.
Laurence Hodson, a wealthy young businessman with an interest in Pre-Raphaelitism and the Arts and Crafts movement, visited the Kelmscott Press in December 1897, and early in the following year he provided funds that made it possible for Ashbee to buy the Press's equipment.
Ashbee was overjoyed, since the transaction offered him a symbolic claim to discipleship that had been denied while Morris was still alive: the great event of the week had been the purchase of the Morris Press by the Guild ofHandicraft,' he wrote at the end of March 1898. ‘Great jubilation! . . .
So next Monday there will be vans of presses and gages, and chairs and tables and paper and machinery — lock, stock and barrel moving East from Hammersmith.
We are thinking of a musical procession.
We engage Morris's printers and carry on the great tradition.' To Cockerell's annoyance, Ashbee emphasized the Kelmscott connection by adopting the same ink, paper, and vellun as Morris had used at the Kelmscott Press; illustrations were to take the form of wood-engravings, with, in some instances, illuminations supplied by Florence Kate Kingsford (who later became Mrs Cockerell).
Since Morris's types did not constitute part of his purchase, Ashbee printed the earliest books of the Essex House Press in Caslon, but he was eager to follow Morris's example by creating his own typefaces.
Ashbee's designs were known as the Endeavour type (named after the first book in which it appeared, An Endeavour towards the Teaching of John Ruskin and William Morris, published in 19o1) and the King's Prayer Book type, a larger, modified version of the Endeavour.
In the design of these founts I sought to follow upon the lines laid down by Morris,’ Ashbee wrote.
Development appeared possible in the direction of combined letters, and my bias was towards a black (in preference to a grey) page and the introduction of a rather gothic and perhaps uncial feeling into the roman character." Ashbee's types are in fact ugly and eccentric, and, if one wished to argue the case that Morris's influence on subsequent type-designs was in the main pernicious, the Endeavour and King's Prayer Book types would probably constitute Exhibit A.
The Essex House Press printed eighty-three books over a period of eleven years, and often they rise above the limitations of Ashbee's types and wood-engraved initials (the latter more art nouveau than Morrisian): the illustrations, some of them by Ashbee himself, are undeniably attractive, and even at their weakest the books now have a certain period charm, not unlike that of an awkward Victorian building which has mellowed with age.
In 19o2 the Essex House Press, along with the Guild's other craft workshops, moved to Chipping Campden in the Cotswolds, and there, for six years, Ashbee and his men strenuously searched for the simple life far from the Modern Babylon.
They sang newly revised texts of old folk-songs (one stanza of Joan's Ale' celebrated a compositor who joins the jovial crew' and declares, The printer's worth his pay | Especially if he come Kelmscott way!’), explored the countryside together, shared their profits, and attempted to revive a somewhat sentimentalized version of the medieval guild.
It was a noble experiment, but, as Morris had predicted, the guild ideal was difficult to sustain in a society dominated by a hostile economic system.
The Essex House Press found itselfincreasingly in competition with machine-press books patterned after the Kelmscott Press style, and the remoteness of Chipping Campden interfered seriously with efforts to market the Press's products.
Ashbee later insisted that all the English private presses were victims ofan unfounded optimism: What we failed to understand was (a) that our public was limited; (b) that the competitive system was so devised that the various presses which should have been co-operating for the maintenance of standards were at times forced into competition; and (c) that ultimately “the Trade", seeing there was money in it, would set up sham presses to catch that money.' 97 As early as the summer of 1904 Ashbee's printers were put on half-time, yet he was reluctant to close the Press because of its potent syıbolism suggesting that Morris's mantle had fallen upon his # ANENDEAVOURTOWARDSTHETEACH\~ ING OF JOHN RUSKIN AND WILLIAM MORRIS.B BEINGABRIEFACCOUNTOFTHEWORK, THEAIMS,ANDTHEPRINCIPLESOFTHÉ GUILD OF HANDICRAFT IN EAST LON\~ DON, WRITTEN BY.C.
R.
ASHBEE, AND DEDICATED BYHIMLESSINTHEWRIT\~ ING,THAN IN THE WORK THE WRITING SEÉKS TO SET FORTH,TO THEIR MEM\~ ORY.AN.DOM.MDCCCCI.
Figure 88 When the Kelmscott Press closed in the spring of 1898, much of its equipment (and some of its staff) was acquired by C.
R.
Ashbee, who believed that his Essex Housc Press would carry on Morris's work.
Ashbee, despite good intentions, lacked Morris's gifts as a type-designer. shoulders.
In August 1906, however, Ashbee and Binning, the stubborn socialist compositor who had worked under Morris at the Kelmscott Press, bore the sad responsibility of stock-taking before Morris's presses and other equipment were sold: old Binning and I entered them up and chalked them down and set out value on them - so much for the working price and so much for the sentiment, except when we came to sizing up the Press on which the Chaucer was printed and we raised that a full £s above its market value."8 If the Essex House Press books represent a debased rendition of the Arts and Crafts typographical style, there were other English private presses at the turn of the century which displayed the influence of Morris more positively, and three of them deserve separate consideration; the Doves Press, the Ashendene Press, and the Vale Press.
The Doves Press, founded in 19oo by Cobden-Sanderson and Emery Walker, was the most distinguished successor of the Kelmscott Press and was patterned after the Kelmscott model very closely indeed.
The Batchelor paper and vellum bindings were virtually identical; the Doves type was based on the same photographs of pages printed by Jenson and Rubeus that Morris had used in designing the Golden type;º and a Kelmscott page stripped ofits ornaments does not differ fundamentally from a Doves page.
Given the close relationship of both its proprietors to Morris, the similarities are of course hardly unexpected.
One of the pressmen at the Doves Press had even served ,an apprenticeship at the Kelmscott Press, and J.
H.
Mason records that a portrait of Morris was pinned over the fireplace in the Doves composing room.
10 But it is misleading to emphasize too strongly thé parallels between Hammersmith's two celebrated private presses.
Cobden-Sanderson did not share Morris's bias in favour of medieval typography and ornament; his own preferences (and Emery Walker's preferences too, for that matter) led him to sixteenth-century French and Italian books as exemplars, and the volumes he produced at the Doves Press were severely simple in design, unadorned by ornament or illustration.
The only form of decoration that Cobden-Sanderson would permit himself was the occasional calligraphic initial, the most-photographed example of which is Edward Johnston's beautiful 'I' on the opening page of the Doves Bible (1903-5).
Candidly, I do not think that William Morris himself is a great printer,' Cobden-Sanderson wrote to Cockerell in 19o9: that his work is remarkable and in itself constitutes an epoch, is, again, another matter.
William Morris came to important question.
The main question is the aspećt which the illustration shall be made to take in order to fit it into and amid a page of Typography.
And I submit that its aspect must be essentially formal and of the same textuire, so to speak, as the letterpress.
It should have a set frame or margin to itself, demarcating it distinctly from the text, and the shape & char. acteroftheframe,ifdecorative,shouldhaverelationto the page as well as to the illustrative content; and the illustrative content itself should be formal and kept under so as literally to illustrate, and not to dim by over brilliancy the rest of the subjećt matter left to be communicated to the imagination by the letterpress alone.

## THEBOOKBEAUTIFULASAWHOLE
FINALLY,ifthe Book Beautifulmaybebeautifulby virtue of its writing or printing or illustration, it may also be beautiful, be even more beautiful, by the union of all to the production of one composite whole, the consummate Book Beautiful.Heretheideatobecommunicated by the book comes first, as the thing of supreme importance.
Then comes in attendance upon it, striving for the love of the idea to be itself beautiful, the written or printed page, the decorated or decorative letters, the pictures, set amid the text, and finally the binding, holding the whole in its strong grip and for very love again itself becoming beautiful because in company with the idea, 8 This is the supreme Book Beautiful or Ideal Book, a dream, a symbol of the infinitely beautiful in which all things of beauty rest and into which all things of beauty do ultimately merge.
The Book Beautiful, then, should be conceived of as a whole, & the self-assertion of any Art beyond the limitsimposed by theconditions ofitscreation should be looked upon as an Aćt of Treason.
The proper duty of each Art within such limits is to co-operate with all the other arts, similarly employed, in the production of something which is distintly Not-Itself.
Thewholeness,symmetry,harmony,beautywithout stress or strain, of the Book Beautiful, would then be one in principle with the wholeness, symmetry, harmony, and beauty without stress or strain, of that WHOLE OF LÍFE WHICH IS CONSTITUT ED OFOURSELVES&THEWORLD,THAT COMPLEX AND MARVELLOUS WHOLE WHICH,AMIDTHE STRIFE OF COMPETI TIVE FORCES, SUPREMELY HOLDS ITS OWN, AND IN THE LANGUAGE OF LIFE WRITES, UPON THEILLUMINED PAGES OF THE DAYS, THE VOLUMES OF THE CENTURIES,&THROUGH THE INFINIT UDES OF TIME & SPACE MOVES RHYTH MICALLYONWARD TO THE FULL DEVE LOPMENT OFITSASTONISHINGSTORY THE TRUE ARCHETYPE OF ALL BOOKS BEAUTIFULOR SUBLIME.
Figure 89 The typography of the Doves Press was directly modelled on that of the Kelmscott Press, particularly in its emphasis on close and even word-spacing.
The sentiments of this essay on The Book Beautiful by Cobden-Sanderson, however, would doubtless have struck Morris as too pretentiously phrased. printing with a mind set on decoration, and with a mind overscored with tapestry and woven effects, all which he reproduced where they were not wanted, on the pages of his books.
And many of his effects, as I told him at the time, are “typographical impertinencies", and utterly destructive of the page as an expression of the author's thought.' Cobden-Sanderson brought his partnership with Walker to an end in 1909, and the Press itself closed in 1916; in order to prevent Walker from using the Doves fount in future books, Cobden-Sanderson melodramatically tossed the punches, matrices, and types into the Thames under cover of darkness.
The antagonism that erupted between Walker and Cobden-Sanderson is, however, only a partial explanation of the ultimate collapse of the Doves Press: a deeper reason lies in the rigidity of Cobden-Sanderson's vision of what he termed the Book Beautiful' (a phrase sometimes erroneously attributed to Morris but presumably derived from Oscar Wilde's House Beautiful').
The unhappy truth is that the Doves books are boring in their cold perfection, each one an immaculate clone of the other.
Albeit stunningly lovely, they disclose none of the vitality that one finds in even the least satisfactory Kelmscott books.
Yet this extreme simplicity of the Doves Press volumes mediated between the ornate pages of the Kelmscott Press and the more austere tastes of twentieth-century typographers.
By demonstrating that Morris's principles of book-design could be applied with equal success to a completely undecorated text, Cobden-Sanderson and Walker translated the Morrisian Ideal Book into terms acceptable to printers and publishers who found Morris's own style intolerable.
Giovanni Mardersteig, whose Officina Bodoni has been regarded by some as the most outstanding private press of the twentieth century, is only one notable example of a young man who first became excited about the possibilities of fine printing through the example of the Doves Press.² What we must always bear in mind, however, is that the Doves Press could not have existed without the theories and practice of Morris lying behind it.
C.
H.
St John Hornby's Ashendene Press, on the other hand, had already been launched before Hornby met Morris, and his first books resemble, if anything, the Revd Daniel's little private productions at Oxford.
Hornby began printing on a modest scale in 1894, but in March 1895 he made a pilgrimage to the Kelmscott Press, where he saw the Chaucer in production, talked to Morris in his study, and had tea with both of the Morrises in their dining-room.² Hornby, like Cobden-Sanderson and Walker, was sufficiently strong-minded to avoid a direct imitation of the Kelmscott books, for, as he said, the Chaucer, though ‘in many ways the greatest example of a printed book of all time', is ‘inimitable'.'3 ‘But', Hornby observed in 1935, we can follow the spirit of his work, and that spirit has without a doubt exercised an enormous influencę during the past 4o years upon printing throughout the world.
Even those who profess to make light of Morris as a printer are themselves largely influenced by what was best in the Morris tradition. . . .'14 In 19oo Hornby met Cockerell and through him Walker (Cockerell was at thiş time a partnęr in Walker's business), and they persuaded him that it would be possible to create his own type fount for the Ashendene Press.
After consulting Robert Proctor, they decided to turn to the semi-Gothic of Sweynheym and Pannartz as their model: Hornby was of course aware that Morris had once begun designing a type based on the Sweynheym-Pannartz face, and this no doubt added to its appeal.
The resulting Subiaco type was so successful that he continued to use it regularly until the Ashendene Press ceased operation in 1935.
The Press had a long, illustrious history, much of it falling outside the boundaries of this book; but what Hornby's career as a printer especially demonstrates is that it was (and is) possible to be a disciple of Morris yet to chart one's own course in book-design.
The Ashendene volumes convey the impression that Hornby still speaks Morris's language but has translated it into a new and unexpected dialect.
The Vale Press (1896-1904) of Charles Ricketts offers another instructive example of how the Morrisian influence could be diluted by other forces.
Ricketts admired Morris's books immensely, but he recognized that it was only too easy for the Kelmscott style to be debased by less talented designers.
In an essay entitled A Defence of the Revival of Printing (1899), Ricketts commented on the ornamental tendency' in Morris which led him to create books ornamentally conceived and of ornamental use.
With such pomp of circumstance', he observed, they became more and more large, necessitating ornamental type such as the Troy type; certain books like the Chaucer should be perused at a lectern.' When the designing of such a book fell into the hands of a typographer who only half-understood Morris's principles, the results were disastrous: deprive ornament of its excellence, relate it to some casual fount, throw in with this a photogravure frontispiece, badly blocked boards, various papers, &c., and the new usual ornamental book becomes unbearable; instead of a revival in the shaping of books we have a new cumbersome trade article.' The type that Ricketts designed for the Vale Press is a bold, slabserifed Roman which openly proclaims its indebtedness to the Golden type, and Ricketts's borders also owe something to Morris's, though the general effect of a Vale page is much lighter, more delicate and open.
Ricketts, like Cobden-Sanderson, found the dark coloration of the Kelmscott books unattractive and sought inspiration instead in the less cluttered pages of the early Venetian printers.
I would define the page of a fine Kelmscott book as full of wine, an Italian book as full of light,' he declared.
Yet if Hornby veers off into a different dialect of the Morrisian language of bookmaking, Ricketts seems to be speaking in a different tongue altogether: the earnestness and solidity ofthe Kelmscott style have, through some strange sea-change, become mannered and precious.
Ricketts's deepest links, despite his professed emulation of Morris, are in fact with the art-nouveau book that coexisted with the Morrisian style during the final decade of the century.
John Russell Taylor, in a lengthy study of the art-nouveau fashion in typography, has offered a useful and concise distinction between the two traditions: Arts and Crafts [i.e. the Kelmscott-inspired style] is essentially, as the label suggests, direct, unaffected, simple (or at least simpliste); it is fairly solid and robust; and it is solidly traditional, often with a sort of revivalist fervour.
Art nouveau, on the other hand, is essentially sophisticated and eclectic, accepting and assimilating influences from here, there and everywhere – Japanese, Greek, Celtic, Pre-Raphaelite. . . , Rococo, Dutch, Blake, Victorian industrial design, etc., etc.
Moreover, art nouveau is above all highly mannered."7 The origins of art nouveau are obscure and complex; suffice it to say here that Aubrey Beardsley was responsible for introducing the style in book illustrations and ornaments, and, as we saw earlier, this came about as a direct result ofhis failed attempt to illustrate a Kelmscott book.
When Morris peremptorily refused his frontispiece for Sidonfa the Sorceress, Beardsley saw Dent's edition of Le Morte Darthur (1893-4) as a wonderful opportunity to achieve revenge by parodying the Morris-Burne-Jones conventions and modifying them in a sensual, decadent fashion.
The Morte Darthur was a great commercial success, and it effectively launched Beardsley's career as an illustrator.
William Morris has sworn a terrible oath against me for daring to bring out a book in his # KING ARTHUR. # Book j.
Chapter j.
HOW UTHER PENDRAGON SENT FOR THE DUKE OF CORNWALL AND IGRAINE HIS WIFE, AND OF THEIR DEPARTING SUDDEN-LY AGAIN.
T befell in the days of Uther Pendragon, when he was king of all England, and so reigned, that there was a mighty duke in Cornwall that held war against him long time.
And the duke was called the duke of Tintagil.
And so by means King Uther sent for this duke, charging him to bring his wife with him, for she was called a fair lady, and a passing wise, and her name was called Igraine.
So when the Juke and his wife were come unto the king, by the means of great Figure 90 Aubrcy Beardsley's ornamcnts in the Dcnt edition of Le Morte Darthur (1893-4) represcnt a savage parody of thc Kelmscott Press manner. manner,'Beardsley exulted in 1893. ‘The truth is that, while his work is a mere imitation of the old stuff, mine is fresh and original.'8 Beardsley's clever travesty of the Kelmscott books was both a personal rebellion against his respectable elders and a fin-de-siècle response to Morris's medievalism, which seemed to Beardsley's generation a tiresome symptom of mid-Victorian values.
When Leonard Smithers proposed establishing a magazine with the very ninetyish name of Peacock, Beardsley wrote to him in 1897, On the art side I suggest that it should attack untiringly and unflinchingly the Burne-Jones and Morrisian medieval business, and set up a wholesome seventeenth and eighteenth-century standard of what picture making should be.'9 Beardsley's crusade was not without effect.
The Studio during the latter part of the decade sponsored periodic competitions for illustrated title-pages, and the winning pages were almost always more in the Beardsley vein than in a style of which Morris or Burne-Jones would have approved.
There was another emerging typographical fashion in the 189os, most brilliantly represented in the skilfully designed books of Whistler, which emphasized asymmetric layout, minimal ornamentation, and mechanical methods of printing: the Aesthetic book' (as one scholar has called it)2° seems to owe almost nothing to Morris's example, except perhaps a shared preoccupation with minute and careful planning of the printed page.
Some historians are persuaded that there would have been a great revival of printing in England at the end of the nineteenth century even without the Kelmscott Press; this overstates the case, since Morris went well beyond the achievements of artists like Beardsley and Whistler by reaffirming the primary importance of fine materials (paper, ink, bindings) and by asking searching questions about the structure of the book that never entered their minds.
There is no doubt, however, that other views of bookmaking were clamouring to be heard at the time, and that in many instances people accepted only selected portions of the typographical gospel according to Morris.
Bernard Shaw, for example, was strongly impressed by The House of the Wolfings and The Roots of the Mountains (Morris's two pre-Kelmscott Press experiments at the Chiswick Press), became a Caslon-addict, and for decades badgered his printers whenever they failed to produce the closely spaced, evenly textured page that Morris had advocated.²¹ Shaw never aspired to emulate the Kelmscott Press volumes, yet he had an intelligent grasp of Morris's typographical principles, which he attempted to follow while at the same time ignoring the private mannerisms of Morris's own books.
Shaw's case exemplifies the difficulties in tracing the reputation and influence of the Kelmscott Press during the final decade of the century, for Morris's ideas (which seem distinctive enough in retrospect) about book-design mingled subtly and confusingly with the notions of other practitioners of bookmaking.
Nor was there any unanimity of opinion about the Kelmscott Press, which evoked astonishingly varying responses from readers throughout the decade.
The earliest comments on the Press that appeared in newspapers and magazines were on the whole very favourable, and even the printers' trade journals printed occasional enthusiastic notices about Morris's activities in Hammersmith.
The death of Morris in 1896 and the closing of the Kelmscott Press in 1898 stimulated further public praise of his work as a printer.
Herbert P.
Horne offered a judicious and lengthy appraisal of Morris's achievement in the Saturday Review (24 October 1896), finding the key to his success in the ideal of good craftsmanship; the Library, in commenting on an exhibition of the Kelmscott Press books mounted at the British Museum in 1898, remarked that ‘the Kelmscott books may fitly take their place with those of Gutenberg and Schoeffer, of Zainer and Koberger, of Jenson and Wendelin of Speier, among the masterpieces of the printer's art'.22 A.
W.
Pollard, reviewing the same exhibition in the Guardian (8 June 1898), found encouraging evidence of the spread of Morris's ideals, for three great firms — the Chiswick Press, Messrs.
Constable, and the Clarendon Press — have already begun to use richer founts of type than have been seen in English books since black-letter went out of fashion, and with admirable effect'.
When Henry R.
Plomer wrote A Short History of English Printing for the English Bookman's Library (19oo), the terminal date of his survey was, significantly, 1898, since he regarded the ‘brilliant but short-lived career of the Kelmscott Press' as the culminating episode in a story that had begun with Caxton in 1476.
Yet this chorus of praise for the Kelmscott Press was by no means unchallenged.
The most serious attack on Morris's principles of design grew out of an interview with hin entitled "Master Printer Morris": A Visit to the Kelmscott Press' which was published in the Daily Chronicle on 22 February 1893; shortly thereafter the same newspaper printed a stinging reply called ‘The Kelmscott Press: A Master Critic on a Master Printer'.
The Master Critic' was apparently Morris's old adversary, Joseph Pennell, and the article was reprinted and discussed in at least two printers' journals.23 It certainly pulled no punches: the anonymous critic proclaimed that De Vinne in New York was printing finer books than Morris, assaulted the Troy and Chaucer types, and insisted that the Kelmscott volumes are filled with blemishes which would be ascribed to carelessness even in printers of lesser pretensions'.
Figure 91 By the end of the last century, Morris had become linked in the popular imagination with Gutenberg, as the façade of the Edward Everard Building in Bristol demonstrates.
The Kelmscott Press books did not again come under such strong attack until Morris's death, when Pennell once more made a brutal assault in the Daily Chronicle, the British Printer revived the old charge that Morris divided the lines of verse arbitrarily in his books, and the British and Colonial Printer and Stationer declared that the Kelmscott volumes were characterized by poor inking and register.24 After the hyperbole lavished upon the Kelmscott Press in the preceding years, a reaction of this sort in 1896 was not unexpected.
The Acadery T2 December 1896) remarked that ‘certain journalist writers' had used the occasion of Morris's death to air their opinions as to the superiority of the Continental and Transatlantic fashions of illustration over the style [of Morris] . . .; their opinions also as to the vast superiority of process blocks to hand engraving, machine-made papers and printing to handmade papers and hand-printing, and so on'.
Other indications of the Kelmscott Press's status were equally ambiguous.
The market value of the Kelmscott books – a good rough index of the esteem in which they were held by collectors — rose steadily until late 1903, when their prices inexplicably dipped. ‘The truth is, collectors have tired of [the Kelmscott volumes] and will have none of them,' W.
G.
Menzies wrote in the Connoisseur (May 1905).
That they may regain some of their lost prestige is possible, but that they will ever reach the high values of four or five years ago is improbable.' This prediction was wide of the mark, yet the pattern of prices is suggestive indeed.
Within the printing trade there were also mixed responses to the Kelmscott experiment.
Trade journals that had at first welcomed Morris's attempt at private printing gradually grew more hostile as his condemnations of commercial typography became more widely known through lectures and interviews.
The Caslon typefoundry was reflecting a substantial body of,opinion when it declared in 1897 that it could only condemn the fantastic types and methods adopted by Mr.
Morris'.2 John Southworth, in his book Modern Printing (19oo), reluctantly admitted that the Kelmscott Press had attracted a great deal of attention, but few printers think there is much likelihood that what they regard as its peculiar characteristics will permanently influence present styles of book printing'(p.
28o).
Yet printers continued to pay at least lip-service to Morris's high standards of craftsmanship even while they scorned his isolation from commercial realities.
The Printing Number of The Times published on 10 September 1912, for example, though it was largely devoted to the burgeoning technology of the trade, prominently displayed a huge reproduction of a page of the Kelmscott Chaucer.
An amusing example of this ambivalence can be seen on the cover of the Monotype Recorder of April 1913, which featured a facsimile of one of Morris's decorated initials; the Recorder had of course always been an enthusiastic advocate of increased mechanization, with early covers portraying a Monotype machiņe rising triumphantly into the sky above an old-fashioned compositor, often accompanied by such edifying slogans as ‘The ol order changeth, yielding place to new'.
Paradoxically, at the very time when Morris's reputation as a printer had declined, particularly in the printing trade, there were signs on every side of his influence making itself felt in more oblique ways.
Caslon and Old Style type continued to gain ground in book-work, though Shaw, Walker, and the Chiswick Press as well as Morris's example contributed to the growing revival of old-face types.
Founts based on the Golden type still proliferated in the first decade of this century.
An examination of any representative group of English books published at that time reveals a slight but perceptible darkening of the page through the use of heavier faces and less leading.
Volumes in Everyman's Library series, launched by Dent in 1905, displayed a Venetian typeface and ornaments that were intended to evoke memories of the Kelmscott Press.
The Oxford University Press began issuing a handsome series of English classical texts in 19o6, printed in the Fell types on pure linen rag paper; the Cambridge University Press a few years earlier acquired a new fount bearing a family resemblance to the Golden type which it used for printing limited eitions of English classics.
But whereas the two university presses had special resources at their disposal, most printers before the First World War possessed unsatisfactory collections of typefaces.
Harold Curwen, at the Curwen Press, was delighted when à shortage of lead for the munitions factories in 1915 enabled him to dispose profitably of vast quantities of types accumulated since the firm's founding: he cheerfully got rid of more than 2oo founts, retaining only Caslon Old Face, Monotype Old Style No.
2, and two other faces.26 Few other proprietors were willing to adopt such a drastic solution, if only because Caslon still seemed unsuitable for many varieties of commercial printing.
As Morris had seen so clearly in 1888, English book-design could not be rejuvenated until the tired nineteenth-century modern-face types were supplanted by new founts modelled on classic old-face types.
The earliest really successful effort to do this in England — and simultaneously to make the face generally available on the Monotype machine — came when Gerard Meynell and several associates commissioned an altered version of Caslon, named after their journal in which it was first used, the Imprint, with the first issue appearing in January 1913.
It is symptomatic that the Imprint, while saturated with the ideal of technological progress in printing, is also pervaded by the presence of Morris: the first article, Art and Workmanship' by W.
R.
Lethaby, quotes Morris in its second paragraph; the frontispiece of the Kelmscott Press A Dreaın of John Ball is reproduced; an editorial note declares, ‘The Kelmscott Press, the Doves Press, and the Ashendene Press have done a great deal towards bringing about a Renaissance of Printing, and though the commercial printer is prone to speak slightingly of private presses, much of the improvement in his own work, especially in the design of the types he uses, is to be derived from this source.; The Imprint survived for less than a year but is a harbinger of the resurgence of fine printing, by both private and commercial presses, that was to take place between the wars, a period that deserves to be regarded as the golden age of English printing.
The transformation of the mass-produced book was especially remarkable: while the private presses, nearly all exhibiting Morrisian impulses, continued to pour forth handsome volumes of every variety, the renewal of printing now frequently took the form of books produced under normal trade conditions yet displaying a refinement of typography and illustration hitherto associated only with small, experimental presses.
Morris's principles, after lying almost dormant for two decades, suddenly emerged during the 192os into the different world of commercial publishing, and were applied, with modifications, to large editions of machine-printed books.
What complicates this pattern of influence is that the typographical pioneers of the 192os, committed to modern printing technology, in general were reluctant to acknowledge their ties to Morris or the private-press movement; they prided themselves on being forward-thinking men of a brave new century and professed scorn for the preciousness of Kelmscott-style ventures.
Uncertainty about Morris's position in the history of printing became, therefore, even more acute after 1918, and exorcising his # The Imprint ART AND WORKMANSHIP : By Professor W.
R.
LETHABY W the temperament of the artist that the average man who lives in the street, sometimes a very mean street, is likely to think of it as remote and luxurious, not “for the likes of him." There is the danger in habitual excess of language that the plain man is likely to be frightened by it.
It may be feared that much current exposition of the place and purpose of art only widens the gap between it and common lives.
A proper function of criticism should be to foster our national arts and not to frighten timid people off with high-pitched definitions and far-fetched metaphors mixed with a flood of (as Morris said) "sham technical twaddle." It is a pity to make a mystery of what should most easily be understood.
There is nothing occult about the thought that all things may be made well or made ill.
A work of art is a well-made thing, that is all.
It may be a well-made statue or a well-made chair, or a well-made book.
Art is not a special sauce applied to ordinary cooking ; it is the cooking itself if it is good.
Most simply and generally art may be thought of as THE WELL-DOING OF WHAT NEEDS DOING.
If the thing is not worth doing it can hardly be a work of art, however well it may be done.
A thing worth doing which is ill done is hardly a thing at all.
Fortunately people are artists who know it not—bootmakers (the few left), gardeners and basketmakers, and all players of games.
We do not allow shoddy in cricket or football, but reserve it for serious things like houses and books, furniture and funerals.
If it is necessary that everything must be translated into words, our art critics might occupy quite a useful place if they would be good enough to realise that behind the picture shows of the moment is the vast and important art of the country, the arts of the builder, furniture maker, printer and the rest, which are matters of national well-being.
It is doubtful if we have it in us to form a leading school of painting at B Figure 92 The initial numbcr (January 1913) of the Imprint, a journal that signallcd the beginning of the revival of commercial typography in twentieth-ccntury Britain.
It is significant that thc first articlc is by W.
R.
Lcthaby, who was decply influenced by Morris's ideals, and contains a dircct refercnce to Morris in the second paragraph. ghost was a common activity.
The publication of Sparling's history of the Kelmscott Press in 1924 served as the occasion for much energetic debunking, as in this review in The Times Literary Supplement (22 January 1925): most students of typography will find this a profoundly depressing book.
The book itself — the way in which it is printed — the Morris designs and the Burne-Jones illustrations — are all of a piece, and all have the pathos of dead enthusiasms. . . .
It is certain that the reaction against Morris's canons of printing is not the outcome either of moral baseness or of our system of society; it is due to the discovery that in many things Morris was wrong — wrong as an antiquary, wrong as a. practitioner.'During the interwar period Holbrook Jackson, one of this century's most influential writers on printing history, kept up a series of attacks upon the Kelmscott Press in articles, lectures, and'books: reading a paper at the Morris Centenary Dinner of the Double Crown Club on 2 May 1934, he seized the occasion to describe Morris's books as pretentious, ill conceived, and unreadable.
They were, he said, ‘museum pieces, typographical monuments — beautiful and ineffectual angels beating in the void their luminous wings in vain'.27 At the same dinnér James Shand paid tribute to Morris's magnificent technical achievements, yet found that he meant nothing to the printers of today'.28 Paul Johnston's Biblio-typographical: A Survey of Contemporary Fine Printing Style (New York, 1930), began: ‘The fallacy that Willian Morris is the patriarch of modern fine printing is as common as it is inaccurate.
Naturally Morris had his defenders after the war, chief of whom was Bernard Newdigate, the accomplished printer at the Shakespeare Head Press, who wrote a regular colunn entitled Book Production Notes'for the London Mercury between 192o and 1937.
Newdigate repeatedly reminded his readers of Morris's achievement at the Kelmscott Press, as in this representative passage from a column of 1934: A few years since it came to be the fashion to disparage Morris's work as a printer, or, at best, to dann it with faint praise; and this notwithstanding that in the eight-and-thirty years which have passed since his death, no books have surpassed his in excellence and sheer beauty, and none have been printed anywhere save at the Doves and the Ashendene presses which are worthy to rank with then.'9 Newdigate was one of several typographical enthusiasts — the others were Francis Meynell, Stanley Morison, Oliver Simon, and Holbrook Jackson – who met in London during 1922 in order to establish a publishing society that would issue one book a year, set and printed by mechanical means yet produced to the highest standards.
The would-be founders immediately discovered themselves divided ideologically: There were disputes on the texts suggested and the manner of printing them and Newdigate was felt to have too strong a tendency towards the ideas and practice of William Morris,' Simon recalled.3° The society was disbanded before it was even organized.
Though no more than a minor episode, the stillborn Fleuron Society represented emblematically the struggle between Morrisian handicraft methods and the modern mechanization of printing.
This was in fact the very issue that rendered Morris's accomplishments so problematic during the second and third decades of our century.
Among the intelligentsia of the day there were urgent calls to harness the machine to aesthetic and hunane ends, emphasizing the importance of design in inustrial production, and Morris's Luddite tendencies now seemed distinctly embarrassing.
In England the most eloquent expression of this viewpoint came from Aldous Huxley, who wrote in 1928: It has become obvious that the machine is here to stay.
Whole arnies of William Morrises and Tolstoys could not now expel it. . . .
The sensible thing to do is not to revolt against the inevitable, but to use and modify it, to make it serve your purposes.
Machines exist; let us then exploit them to create beauty - a modern beauty, while we are about it.
For we live in the twentieth century; let us frankly admit it and not pretend that we live in the fifteenth. . . .
The printer who makes a fetish of hand-work and medieval craftsmanship, who refuses to tolerate the machine or to make any effort to improve the quality of its output, thereby condemns the ordinary reader to a perpetuity of ugly printing.' Huxley thus transforms Morris's profoundly moral rejection of industrialism into a conspiracy against the reader of modest means; the same point was often made during Morris's lifetime (Talbot Baines Reed, for example, said something very like this in a letter to Emery Walker written in 1888³²), yet Huxley's polemic is striking both because ofits cleverness and because ofits assumption that the benefits of mechanization and mass-production are so self-evident that they no longer require defence.
Even those whose fundamental attitudes were close to Morris's acknowledged the supremacy of the machine in the contemporary world.
Eric Gill's brilliant but eccentric Essay on Typography (1931) illustrates how Morrisian (and Ruskinian) ideas could, with the passing of time, be metamorphosed into mysterious new shapes.
Gill, a master of reductio ad absurdum, argues that, since industrialism and handicrafts represent two separate worlds of human experience, machine-made goods should be plain and simple, not imitations of craft artefacts.
Once we have decided to make the machine our master, we must learn to accept the only kind of beauty it can create.
On the Continent the Bauhaus movement had been advancing the same case, but what sets his reasoning apart from theirs is that it is coloured by intense hatred of our industrial civilization.
When one probes beneath the surface of Gill's writings, his unlikely advocacy of functionalism proves to be not very different from the position of Morris, whose medievalism Gill claimed to despise.³ Morris's understanding of machinery was certainly more complex than Huxley's caricature of him as a regressive escapist might suggest.
The confusion about his perspective was compounded by the simpleminded propaganda of some of his disciples, particularly Sparling, who offered the world a one-dimensional version of Morris's ideas on handicrafts: Sparling could not even bring himself to admit that an Albion press is a machine.34 When the restoration of Morris's reputation came about in the following decades, therefore, it signalled a growing realization that Morris had anticipated many twentiethcentury notions of functionality and faithfulness to the nature of materials.
The fullest exposition of this revisionist view of Morris is to be found in Pevsner's Pioneers of the Modern Movement (1936), which was retitled in the second edition Pioneers of Modern Design: From William Morris to Walter Gropius; but one can see the same pattern of coming to terms with Morris in the public and private pronouncements of Stanley Morison, who was largely responsible for the important programme of historic type revivals at the Monotype Corporation.
Morison, like many of his contemporaries, viewed Morris's printing with distrust and delivered a steady stream of criticism of it during the 192os and ı93os.
In a 1944 lecture delivered in Edinburgh (published in Morison's The Typographic Arts: Two Lectures, 195o), however, he adopts a more Pevsnerian view of Morris, arguing that he was not really opposed to the machine-made book.
Morison is able to make a persuasive case because Morris does indeed refer occasionally in his typographical writings to the application of good principles of design to commercially produced books.
Yet to make Morris a cryptomodernist who had the misfortune to be born in the nineteenth century, as both Pevsner and Morison imply, is a form of special pleading that brings us no closer to the core of Morris's ideas than do Sparling's purple passages attacking machinery.
Another issue on which Morris was seen to be in the wrong was the ornamentation of books; one of the most widespread manifestations of anti-Victorian sentiment in the 192os was the conviction that ornament, especially of the historicist kind, was morally and aesthetically inappropriate for a machine-made product.
The application of this doctrine to book-design led to what one French critic later described, in a different context, as astyle calviniste',3 characterized by a puritanical fear of any decoration of the text.
Holbrook Jackson offered a trenchant summary of the doctrine in 1924: ‘Decadent printing is always ornamental printing.
When typographical imagination fails it becomes decorative.' It was the Bauhaus school that adopted the idea most zealously, eschewing all ornamental patterns except for the functionalist structure of blocks of plain sanserif type.
In England the hostility to ornament rarely reached such a pitch; even Morison, who espoused a different variety of typographical austerity, admitted that the taste for ornament is an essential human need.37 So long as ornament was seen as a symptom of moral weakness, however, it was impossible for Morris's contributions to fine printing to be acknowledged fully.
Whereas Morris's contemporaries had been startled by his rejection of typographical orthodoxies, in the early decades of this century the Kelmscott books were seen as the final, overripe expression of Victorian ornamental eclecticism.
Under these circumstances, how can one claim any connection at all between Morris's theories of bookdesign and the renewal of English printing that took place after 1918?
There is indeed a direct line of descent from the Kelmscott Press to the 192os and 1939s, but, because Gill's and Morison's generation found it psychologically impossible to acknowledge the ancestry of their typographical views, the line is not always easy to trace; we can glimpse the connection, however, by looking briefly at the careers of two men who, with Emery Walker, were largely responsible for bringing the Morrisian vision of the Ideal Book into the market-place.
J.
H.
Mason, an admirer of Morris and compositor at the Doves Press, began teaching evening classes in printing at London's Central School of Arts and Crafts in 1905, and four years later, on the recommendation of Walker and Cobden-Sanderson, he was appointed the superintendent of the Printing School.38 In that position he was responsible for training many of the next generation of British printers.
The typographical style that he imparted to his students was a simple adaptation of the Kelmscott/Doves manner: closely spaced Caslon Old Face (almost the only type of which he approved) printed on handmade, dampened paper on an Albion press.
Mason was aware that such conditions could not be duplicated in most twentiethcentury printing firms, but he argued that, in order to operate complex modern machinery, a young printer first had to master the fundamental elements of his craft — a principle of which Morris would surely not have disapproved.
Leonard Jay, who enrolled as Mason's first pupil and in 1912 became his assistant at the Central School, eventually grew restless under Mason's regime of Morrisian orthodoxy and in 1925 left to become head of the School of Printing in Birmingham.
There he continued his master's tradition of exacting craftsmanship and careful design, but his students in Birmingham were also encouraged to study advertising typography and to make use of the Linotype and Monotype machines.
Jay regarded these innovations not as a lapse from the purity of the Kelmscott/Doves faith but as an attempt to prepare students for a world in which handicraft methods no longer prevailed.
In a lecture delivered in 1929, Jay declared, ‘The product of machinery can only be improved when its producers are imbued with the same spirit which produced fine work in the past, and every effort should be directed towards breathing life and beauty into the products of machinery.
This is a vital necessity in this mechanical age; for the more machinery that is introduced into the printing trade the greater the need for more alert, intelligent, and enlightened craftsmen.
Perfect machinery is available everywhere; our saving grace lies in better quality of production.'9 In the differences between Mason and Jay one sees the process by which Morris's ideas were adapted to mechanized printing and stripped of their Ruskinian implications.
Jay and his students were just as fastidious in their search for high standards of craftsmanship as Morris was, and they nearly always followed his advice on such crucial matters as wordspacing, leading, and margins; but Morris's fear of the dehunanizing effects of machinery upon workers is no longer in evidence.
Francis Meynell's highly successful Nonesuch Press is another example of how the Morrisian principle of hand-production could be gradually assimilated to more modern methods, though Meynell, unlike Mason and Jay, thought that Morris was something of a calanmity' to the development of printing.4° Meynell recognized the relationship between the Nonesuch Press and Jay's work in Birmingham, because in both instances there was an abandonment of the handicraft ideal, accompanied by an abiding belief in the importance of painstaking design.
In 193o Meynell wrote, My previous experience had shown me quite clearly that, in order to avoid monotony and to produce desirable editions at a reasonable cost, one must intelligently exploit the best mechanical equipment and the highest technical skill available.
To-day there is more fine typographical material to be had than even the largest printing house in the country could possess; and the various “mercenary" presses have developed technical skill and variety along various lines.
There was therefore no good reason, we thought, for a new private press in the old style, arrogantly selfcontained, and with but one type and obsolete “hand" machinery. . . .
In this faith we began to mobilise other people's resources; to be designers, specifiers, rather than manufacturers; architects of books rather than builders.
In this faith we proceed to compel the fully mechanical processes to serve for the first time the end of Fine Book making.'41 Though this stridently anti-Morris blast sounds like a complete rejection of all that the Kelmscott Press stood for, in reality Morris's principles of book-design had simply been detached from their original intellectual context and were now being claimed as their own by a new generation which believed that machinery could be tamed and civilized.

## 上安
Morris's impact on printing and book-design outside England was, for at least a decade or two, unparalleled, with the force of his influence being felt most dramatically in northern Europe and English-speaking countries.
In the United States imitating the Kelmscott books became a temporary craze towards the end of the century, as Professor Susan Thompson has shown in her exhaustive study American Book Design and William Morris (New York, 1977).
Roberts Brothers of Boston published a pirated facsimile of The Story of the Glittering Plain a few months after it was issued by the Kelmscott Press in 1891, and within a few years the New York periodical the Bookman (November 1896) was complaining that ‘Kelmscott-Jenson type, more or less accurately reproduced, has spread from Portland to San Francisco, and seems especially dear to the heart of advertisers of bicycles and malt extracts’.
The more responsible American printers and book-designers were alarmed by this commercial degradation of Morris's ideals.
Everyone who cared for William Morris must regret the extent to which his name and work are being used in the matter of printing,’ wrote Theodore L.
De Vinne. ‘Tasteless imitations of his types and ornaments threaten us on every side, and no one seems able to start a new press or get some type without taking Mr.
Morris's name in vain.'4² Bruce Rogers, looking back on the phenomenon in 1912, recalled that for a period of ten years the work of William Morris had an enormous influence on American printing - and not, on the whole, a beneficial one, except in its insistence upon sound craftsmanship'.43 The Kelmscott style in the United States was initially promulgated by a handful of periodicals and publishers of anglophile or Arts and Crafts tendencies.
The most ambitious of the periodicals was the Knight-Errant of Boston, which survived for only four issues during 1892-3; set in Old Style type on handmade paper and ornamented with initials and tailpieces designed by Bertram Grosvenor Goodhue, the Kuight-Errant was self-consciously Morrisian and aesthetic.
In the July 1892 issue an essay by Francis Watts Lee, ‘Some Thoughts Upon Beauty in Typography, Suggested by the Work of Mr.
William Morris at the Kelmscott Press', provided facsimiles of three Kelmscott pages and the printer's mark designed by Morris, and described his books as the crowning glory of the nineteenth century'.
Another Boston periodical was Modern Art, a quarterly published between 1893 and 1897.
Its design, though more satisfactory than that of the Knight-Errant, was equally Morrisian (Kelmscott-style borders and initials, a replica of the Golden type, shoulder titles and notes in red), and it had the distinction of publishing A Note by William Morris on His Aims in Founding the Kelmscott Press', an essay reprinted in the last Kelmscott book.
A considerably less imposing journal was the Craftsınan, produced by a crafts guild called the United Crafts of Eastwood, N.Y., the first issue of which (October 19o1) was devoted entirely to Morris, with vaguely Morrisian borders and indifferent typography.
The publishing houses that fell under Morris's sway in the 189os displayed his influence at a relatively sophisticated level, since they were responding to a variety of styles in book-design that floated across the Atlantic.
Copeland and Day of Boston, for example, occasionally imitated the Kelmscott manner, most conspicuously in their edition of Rossetti's House of Life (1894), but many of their books possessed a strongly aesthetic and decadent quality reminiscent of John Lane's characteristic style.
W.
Irving Way of Way and Williams (Chicago) made a pilgrimage to Hammersmith in the spring of 1895 and persuaded Morris to print Hand & Soul, the only Kelmscott book copublished in the United States (though Copeland and Day nearly Figure 93 The Boston mnagazine Knight-Errant gave clear evidence of being under Morris's spell in 1892.
Pages of some of the Kelmscott books were reproduced in it for the edification of American readers. # LOVE ENTHRONED MARKED all kin dred Powers the heart finds fair:— Truth, with awed lips; and Hope,with eyes upcast; And Fame,whose loud wings fan the ashen Past To signal-fires,Oblivion's flight to scare; And Youth, with still some single golden hair Unto his shoulder clinging, since the last Embrace wherein two sweet arms held him fast; And Life, still wreathing flowers for Death to wear.
Love's throne was not with these; but far above All passionate wind of welcome and fareweli He sat in breathless bowers they dream not of; Though Truth foreknow Love's heart, and Hope foretell, And Fame be for Love's sake desirable, And Youth be dear, and Life be sweet to Love.
Figure 94 The Amcrican publishing house of Copcland and Day in Boston closely imitated the Kelmscott Press style in this edition of Dante Gabriel Rossetti's The House of Life (1894).
Both the initial and border, however, are more cluttered than Morris's ever werc. convinced Morris to print an edition of Emerson's poems for them).
On the same trip to London, however, Way also secured nine books from John Lane.
A third American publisher, Thomas B, Mosher of Maine, produced beautiful pirated editions of English books rather more in the Whistlerian than Morrisian vein, but in 19oo he issued a Hand & Soul that was a thoughtful imitation of the Kelmscott version.
Unlike many of his American contemporaries, Mosher realized that the secret of Morris's success as a printer lay not in his types and ornaments (which were so easily facsimiled by photoengraving methods) but in patient craftsmanship. ‘Mr.
Irving Way who brought out the Morris edition in this contry recently wrote me desiring to know how I succeeded in getting so close a duplicate [of Hand & Soul],' Mosher said.
It was done by using our best efforts on the press work and by procuring special inks, which I think on comparison fully come up to the Morris ink.'44 Copeland and Day, Way and Williams, and Mosher were all reputable publishers (Mosher, known as the moral pirate', actually sent royalties to English authors) who responded briefly and with restraint to the enthusiasm for Kelmscott-style typography in the United States; to see the Morris mania in a less inhibited form, we must turn to Elbert Hubbard, who, after meeting Morris, created the Roycrofters, a self-described crafts community, in East Aurora, N.Y.
(‘Take the train for East Aurory | Where we work for Art and Glory' was one of Hubbard's many slogans.) There the P.
T.
Barnum of art', as one newspaper justly called him, built the Roycroft Inn for visitors (with one of the rooms named after Morris), a printing plant, a book bindery, and a chapel, all in a Ye Olde Worlde fashion.
The Morrisian doctrine of handicrafts was oddly blended with inspirational capitalist sentiments (‘I am not in the business of defaming America . . .
I believe in big business and more of it,' Hubbard announced⁴), and at annual conventions in East Aurora the numerous disciples of Fra Elbertus' listened to band concerts on the lawn and pondered such themes as HEALTH, HAPPINESS, TRUTH'.
May Morris, visiting Buffalo in 19o4, was handed an invitation from Hubbard to visit the Roycrofters; angrily crumbling the letter in her hand, she replied, 'I most certainly will not go to East Aurora, nor do I have any desire to see that obnoxious imitator of my dear father.'4 Hubbard, who had called at Kelmscott House a second time in June 1896 when Morris was too ill to see him, always regarded himself as Morris's chief American disciple.
The sentimental, self-indulgent tone of the Roycroft propaganda is well represented by this typical effusion from Hubbard's son after his father's death: When the Roycroft workers have departed and the still serenity of deep night has settled down like a blanket upon the shops, the kindred spirits of William Morris and Elbert Hubbard often glide noiselessly through the print shop, the chapel and the book-bindery to learn how we, their earthbound successors, are carrying on with the heritage of fine craftsmanship which they have left to us.'47 Figure 95 Elbert Hubbard (known as Fra Elbcrtus'to his numcrous followers), Morris's flamboyant Amcrican disciplc, printed a scrics of Kclmscott-style books, often with such odd fcatures as limp chamois covers and halftonc illustrations.
May Morris describcd him as that obnoxious imitator of my dcar father'.
The first Roycroft books published in the 189os were printed on a hand-press, but ‘gradually we got over that idea', Hubbard confessed,4⁸ and his shop in time was filled with rotary presses, many of them used to produce Hubbard's popular magazine the Philistine.
The books offered a bastardized rendition of the Kelmscott style, with coarse paper, Morris-like ornaments, incongruous halftone illustrations, abundant red ink, conventional typefaces, and limp chamois covers.
Yet, unappealing as the Roycroft volumes may seem to us todáy, they had a strong influence on some young American booklovers such as Dard Hunter and Will Ransom.
