Through the improbable medium of Hubbard's tasteless imitations, the Morrisian idea of fine printing was translated into a vernacular easily grasped by a mass audience. ‘That is how the tradition first came to America,’ wrote Ransom, was expressed in terms of the national characteristic, publicity, by a master of that delicate art, and set many of us on the road to our own goal.'49 The usual superficial indications of Morris's presence in American book-design at the turn of the century, apart from a predilection for the ATFJenson type, were title-pages surrounded by dark Kelmscottlike borders, often printed separately on coated paper and merely inserted in a book that had been produced earlier.
Such affectations do not suggest a deep understanding of Morris's views on bookmaking, and, if we wish to assess his more permanent contribution to American typography, we must consider his influence on the most accomplished printers and designers of the United States.
To cite only a few random examples, Frederic Goudy, the type-designer, bought Morris's Chaucer press, issued Morris and Walker's essay Printing' as the first publication of the Village Press, and made his Village type remarkably similar to the Golden fount; John Henry Nash, the celebrated California printer, commissioned a portrait of Morris for his library; Carl Purington Rollins, for many years the director of the Yale University Press, began collecting Kelmscott books when still a young man and, O one gocs there now: for what is lcft to fctch amay from the dcsolate battlencnto all arow, And the lead roof heavy and grey?
"Chercfore," oaid fair Yoland of the flowers, " This io the tune of Scren Towers." NO one walks there now; Exccpt in the mhite moonlight Che whitc ghosts walli in a row; If one could scc it, an awful sight,— "Listen !" eaid fair Yoland of the flowcrs, " Chis is the tune of Seven Cowers." BUC none can oce them now, Chough thcy sit by the side of the mnoat, feet half in the watcr, there in a row, Long bair in the wind afloat.
" Therefore," said fair Yoland of the flowero, "Chis ís the tune of Scren Cowers." Figure 96 A sadly characteristic example of a debased Morrisian style in American bookmaking at the turn of the century: an edition of Pre-Raphaelite Ballads by Morris (New York, 19oo), with illustrations and ornaments by H.
M.
O'Kane. after arriving at Yale, rescued the Chaucer which he found being used as a doorstop in the Art School library.s° Reading the memoirs and biographies of numerous important figures in American typography who came of age in the early decades of the twentieth century, one can discover a curiously similar pattern of influence: it was the Kelmscott books that first opened their eyes to the possibilities of fine printing, and, long after they stopped imitating Morris's typographical peculiarities, they continued to revere the founder of the Kelmscott Press as a symbol of craftsmanship and moral integrity.
One can trace this evolution of attitudes in the lives of Daniel Berkeley Updike and Bruce Rogers, the most distinguished printer and book-designer respectively that the United States has yet produced.
The first ambitious book undertaken by Updike during the 189os was the Altar Book for the Episcopal Church, and from the beginning it was conceived as a homage to the Kelmscott Press.
In August 1893 Updike wrote to Morris, I am engaged in the printing of a devotional book which I sincerely desire to make the most perfect piece of printing that is possible.
And to this end I take the liberty of addressing you to know if through you, I can get a Roman type for the printing of it that has enough blackness and strength to allow the use of decoration of a solid yet brilliant kind.'Would Morris sell him founts of the Golden type or allow it to be recut in Great Primer size?
It is not known whether Morris replied to this request, and if he did his answer was no doubt negative.
Updike therefore turned to the unauthorized adaptation of the Golden type produced at the Dickinson Type Foundary in Boston but decided not to use it when it was supplied to another printing house.
The only remaining solution was to commission his own design, a task put into the hands of Bertram Goodhue.
The Altar Book, published in 1896, was perhaps the most stupendous tribute to the influence of the Kelmscott Press ever published in the United States (or in any other country): an enormous folio, it was embellished with ornaments by Goodhue and illustrations by Robert Anning Bell (an Englishman) and was printed by De Vinne in New York.
This remarkable pastiche signals the high-water mark of Morrisian enthusiasm in both American book-design and Updike's career, for the majority of the early books printed at his Merrymount Press in Boston are set in Caslon and resemble the exercises in puritanical severity produced by Mason's students at the Central School of Arts and Crafts.
Nor, dazzled though he was by Morris's ornaments, did Figure 97 Daniel Berkeley Updike's folio Altar Book (1896) for the Episcopal Church in the United States is perhaps the most sumptuous imitation of the Kelmscott style ever produced on either side of the Atlantic.
EASTER-DAY.
THE COLLECT.
LMIGHTY God, who through thine only, begotten Son Jesus Christ hast overcome death, and opened unto us the gate of ever lasting life; We humbly beseech thee that, as by thy special grace preventing us thou dost put into our minds good desires, so by thy continual help we may bring the same to good effect; through Jesus Christ our Lord, who liveth and reigneth with thee and the Holy Ghost ever, one God, world without end.
Amen.
THE EPISTLE.
Col.iii.
1.
F ye then be risen with Christ, seek those things which are above,where Christ sittethon the right hand of God.
Set your affection on things above, not on things on the earth.
For ye are dead, and your life is hid with Christ in God.
When Christ, who is our life, shall appear, then shall ye also ap pear with him in glory.
Mortify therefore your members which are upon the earth; fornication, uncleanness, inordi, nate affection, evil concupiscence, and covetousness, which is idola try: for which things'sake the wrath of God cometh on the children of disobedience: in the which ye also walked some time,when ye lived in them.
THE GOSPEL.
St.
John xx.
1.
HÉfirst day of the weekcometh MaryMagdalene early, when it was yet dark, unto the sepulchre, and seeth the stone taken away from the sepul chre.
Then she runneth, and cometh to Simon Peter, and to the other disciple, whom Jesus loved, and saith unto them, They have taken away the Lord out of the sepulchre, and we know not where they have laid him.
Peter therefore went forth, and that other dis ciple, and came to the sepulchre.
So they ran both together: and the other disciple did outrun Peter, and came first to the sepulchre.
Bertram Goodhue furnished the ornaments, and the illustrations were by Robert Anning Bell.
Updike share his dislike of machinery.
He did not hesitate to install cylinder presses, though he operated them at slower speeds than most printers did, and he was an advocate of Monotype composition, especially after more attractive typefaces became available from Monotype in the 192os.
Updike, in short, moved from a youthful phase of uncritical adulation of Morris to a more judicious, mature appreciation of his achievement.
Updike in later years criticized Morris's types and his failure to follow always his own typographical principles, but the American never abandoned his conviction that Morris ‘was a great printer because he was a great man who printed greatly, as he did much else'.s² Never as bewitched by Morris as Updike was, Bruce Rogers none the less discovered his vocation as a book-designer when J.
M.
Bowles, the founder of Modern Art, showed him several Kelmscott volumes.
Only in one of Rogers's youthful productions, R.
B.
Gruelle's Notes: Critical and Biographical.
Collection of W.
T.
Walters, published by Bowles in 1895, did he attempt a frank imitation of the Kelmscott style, and, like Updike, he subsequently expressed serious misgivings about Morris's books.
Though he remained primarily a limited edition' designer, Rogers faced directly (and solved) many typographical problems — such as the designing of footnotes and indexes — that Morris, with his disdain for scholarly apparatus, never had to confront, and he went beyond Morris also in his ready acceptance of mechanical typesetting equipment and power presses.
Rogers's work occasionally rather reminds one of Francis Meynell's books at the Nonesuch Press, for in both instances it is the sheer versatility and inventiveness of the designer that astonishes.
And, as in Meynell's case, the impact of Morris is sometimes difficult to discern on the brilliant surface of Rogers's books, yet, as Beatrice Warde suggested, we can discover just beneath that surface the surprising truth that Rogers managed to steal the Divine Fire which glowed in the Kelmscott Press books, and somehow to be the first to bring it down to earth's3 — a phrase which adroitly summarizes the transformation of Morrisian principles in both the United States and England during the first half ofthe twentieth century.
In Germany there was rapid recognition of the importance of the Kelmscott Press, which inspired a flourishing private-press movement in the first decade of the new century.
As early as 1898, when the Press closed, an account of Morris and his āccomplishments as a printer was published in Zeitschrift fr Biicherfreunde which included numerous facsimile reproductions of his types and ornaments.s4 The centre of German interest in the Kelmscott Press was Weimar, where in May 1905 there was an exhibition of the Kelmscott and Doves books and where Count Kessler, an admirer of the English private presses, set up one of his own, the Cranach-Presse.
J.
H.
Mason was brought over to instruct the German compositors; the services of Edward Johnston were enlisted; Emery Walker gave advice and assistance.
A few years earlier Walker, Johnston, and Eric Gill also contributed their skills to a series of German classics issued by the Insel Verlag of Leipzig, a commercial publisher.
The Morrisian influence, as mediated through his associates and disciples, was strong in Germany until 1914, and after the war, in a backward flow of ideas, some English printers received their first exposure to Morris's typographical principles through German books.
Other European countries also reacted enthusiastically to the Kelmscott Press books.
Switzerland, at this time under German cultural sway, tended to reflect the typographical fashions of its larger neighbour.
In 1894 visitors to the Libre Esthétique' exhibition in Brussels weré able to see the Kelmscott books assembled for the first time, and a correspondent for the Studio (April 1894) reported: 'It is with the most lively admiration that connoisseurs are arrested before the books of William Morris – The Defence of Guenevere, The History of Troy, and A Dream of John Ball, of which the frontispiece has been designed with such distinction by Sir Edward Burne-Jones.
One recognizes in them the perfection of quality; and the first appearance of these works of the Kelmscott Press before the Brussels public, one can say, has been a real triumph.' For the opening of a similar exhibition in Antwerp the same year, Emmanuel de Bom delivered a lecture on Morris and his influence on typography which was later published.
On the Continent, however, the art-nouveau influence was strong, and at that distance from England both readers and book-designers found it difficult to distinguish between the rival styles; thus Henry Van de Velde, the most influential figure in Belgian book-production in the early years of the twentieth century, may seem a very Morrisian figure, printing in Caslon on a hand-press and designing all his own ornaments, but the latter prove, on closer inspection, to be more in the manner of Beardsley than of Morris.
In the Netherlands there was also a Morris-like personality, Dr P.
J.
H.
Cuypers, an ardent medievalist architect, not to mention a book-designer of thé first rank, S.
H. de Roos, whose earliest volume was a translatiòn of some Morris essays (1903).
Morrisian ideas were spread in Scandinavia primarily by a few printers such as Waldemar Zachrisson in Sweden and Simon Bernsteen in Denmark.
Frederik Hendrikson, a Dane, had even anticipated Morris by writing an article on the deficiencies of modern book-design for the newspaper Politiken in 1884, and in 1888 he founded Forening for Boghaandvaerk (the Danish Society for Book Craft).
But if northern Europe was receptive to Morris's theories, this was not the case in France and Italy, both of which were dominated by strong native traditions of typography and illustration.
The French especially had their own views on the Ideal Book, and their ideal bore scarcely any resemblance to Morris's.
French bibliophiles, moreover, were so convinced of the superiority of their own éditions de luxe that they were not at all receptive to an Anglo-Saxon standard.
Octave Uzanne, in Nos amis les livres (Paris, 1886), declared unhesitatingly: ‘The art of producing beautiful books is the only one that the foreignērs have not been able to imitate.
Essentially it is a purely French art and one could parody Villon and say: "Il n’est bon livre que de Paris!" Printing, illustration, stitching, binding make us the absolute superior of all others in Europe or America, and this applies to everything, not only to the talent of our artists, but to the eye and perfect taste of our main publishers.'s In Italy the only minor evidences of Morris's influence are some volumes published by the Fratelli Treves of Milan early in the century in an imitation of the Golden type, and a temporary revival of darker typefaces and wood-engraved title-pages.

## A. W. Pollard, long an astute observer of the contemporary printing scene as well as a bib
liographer of early English books, in 1934 offered this summary of the meaning of Morris's typographical adventure: 'The beauty of all these books [by the Doves, Vale, and Ashendene presses and Robert Proctor] reinforced the influence of the Kelmscott Press ones, by proving that what Morris had done on his own lines could be done by lesser men with the variations suggested by their individual tastes.
They reinforced also the proof which Morris had given that so long as it is regarded as a hobby (or in a commercial house as an advertisement) the production of really fine specimens of printing is not an impossibly expensive one.
Morris made no profit from the Kelmscott books as a publisher. . . .
But he proved the existence of a public willing to pay for the cost of print and paper, even when print and paper were the best which money could buy.'s Morris's discovery ofa market for limited-edition books was indeed significant, and his customers were for the most part the same middleclass bibliophiles who were caught up in the first-edition mania fostered at the end of the nineteenth century by T.
J.
Wise and Harry Buxton Forman; it is no accident that the first (and only) full-length bibliographical account of Morris's books was written by Forman.
In order for private presses to move beyond the Victorian parlour, it had to be demonstrated that there was a substantial number of book-lovers who were prepared to purchase handsomely printed volumes.
The success of the Kelmscott Press proved this was so.
At the same time Morris.supplied an ideology, rooted in Ruskinian thought and the Arts and Crafts movement, which justified the hand-labour required by such undertakings.
The nature of Morris's contribution to modern book-design may still inspire debate, but it is indisputable that he is the progenitor of nearly all twentieth-century private presses that have emphasized fine printing.
Naturally these presses have evolved in various ways, and few of them today are similar in every respect to the Kelmscott or Doves model.
After the economic collapse of 1929 the bibliophilic market shrank dramatically, making it unthinkable to produce works on the scale of the Kelmscott Chaucer, the Doves Bible, or the Ashendene Dante.
Many of the private presses in Britain and the United States in the period following the Second World War embodied, in fact, a temporary reversion to the Daniel ideal of the hobbyist printer distributing slender little volumes to his family and friends.
But that pattern has changed yet again in recent decades, and today the private presses seem to be prospering once more, flourishing with amazing vitality in such centres as Leicester, Iowa City, and San Francisco.
One major difference between the earlier Kelmscott-type presses and contemporary private presses, however, is that the latter are less likely to have any direct influence on the world of commercial printing.
The reason for this appears to be that their respective printing technologies have diverged so abruptly in the last two or three decades that they no longer have much in comnon except certain problems of designing.
The hand-press methods still used by the majority of private printers and the automated realm of offset presses and computerized typesetting simply represent two separate worlds, and, despite occasional attempts to bridge the gap - such as the University of California's admirable lithographic reprints of private-press books like the Arion Press Moby Dick and the Pennyroyal Alice volumes — it seems unlikely that any private press in the future will ever have the impact on printing and publishing at large that the Kelmscott and Doves Presses and their ilk once did.
This is an unhappy development, since there is unmistakable evidence of deteriorating typographical standards at the moment as a result of the new technologies.
Except for Blake, who rejected letterpress printing altogether, Morris was the first English artist of major stature who succeeded in controlling every aspect of the printing of a book from types to bindings.
He did so not only because he had a passion for thoroughness but also because it seemed to him the only way to achieve that unity of effect which he and Walker regarded as the primary characteristic of fine printing.
Two centuries earlier Joseph Moxon, in his Mechanick Exercises on the Whole Art of Printing, had offered a definition – often since quoted - of the typographer (or, as we might say today, the book-designer) as ‘such a one, who by his own Judgement, from solid reasoning with hinself, can either perform, or direct others to perform from the beginning to the end, all the Handy-works and Physical Operations relating to Typographie'.s7 With increasing automation and specialization in modern printing, there has been an ever greater need for one man or wonan to orchestrate all the multitudinous activities now required to produce a book: Morris, it might be argued, invented (or reinvented, if we take Moxon into account) the role of book-designer.
Yet such a claim on his behalf has troubling implications, because it suggests an acceptance of the division of labour which Morris, as a disciple of Ruskin, found abhorrent.
Peter Floud, in a talk on Morris delivered on BBC radio in 1954 and later printed in the Listener, made the point that though Morris's designs [for fabrics and wallpapers] were certainly executed by hand, they are not examples of individual craftwork, but rather what one can call serial hand-production on a factory basis, which makes full use of the division oflabour and differs very little in its moral and social implications from straightforward machine-production'.s8 Floud may have put his case in strong language, but there can be no doubt that he has discovered the essential discrepancy between Morris's theory and practice.
A similar analysis of the Kelmscott Press would show that Morris's printers were compelled to follow the typographical patterns and designs he devised in his study at Kelmscott House rather than to express the roughness and majesty of their own souls (as Ruskin phrased it in The Nature of Gothic).
On the rare occasions when they were allowed complete freedom of choice, the Kelmscott printers produced something conspicuously hideous like the Wayzegoose menus.
But it is almost too easy to point out the inconsistencies of Morris's system ofideas.
If we find Morris the thinker irritating, we can do what Pevsner, Morison, and others have done: ignore the intellectual foundations of his medievalism and concentrate instead on Morris the precursor of twentieth-century functionalism.
But to do so is surely to ignore the richness and complexity of the man and to isolate only a small part ofhis mind that happens to suit our modern tastes.
In the end the Kelmscott Press is important to us for reasons that go beyond the technical and aesthetic innovations described in earlier chapters of this book.
The Press still fascinates us today because of the profound questions that Morris posed about the triumph of the machine.
Morris saw that more was at stake than merely the revival of attractive printing, and it is the reverberation of his ideas, quite apart from whether one is personally drawn to the Kelmscott Press books, that makes the books so extraordinarily compelling in their appeal.
Although he was unable to foresee the specific technology of the next century — films, radio, television, video cassettes, computers — Morris once expressed to Burne-Jones, in a characteristically jocular way, a dark vision of the future of the printed book in modern civilization: he railed fiercely against the invention of printing and gave it a hundred years at most to come to an end.
Already said he the magazines are driving books out of the field — presently newspapers will have killed magazines — then the telephone will come — bottles of talk — & the newspapers will be ended, & thats a comfort — as a centipede eats a cockroach & a cockroach eats a bug — a hundred years will do it said he. . . .'s9 The Kelmscott books are imperfect creations, produced by occasionally uneasy compromises with modern technology and often flawed by Morris's hatred of all post-medieval thought and art.
But from our point of view today, a century later, they represent a logical and, on the whole, satisfying culmination of the art offine printing that began with Gutenberg.
Of course Morris was wrong in a literal sense: books that are in themselves works ofart are still being printed and will continue to be printed in the future.
But Morris's astonishingly accurate prediction of the death of the book as a central cultural artefact in the twentieth century, with moral and social consequences we cannot yet gauge, makes the Kelmscott Press seem, more and more, like the end rather than the beginning of something good. # APPENDIXA CHECKLIST OF THE KELMSCOTT PRESS BOOKS HE following list includes all the books printed at the Kelmscott Press but not the ephemera such as advertising circulars.
My A Bibliograply of the Kelmscott Press (Oxford, 1984) supplies much more detailed descriptions of each of the books.
The books were all produced in one of several buildings in the Upper Mall, Hammersmith, except for Gotlic Architecture (no.
18), which was printed during the Arts and Crafts Exhibition at the New Gallery, London.
Unless otherwise indicated, the books are printed in black ink only.
The two sizes of Flower paper (16 × 11 inches and 16 × 22 inches) are designated Flower (1) and Flower (2) respectively.
I have made no attempt to describe the initials, borders, and engraved title-pages in this brief list; suffice it to say that every Kelmscott book was decorated with wood-engraved ornaments designed by Morris, some much more fully than others.

(paper) and 2 guineas (vellum). # APPENDIXBLECTURE BYEMERY WALKER HE lecture by Emery Walker on Letterpress Printing and Illustration', delivered on 15 November 1888 to the Arts and Crafts Society, was a turning-point in the history of modern printing, for it inspired William Morris to design his own typeface and to establish the Kelmscott Press.
Since Walker spoke (rather nervously, according to Morris) from notes, the lecture was never published in the form in which it was delivered; yet, because it had such far-reaching consequences, I have made an attempt here to reproduce the substance of the lecture.
There are three important sources of information: Walker's notes (in the Humanities Research Center, University of Texas); Oscar Wilde's (unsigned) article, Printing and Printers', Pall Mall Gazette, 16 November 1888, p.
5; and ‘The Art of Printing. . .
A Lecture by Emery Walker', British and Colonial Printer and Stationer, 6 February 189o, p.
94, which reports a slightly different version of the talk, delivered at the City of London College in 189o.
Of these, the notes and Wilde's article are more significant, and I have therefore transcribed them in their entirety, but I have also made use of the article in the British and Colonial Printer and Stationer (which is more detailed but filled with inaccuracies) to clarify certain doubtful allusions.
Two other reports of essentially the same lecture by Walker were published in the British and Colonial Printer and Stationer, 2 January 1896, pp.
10−11, and 31 March 1898, pp.
205−6, both accompanied by illustrations based upon Walker's slides.
In the later variants of the lecture Walker concluded by showing pages from the Kelmscott Press books.
An important recent study of the lecture is John Dreyfus, Emery Walker's 1888 Lecture on “Letterpress Printing": A Reconstruction and a Reconsideration', Craft History, 1(1988), 118-30.
Since Walker used these lecture notes on more than one occasion, it is not always possible to distinguish precisely between the original text and later additions to it.
The inconsistencies in numbering the slides reflect these revisions: only Walker's final renumberings have been transcribed here.
Any necessary annotations of the two texts have been placed within square brackets; Walker's own square brackets in his notes are reproduced as arrow brackets.
##

## Walker's
lecture notes Although, I suppose one may not unreasonably think that every person here, has at least some idea, of the methods by w[hi]ch books are made, I hope I may be pardoned, if for the sake of impressing the essentials of the various processes on your minds, I, with the assistance of my friend here, very briefly demonstrate the rudiments of the art.
Type and paper, may be said to be to a printed book, what stones or bricks, and mortar, are to architecture.
They are the essentials, without which there can be no book in the one case, and no architecture, in the other.
I do not want to dwell on the purely historical aspect longer than I can help, but it is necessary to say in what way modern, that is, type printing differs from the method which has been practised in China for centuries.
Shortly it is the use of moveable letters instead of engraved blocks, and as Mr.
Talbot Reed [inserted in pencil: whose book is certainly the best historical work in the languagel has said, the man who invented typography, was doubtless he, who invented moveable type. 〈Here show punch, matrix, adjustable mould and type, and Japanese wood block.> <metal composed of lead[,] tin and antimony〉 Early printers cast their own type Describe and names [of] various parts of the type, face, serif, feet[,] shoulder[,] nick.
Now generally cast by machines when large quantities are wanted Compositor['ls Frame[.] Upper and Lower Case arrangement of letters[:]e' in centre of the lower case.
Composing stick.
Galley. chase.
You will see that the labour of casting and ‘setting up' is precisely the same whether the type is as beautiful as that of the early printers or as ugly as the latest American importation.
The earliest punch cutters naturally copied the current handwriting of the country in which they lived, and in the specimens which will now be shown, enlarged on the screen, we shall see how close the resemblance is to the written books [Inserted in pencil: 1 Block book ars Moriendi.] i First edition of Bible known to Bibliographers as the 42 line[,] usually called the Mazarin or Gutenberg's[,] printed at Mentz

##

## Oscar
Wilde's article Nothing could have been better than Mr.
Emery Walker's lecture on Letterpress, Printing, and Illustration, delivered last night at the Arts and Crafts.
A series of most interesting specimens of old printed books and manuscripts were displayed on the screen by means of the magiclantern, and Mr.
Walker's explanations were as clear and simple as his suggestions were admirable.
He began by explaining the different kinds of type, and how they are made, and showed specimens of the old block-printing which preceded the moveable type and which is still used in China.
He pointed out the intimate connection between printing and handwriting - as long as the latter was good the printers had a living model to go by, but when it decayed printing decayed also.
He showed in succession on the screen a page from Gutenberg's Bible, (the first printed book – date about 145o-5) and a manuscript of Columella; a printed Livy of 1469, with the abbreviations of handwriting, and a manuscript of the History of Pompeius by Justin of 1451.
The latter he regarded as an example of the beginning of Roman type.
The resemblance between the manuscripts and the printed books was most curious and suggestive.
He then showed a page out ofJohn of Spier's edition of Cicero's Letters, the first book printed at Venice, an edition of the same book by Nicholas Jansen [Jenson] 147o, and a wonderful manuscript Petrarch of the 16th century.
He told the audience about Aldus who was the first publisher to start cheap books, who dropped abbreviations, and had his type cut by Francia pictor et aurifex, who was said to have taken it from Petrarch's handwriting.
He exhibited a page of the copy-book of Vicentino the great Venetian writing master which was greeted with a spontaneous round of applause, and made some excellent suggestions about improving modern copy-books and avoiding slanting writing.
A superb Plautus printed at Florence in 1514 for Lorenzo di Medici, Polydore Virgil's History with the fine Holbein designs, printed at Basle in 1556, and other interesting books were also exhibited on the screen, the size of course being very much enlarged.
He spoke of Elzevir in the seventeenth century, when handwriting began to fall off, and of the English printer Caslam [Caslon], and of Baskerville, whose type was possibly designed by Hogarth, but is not very good.
Latin, he remarked, was a better language to print than English, as the tails of the letters did not so often fall below the line.
The wide-spacing between lines occasioned by the use of a lead, he pointed out, left the page in stripes, and made the blanks as important as the lines.
Margins should of course be made, except the inner margins, and head lines often robbed the page of its beauty of design.
The type used by the Pall Mall [Old Style] was, we are glad to say, rightly approved of.
With regard to illustration, the essential thing, Mr.
Walker said, is to have harmony between the type and the decoration.
He pleaded for true book-ornament as opposed to the silly habit of putting pictures where they are not wanted, and pointed out that mechanical harmony and artistic harmony went hand in hand.
No ornament or illustration should be used in a book that cannot be printed in the same way as the type.
For his warnings he produced Roger's [sic] Italy' with a steelplate engraving, and a page from an American magazine which being florid, pictorial, and bad, was greeted with some laughter.
For examples we had a lovely Boccaccio printed at Ulm, and a page out ofLa Mer des Histoires' printed in 1488.
Blake and Bewick were also shown, and a page of music designed by Mr. [Herbert P.] Horne.
The lecture was listened to with great attention by a large audience, and was certainly most attractive.
Mr.
Walker has the keen artistic instinct that comes out of actually working in the art of which he spoke.
His remarks about the pictorial character of modern illustration were most well-timed, and we hope that some of the publishers in the audience will take them to heart.
Next Thursday Mr.
Cobden Saunderson [Sanderson] lectures on book-binding, a subject on which few men in England have higher qualifications for speaking.
We are glad to see these lectures are so well attended.
Appendix B # APPENDIX C KELMSCOTTPRESS EXPENSES HE document transcribed below came to light after this book was completed.
At the Sotheby sale of Sydney Cockerell's books and manuscripts, 10 December 1956, lot 99 was described as‘KELMSCOTT PRESS Ex-PENSES,I89I.MS.
ACCOUNT BOOK in the handwriting of William Morris, his daughter May, and his pressman William Bowden, bound in at the end are some leaves fromn Morris's diary for 1892 with a few entries in his hand, red morocco, the original limp boards of the account book preserved, in slip-case smn.
8vo 1891-2'.
The buyer was the firm of Bernard Quaritch, which in turn sold the document to Arnold Yates, an English collector.
In 1971, near the end of his life, Yates allowed an acquaintance to make a photocopy of it for Dr Joseph R.
Dunlap of New York, who has recently given me a copy.
A note in Cockerell's hand appears on the flyleaf: ‘This was the original cash book of the Kelmscott Press.
It was begun on Jan 17 1891 and the entries as far as Mar 7 are in the hand of Willian Morris, who was then disabled with a gouty illness.
The entries for Mar 14 are by his daughter May, and those for Mar 21 & 28 by the overseer Mr Bowden.
The remaining entries to July 7 are by Morris.
At the end are bound some leaves from WM's diary of 1892, which was but little used.' On the facing page are three notations that frequently appear in Cockerell's volumes of Morris-related documents: Cockerell's signature; the private shelf-mark ‘Morrisiana 28'; and an indication that the expense book was no.
3o8 in the Morris centenary exhibition at the Victoria and Albert Museum in 1934.
On the cover of the book is a label in Morris's hand that reads Kelnscott Press | - Expenses - | January 1891.' Cockerell, following his usual practice, has pasted a signature of Morris (probably clipped from a cheque) over a calendar on the cover verso.
At the end of the expenses, on a separate page, is a list, not in Morris's hand, which consists of the nanes of Morris's printers, though it cannot necessarily be assumed that all of them were employed by Morris during the first half of 1891.
The following names appear in the list: Tibbett [probably J.
Tippet], [L.] Tasker, [Thomas] Binning, [R.] Eatley and [G.] Heath, Collins jr [two Collinses worked at the Kelmscott Press], Carpenter, Barks, Eatley and [illegible: Freestone?], H.
Gage Cole.
When the staff of the press was photographed in 1895, all but three were still employed by Morris.
The names of three other printers – Stephen Mowlem, H.
Howes, and Mrs Pine – appear in the list of expenses.
The three diary entries by Morris alluded to in the Sotheby catalogue are for 2o January, 3 March, and 4 March.
The latter records in stock paper | big 5 | reams | small 3o.- | & retree'.
Pasted into the last pages of the document is Quaritch's receipt to Yates for £6o (dated 15 January 1957) for the expense book.
In the following transcription I have recorded all the information in the book but have made no atempt to reproduce its actual form.
The expense book serves as a useful supplement to the later ledger in the Morgan Library, because precise documentary evidence of the Press's activities is relatively sparse before Cockerell, an unusually assiduous record-keeper, became the second Secretary of the Kelmscott Press in the summer of 1894.
Perhaps the most illuminating entry is that of 2o May, which tells us that Morris bought two manuscripts of the eleventh and twelfth centuries ‘for study for type': it is characteristic of Morris as a collector that he purchased early books and manuscripts primarily as an aid in designing his typefaces.

Sydney Cockerell took a copy of the Kelmscott Press The Nature of Gothic to Ruskin at Brant wood in the spring of 1892.
Cockerell recorded in his diary: He [Ruskin] was pleased with it and interested in my telling him about the new blackletter fount [the Troy type].
He said that Morris was the ablest man of his generation' (17.4.92).
A few days later Cockerell conveyed this remark to Morris: 'He seemed very pleased to hear about J.R. and of what he said of him' (21.4.92).

It is difficult to determine precisely what manuscripts Morris saw in the Bodleian.
As an undergraduate he would not have been allowed to use the Bodleian under normal circumstances, and his name does not appear in the library's records during those years.
Yet Morris had vivid memories of studying the Bodleian manuscripts, and possibly he might have used the reading room by special permission, and the documents might have been requested by a companion.
Morris's name does figure in the Bodleian's 'L.P.
Lending Book' after he received his degree, but here the evidence is complicated by the occasional appearance of an R.
Morris'(probably the Revd Richard Morris, Winchester Lecturer on English Language and Literature at King's College School, London) in the records.
'Mr Morris' (not always, therefore, demonstrably William Morris) called for the following manuscripts (identified by Bodleian shelfmarks): (1) Bod.
264 − 27 Apr.
1856 (certainly William Morris, because of Exeter' is added after the name); (2) Auct.
F.
IV.
32 — 26 Sept.
1856; (3) Ashmole 789, 1114 - 2 Jan.
1863; (4) Dugdale A1. − 4 May 1865; (5) Auct.

L.

1866; (10) Auct. π.

5 - 9 Mar.
1867; (11) Douce 381, 302 − 17 May 1867.
In a later L.P.
Entry Book' it is recorded that Morris (accompanied by Emery Walker) came on 28 Nov.
1894 to inspect the ‘Douce Apocalypse’ (Douce 180) and several other manuscripts: Auct.
D.
4.
17; Douce 366; Bod.
264.
On this occasion Morris was investigating the possibility of publishing a facsimile of the Apocalypse: see Bibliography, p.

55.
The present locations of three of Morris's four presses are known.
(a) The small proofing press, unlike the other three, was not acquired by C.
R.
Ashbee; it was advertised for sale at 3o guineas by Tregaskis in 1898, apparently without success, for it remained in the basement of the Tregaskis Bookshop in London for a number of years.
In the 192os Dr John W.
Bartlett of New York bought it.
Theodore M.
Lilienthal purchased it from Bartlett in 1939; and it is now in the Huntington Library.
(See Lilienthal, A William Morris Press goes W’est (Berkeley, Calif., 1961).) (b) Either the first or second of Morris's presses passed from Ashbee in 1913 and through several intermediate owners to the Shakespeare Head Press; Sir Basil Blackwell gave it to the William Morris Society, and it is at present, appropriately, in the basement of Kelmscott House.
(See the Morris Society pamphlet William Morris's Printing Press (1983).) (c) The large Chaucer press, after it left Ashbee's hands, wandered about for some years before it came into the possession of Frederic Goudy, the American typedesigner, who used it at his Press of the Woolly Whale.
It is now in a private collection in New York.
(See J.
Ben Lieberman, ‘The Liberty Bell on the Kelmscott Goudy Press', Black Art, 1 (Summer, 1962), 41–9.) 56.

Douglas Cockerell, who had been much more closely associated with Cobden-Sanderson than his brother Sydney was, wrote some years later: ‘Cobden-Sanderson's egotism was almost pathological.
He lacked the power of co-operation almost entirely and was almost insanely jealous of any reputation, even Morris's, that might rival his own.
He lived in a world of his own creation, swayed by emotional storms of great intensity, and I doubt if he was capable of true friendship’(Cockerell, p.
85).

WM to Walter Crane, 17.2.94 (letter privately printed by William P.
Barlow, Jr., 1975); ‘The Work of Walter Crane', Art Journal, Easter Annual, 1898, p.
10.
Crane's remark about being insufficiently Gothic in outlook was a commonplace in the Morris circle.
Cockerell said it to Crane's face: 'In m[ornin]g called on Walter Crane at Shepherd's Bush. . . .
Urged him to go to Beauvais, and to pay more attention to Gothic forms and traditions — as I consider him to be Gothic in temper, and to have been led by accident into the acceptance of classical ornament and ideas' (diary, 2.6.89).
And C.
R.
Ashbee quoted Morris as saying that Cranewas a Gothic soul with a Renaissance training' (Ashbee Memoirs, vii.
266).

25.
Ellis offered an energetic apologia for his decision to adopt old-spelling' in a letter to T.
R.
Lounsbury, 16.12.93 (Yale): 'It would be presumptuous of me to offer any argument as to the adoption of modern spelling for Chaucer, but I am quite convinced an edition so printed would not gain him one additional rcader....
I have myself gone so far in the other direction with regard to orthography as to have printed Shakespeare's Poems, for the first time in a collected form with the author's spelling.
Though the book was published at 25/- the soo copies were all sold on the day of publication.
I did the same good work this year for Cavendish's Life of Wolsey, with a like satisfactory result, holding that an author's orthography is a part of his work.
Furnivall I am happy to say is now engaged in editing the whole of Shakespeare in like manner — at last we shall be able to read him with the full 1sth-17th century flavour.' 26.

Even Burne-Jones was struck by the similarities between Morris and Chaucer (Memorials, ii.

For a dissenting viewpoint which emphasizes their differences, see Geoffrey Chaucer and William Morris', New Monthly Magazine, 149 (1871), 280–6.

See Charles Muscatine, The Book of Geoffrey Chaucer: An Account of the Publication of Geoffrey Chaucer's Works fromn the Fifteenth Century to Modern Times (San Francisco, 1963); Elwin F.

Morris himself owned Chaucer's Works, published by Thomas Godfray in 1 532 (the first collected edition), Skeat's six-volume edition (Clarendon Press, 1894), and many of the publications of the Chaucer Society, including Furnivall's six-text edition of The Canterbury Tales.

In a report of a lecture by Morris published in the Printers' Register – Supplement, 6 Dec.
1893, p. viii he is quoted as saying, Mr.
Morris concluded by a prediction that within fifty years printing books would be an extinct art - we should be carrying all our books about in bottles with patent stoppers.
While there was still a chance, they should try and produce a few specimens of what was really good printing. # SELECT BIBLIOGRAPHY SıNCE the quantity of words written about Morris and the Kelmnscott Press is beyond measure, the following lists are deliberately brief.
The reader who wishes further information will find many additional sources listed in the notes above and in A Bibliography of the Kelnscott Press, pp. xvii-xxi.

## Unpublished
Sources Detailed lists of Kelmscott Press proofs and trial pages are supplied in A Bibliography of the Kelmscott Press.
Only a few of the most important repositories are included below.
BERNARD QUARITCH LTD., LONDON Morris's letters to Bernard Quaritch.
BoDLEIAN LIBRARY, OxFORD Morris's letters to J. and J.
Leighton; Bernard Quaritch'sletters to his son; miscellaneous correspondence; several folders ofKP material in the John Johnson Collection.
BRITISH LIBRARY in the Department of Printed Books many volumes of KP proofs and trial pages assembled by Sydney Cockerell and Robert Proctor; in the Department of Manuscripts diaries and correspondence of Cockerell and Morris; letters of Burne-Jones.
FITZWILLIAM MUSEUM, CAMBRIDGE in the Departmnent of Manuscripts and Books letters of Burne-Jones and correspondence and diaries of Wilfrid Scawen Blunt; in the Department of Prints and Drawings Burne-Jones's designs for the Chaucer illustrations.
HAMMERSMITH PUBLIC LIBRARIES proofs and trial pages.
HARRY RANSOM HUMANITIES RESEARCH CENTER, UNIVERSITY OF TEXAS AT AUsTIN papers of Emery Walker.
HENRY E.
HUNTINGTON LIBRARY, SAN MARINO, CALIF. miscellaneOus trial pages and correspondence.
OxFORD UNIVERSITY ARCHIVES Morris's correspondence with the Oxford University Press.
PIERPONT MORGAN LIBRARY, NEW YORK the largest collection of KP material in the USA, including proofs, trial pages, designs by Burne-Jones, correspondence, and the KP ledger.
PRIVATE COLLECTION OF SANFORD AND HELEN BERGER, CARMEL, CALIF. proofs, trial pages, correspondence.
UČLA LiBRÀRY archives of Ellis and Elvey.
VICTORIA AND ALBERT MUSEUM LIBRARY miscellaneous Correspondence and proofs.
WILLIAM ANDREWS CLARK MEMORIAL LIBRARY, LOS ANGELES trial pages, proofs, and ephemera.
WILLIAM MORRIS GALLERY, WALTHAMSTOW Correspondence, designs.
